Microsoft offers to buy out a quarter of its workforce
2026-10-26

Microsoft offers to buy out a quarter of its workforce

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-26

Visit Original Website
On Monday, Microsoft said it is offering voluntary buyouts to eligible employees in select parts of the company. CNBC reports that the buyouts will be offered to roughly 60,000 employees, or about a quarter of its global workforce. The company describes the offer as an opportunity for employees who may have already been considering career moves outside Microsoft. While Microsoft did not give a direct reason for the buyout package, it comes as the company has spent the last several years managing costs amid investment in AI infrastructure and data-center buildout. Microsoft will extend the voluntary exit offer (VEO) to qualifying employees in the United States, India, Canada, and the United Kingdom. Microsoft employees at level 70 or below will be eligible for the buyout program. The company’s employee rankings are internal, but according to the salary and career database Levels.fyi, levels 65–67 correspond to principal positions, and 68–70 are at the partner/distinguished level. According to Chief People Officer Amy Coleman, eligible organizations were selected “based on each business’s priorities, customer commitments and workforce needs.” The effort is the second time Microsoft has run a voluntary workforce reduction in 2026. In April, the company offered its first voluntary retirement package to 8,750 US employees. In July, the Xbox division laid off 3,200 employees, or about 20 percent of its entire gaming division. As reported by CNBC, about 30 percent of eligible employees accepted the retirement offer, lessening the impact of the planned layoffs, according to Coleman. While the offer in April went out to long-tenured employees who might be nearing the end of their career, the VEO expands the effort to a much larger potential pool. In comments to GeekWire in July, Coleman said Microsoft was looking into making voluntary exits part of workforce management more regularly. She described the current VEO as something that “builds on what we learned earlier this year through the Voluntary Retirement Program.” While layoffs remain the most common way to reduce headcounts in the tech world, Microsoft may be following Google’s playbook. As Business Insider reported, Alphabet offered employees voluntary exit packages in June and October 2025, and again in February 2026. Now, eligible Microsoft employees may have to decide whether to take a deal now or keep their jobs and face the uncertainty of future company-wide layoffs. According to Microsoft’s blog, employees eligible for the VEO were to be contacted via email today. Microsoft will announce its latest earnings after market close later this week.

Worms mate in man's eyes, get plucked out right before larvae burst out
2026-10-26

Worms mate in man's eyes, get plucked out right before larvae burst out

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-26

Visit Original Website
After two months of discomfort, a man in South Korea went to an eye clinic where doctors pulled 11 living, wriggling worms from his eyes. They apparently made the most of their time on the man’s peepers; at the time of extraction, many of the female worms were pregnant. The man narrowly avoided having them spew their loads of living larvae directly into his eyes. The man in this case was a 71-year-old crop farmer. He told doctors that for the previous two months he had the sensation of having something stuck in both his eyes, which were also red and blurry. When ophthalmologists took a look, they quickly saw the problem: multiple mobile, whitish roundworms. His eyes were not in good shape either, as one might expect. The blood vessels in the membrane over the eye, the conjunctiva, were enlarged, making his eyes look red. And the cells of his cornea—the transparent, dome-shaped outer layer of the eye—had eroded, potentially after the helminths’ hanky-panky. According to a case report published in the New England Journal of Medicine, the doctors gave the man a topical treatment to numb his eyes and then began plucking out the worms one by one with fine forceps. They collected 11 adult worms, which ranged in size from 11 to 22 mm. Examining the worms under a microscope, the doctors identified them as Thelazia callipaeda nematodes, which are widespread across Asia and have also established in parts of Europe. These parasites primarily infest the eyes of cats and dogs but can sometimes make their way into human eyes inadvertently. The worms’ life cycle depends on flies that feed from the animals’ eyes, slurping up tears and secretions. When they feed from an infested eye, the flies can pick off the worms’ first-stage larvae, which use the insect as both an incubator and an air taxi. Inside the fly, the larvae molt twice, becoming third-stage larvae, which then migrate to the fly’s mouthparts and await the next stop at a suitable watery eyeball. The larvae disembark their flying nannies and take up a permanent peeper residence, where they develop into adults after one more molt. Adult worms then live and mate directly on a victim’s eyes. Unlike other worms, female T. callipaeda are ovoviviparous, meaning that embryos develop within eggs inside the female worm’s body. Females release large broods of densely coiled first-stage larvae, which await being lapped up by a thirsty fly. Some of the female worms pulled from the man’s eyes were plump with embryonated eggs. Doctors noted that coiled larvae began bursting out of some of them after they were pulled from the man’s eyes. The male worms, meanwhile, were identified by their copulatory spicules, needle-like structures used to facilitate mating. Luckily for the man, having all of the adult worms plucked from his orbs meant the infection was over—no antiparasitic medication required, even if some of the larvae had been left behind. At a follow-up appointment two months later, doctors noted that all his eye symptoms had resolved and there were no more worms.

Meet the real-life Scottish surgeon who inspired Sherlock Holmes
2026-10-26

Meet the real-life Scottish surgeon who inspired Sherlock Holmes

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-26

Visit Original Website
Sir Arthur Conan Doyle created one of the most beloved fictional detectives of all time in Sherlock Holmes, spawning countless novels, films, TV series, and so forth featuring the sleuth. Far fewer people know about the real-life inspiration for Holmes: a renowned surgeon at Scotland’s University of Edinburgh named Joseph Bell, who taught Doyle when the author was still a young medical student. Bell is the subject of a new, eminently readable biography, Sleuth-Hound: The Case of the Real Sherlock Holmes, by medical historian Lindsey Fitzharris. A noted science communicator with a fondness for the medically macabre, Fitzharris published a biography of Scottish surgical pioneer Joseph Lister, The Butchering Art, in 2017—a great, if occasionally grisly, read. She followed up with 2022’s  The Facemaker: A Visionary Surgeon’s Battle to Mend the Disfigured Soldiers of World War I, about a WWI surgeon named Harold Gillies who rebuilt the faces of injured soldiers. In 2020, Fitzharris hosted a documentary for the Smithsonian Channel, The Curious Life and Death Of…, exploring famous deaths, ranging from drug lord Pablo Escobar to magician Harry Houdini. Fitzharris performed virtual autopsies, experimented with blood samples, interviewed witnesses, and conducted real-time demonstrations in hopes of gleaning fresh insights. And last year she and her husband, caricaturist Adrian Teal, collaborated on an illustrated children’s book, Dead Ends! Flukes, Flops, and Failures that Sparked Medical Marvels. With Sleuth-Hound, Fitzharris returns to the Edinburgh of The Butchering Art, of body snatchers and surgeons’ discovery of ether, which was also the Edinburgh of Arthur Conan Doyle and Joseph Bell. Bell was notorious for his peculiar method of diagnosis: he was able to infer a patient’s occupation and personal habits, as well as any ailments, just by looking at them. For instance, Bell once figured out the route a patient had taken walking to the infirmary by identifying the type of clay on the patient’s boots—an incident Doyle used in The Sign of the Four. Bell was also something of a medical detective, working with a local police surgeon on some of the most famous murder cases of his day. Ars caught up with Fitzharris to learn more. Ars Technica: What drew you to writing an entire book about Joseph Bell? Lindsey Fitzharris: I’ve known about Bell because I’m a medical historian and I knew that he was an inspiration for this character, but nobody had written a big commercial book on him. There was a book in 1982 by a guy named Ely Liebow. So people in the Sherlockian world, they know a bit about Bell through him, but I really wanted to bring a medical history lens to it. When I sold the Sleuth-Hound proposal, I had never read the original Sherlock Holmes tales. I decided to put off reading them initially because I knew enough about Bell to know that it became an irritating comparison; he found it rather reductive at the end of his life. I really wanted to bring the real man behind the myth to life in Sleuth-Hound, not just as the inspiration for Sherlock Holmes, not just as this parlor trick, but as this complex character. I’ve been talking to a lot of Sherlockians who have been really surprised at how much Bell is Sherlock, but also how much he isn’t Sherlock. That’s an interesting discussion as well: why Conan Doyle focused on this one aspect of him. Ars Technica: What are some of the ways Bell is and isn’t Sherlock? Lindsey Fitzharris: He had this Sherlockian way about him where he could tell everything about a person before they ever spoke a word: a callous, a stain, a limp, all were clues to Bell if you only knew how to see it. But in a lot of ways he wasn’t Holmes. For instance, he was a deeply empathetic character. He was very feeling for his patients. And he also was a family man. His wife dies when he’s in his 30s, and he doesn’t remarry his entire life. He holds the torch for her. She dies in childbirth, and he raises his three children alone. My husband is a caricaturist, and we have this discussion about how he can only caricature people he doesn’t know well. That’s because caricature is about those first impressions. I think Conan Doyle knew Bell in his capacity as a student, but he didn’t know Bell the man. So he took the most recognizable flashy trait of Bell and transposed that onto Sherlock Holmes. The question I pose at the end of the book is, would Holmes exist if Conan Doyle had known Bell better, or would he exist in the same format? Ars Technica: Did Conan Doyle’s contemporaries recognize the similarities? Lindsey Fitzharris: Those who knew Bell recognized it immediately. Robert Louis Stevenson reads Sherlock Holmes. He writes to Conan Doyle and he says at the end of the letter, “One thing bothers me, could this be our old friend Joe Bell?” But Bell was a lot more complex, and I really wanted to bring that medical history lens, because this isn’t just a story about his relationship with Conan Doyle and the inspiration for Sherlock Holmes. It’s about the development of forensic medicine. It’s about the moment that medicine enters the courtroom for the first time. Bell wasn’t just the inspiration for Holmes; he was actually doing what Holmes was doing in practice. He was working officially with a police surgeon named Henry Duncan Littlejohn, who was fascinating in and of himself. He was a pioneer of fingerprinting and courtroom photography. They end up partnering and working on some of the biggest high-profile cases in Scotland in the 19th century. Ars Technica: I was frankly shocked about the baby farming case; I had no idea that was a common practice. Lindsey Fitzharris: It’s such a crazy story. The thing that I want people to contextualize here is that there was no birth control. So there were a lot of women having a lot of children, and they were deeply impoverished, and they didn’t have enough money to feed these children. So they turned to the baby farmer. In theory, the baby farmer took the child and then took a fee from you, the mother, with the promise that they would farm out the child to somebody who could take care of the child. That was how it was supposed to work. There were no legal routes for adoption in the 19th century, so they turned to the baby farmer. The case in Sleuth-Hound, the baby farmer, Jessie King, ends up taking the fee and then just killing the infants. This was a horrific case. The chapter opens with these boys finding this bundle of rags and using it as a football and kicking it around. And then they open it, and it’s the decomposing remains of a child. Littlejohn and Bell work on the case. They perform the postmortems; they testify in the trial. One of the dark themes of this book is that a lot of early forensics was built on the brutalized bodies of women in particular. Jessie King was killing infants, but this is a crime that is particular to women—women trying to get their children into families that could afford to feed them. A lot of the crimes are committed on women who never receive any justice, but the science outlives what their bodies taught these early forensic investigators like Bell and Littlejohn. Ars Technica: Case in point: the infamous Chantrelle murder case and how it coincides with the rise of toxicology. Lindsey Fitzharris: The Chantrelle case is so fascinating to me. Eugene Chantrelle was this respectable gentleman who lived in Edinburgh. He was a French tutor, and his wife Lizzie is seemingly poisoned by a gas leak, a broken pipe in the flat, and she’s removed from the property and later dies. But it turns out that Chantrelle was abusing her the entire marriage. We think Littlejohn brought Bell onto the case. Bell and Littlejohn discover that it was opium poisoning, and they test the vomit on her nightgown, and they’re able to tie him to this crime. Bear in mind, Bell worked like Sherlock Holmes. He worked in the shadows. So in these earlier cases, it was very difficult to know if Bell really did work on them. But there’s a really intriguing clue at the end of this story. Littlejohn investigates, [Eugene Chantrelle] is convicted, and he is hanged for this murder. On the scaffold, he allegedly looked around at the audience, locked eyes with Littlejohn, and shouted, “Give my regards to Joe Bell. He did a good job putting the noose around my neck.” This was such a Sherlockian moment. How did Chantrelle know that Bell had worked on this case? We don’t have any records that actually confirm this except that he allegedly said this on the scaffold. The other extraordinary aspect of this story is that Chantrelle was drinking buddies with Robert Louis Stevenson, so he becomes one of the inspirations for Dr. Jekyll and Mr. Hyde: this respectable person on the outside and very sinister and evil on the inside. The prosecutor told Stevenson that this was not Chantrelle’s first victim and that he had killed four or five other people with his opium and cheese parties. Ars Technica: Your book launch should feature an opium and cheese party. Lindsey Fitzharris: (laughs) And ether drinks. You’re invited. The Victorian period was wild because there were a lot of poisons that you could just get over the counter from your pharmacist. Chantrelle had trained as a doctor at one point and had bought a huge amount of opium just before the murder. Even the chemist thought that this was strange, but he assumed that Chantrelle was a doctor. So poison was a big problem in the Victorian period. Some of it was legal, it was hard to detect. Forensic medicine was just developing. I call it the golden era of murdering because you probably had more of a chance in the 19th century of getting away with it. Ars Technica: Sleuth-Hound is written in a deceptively novelistic style, but you’ve clearly done very extensive research on this. What sources did you find most helpful? Lindsey Fitzharris: One of the things that happened was that Bell’s diary became available, that had been locked away for over a century by the Bell family. Perhaps they were waiting for enough time to pass. I almost called the book Sleuth-Hounds because I do also think Littlejohn taught Conan Doyle as well. Bell gave Conan Doyle the method, and Littlejohn gave him the world of forensics. He was a professor of forensics in Edinburgh at the time. We can flesh out what Bell was doing because we know that they worked together often on these cases. You do get dialogue through the murder trials, because there’s so many documents and court records that go along with these trials. There are a lot of letters, diaries. Nothing’s made up, but sometimes I had to take educated guesses. For instance, Conan Doyle is a terrible narrator of his own life. He constantly is contradicting himself, even in his own memoirs. It was difficult figuring out when he came into contact with Bell for the first time. I believe it’s in the winter of 1878, and I provide a lot of context for that in the back notes of the book. I’m a historian. It’s got to be armed to the teeth with sources. But I want people to be able to pick up the book, not know anything about Sherlock Holmes, not know anything about medical history or forensics, and just be able to enjoy it. Medical history is the most universal way into the past. I make the past relatable. I tell you what that was like a hundred years ago. My superpower in 2026 is that I’m one of the few people who can make you feel good about living today. Progress is not a straight line necessarily, but if you had that medical crisis a hundred years ago, it would’ve been a lot worse. I’ve been through cancer myself. Today is the best day to be alive and to get that diagnosis and tomorrow will be a little bit better, because I’m an optimist when it comes to medicine. I’m hoping that chemotherapy or radiation treatments will become obsolete. They’re brutal. And there’s a cancer vaccine on the horizon. Ars Technica: In the US, sadly, we’re going backwards on vaccines for preventable diseases like measles. Lindsey Fitzharris: I know. We have short memories. We don’t remember. It’s weird to me as a medical historian because I remember it all. I remember the times when parents lost their children to preventable diseases. It’s coming back, and it’s sad to see that repeat. If I could just show people the diaries and the letters of brokenhearted parents who lost child after child to measles or polio and all of these diseases we can now treat and prevent. I put up pictures of smallpox on social media, and people think it’s AI. They can’t believe that something like that existed. It’s the only human virus we’ve ever eradicated. It should be celebrated. So I’m always out there banging the drum. I hope that we steady the ship a bit and people learn from the past again.

Apple releases iOS and macOS 27.2 with new Siri AI features, new Music logo
2026-10-26

Apple releases iOS and macOS 27.2 with new Siri AI features, new Music logo

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-26

Visit Original Website
Today, Apple rolled out the 27.2 updates for its operating systems, including iOS, macOS, watchOS, tvOS, visionOS, and so on. Let’s start with iOS 27.2. This update is heavily focused on Siri AI. It adds a memory feature to Siri, so it can “recall your past conversations” and reference them when chatting with you. It also expands what data sources Siri can access when giving responses to include voicemails, Messages attachments, and more. On the flip side, there is now an “Excluded Apps” option that allows you to “choose which apps Siri, Search, and suggestions can access.” Further, Siri AI is now available in five more languages beyond English, as previously announced: French, Japanese, Korean, Portuguese, and Spanish—though Siri AI remains unavailable in Europe (and mainland China). Other additions include context-aware suggestions within Messages (like turning a message inviting you to something into a calendar entry), SynthID watermarking support in Photos, and support for user profiles in the Apple TV app. There’s also the new Apple Music branding, plus the arrival of previously promised Apple Pencil support on the iPhone Duo foldable. The macOS Golden Gate 27.2 offers some of the same things: contextual memory for Siri, the same additional Siri AI languages, and miscellaneous unspecified bug fixes. The same goes for the Apple Watch—again, mostly the same Siri-related updates. visionOS 27.2 includes the new languages and updated Music icon, as well. Also, as previously discovered in the beta releases of macOS 27.2, there are some references to new gesture recognition capabilities in applications like Photos, Notes, and Safari—which may be related to unconfirmed reports of touch support on the next MacBook Pro refresh, which may be revealed at a scheduled public briefing this Wednesday. Apple’s own iOS and macOS update notes are below. Siri AI (All iPhone 16 models and later, iPhone 15 Pro, iPhone 15 Pro Max) This update provides enhancements to Siri AI with the ability to retain useful context from previous Siri interactions as well as draw from a wider range of personal context. It also introduces Suggestions in Messages that help you take quick actions in apps like Calendar and Reminders based on context from your conversation. This release includes additional features, bug fixes, and security updates for your Mac.

Apple reveals updated iPad mini with a bigger OLED screen for $700
2026-10-26

Apple reveals updated iPad mini with a bigger OLED screen for $700

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-26

Visit Original Website
The iPhone Duo may be a bit of a risk right now, but there’s a new mid-size Apple touchscreen device that might do the job. Starting at $699, the 8th-gen iPad mini price has gone up, but there are some big changes. Apple says the refreshed 8th-gen iPad mini marks the most significant update ever for its petite tablet, featuring an OLED screen and the A20 Pro chip that we first saw in the iPhone 18 Pro and iPhone Duo. The new iPad mini has roughly the same footprint as the 2024 release, but the display is slightly larger thanks to a 24 percent reduction in bezel thickness. The device is almost a full millimeter thinner than the last iPad, coming in at just 5.4 mm. The chassis is also IP67 rated for water and dust protection. The 7th-Gen iPad mini didn’t have any official IP rating. The 8.5-inch Super Retina XDR display (up from 8.3 inches) moves on from the IPS technology of the last-gen device to adopt OLED. That means dapper blacks and a much higher peak brightness—1,200 nits versus just 500 nits for the previous mini. It can also manage 1-nit minimum brightness so as not to sear your retinas in a dark room. There will also be a nano-textured matte display option. The display experience on the new mini should be much more in line with what we’ve come to expect from Apple’s Pro-level devices. Credit: Apple Credit: Apple The 2-nanometer A20 Pro chip was a noticeable upgrade in the iPhone 18 Pro, and it will be even more significant in this tablet. The previous model had a 3-nanometer A17 Pro chip, which was already a year old when it appeared in the iPad mini. In the iPhone 18 Pro, we found the A20 Pro significantly improved graphics performance thanks in part to a 50 percent increase in memory bandwidth. Higher memory speed also helps with on-device AI applications, but the Neural Engine is the primary factor. Unfortunately, not all versions of the new iPad mini will be equally capable. Apple notes the 512GB configuration will have the dual 16-core Neural Engine we saw in the iPhone 18 Pro, but the 128GB and 256GB versions will have a single 16-core NPU. These versions will also have a slightly different CPU configuration, with five cores instead of six. Apple has retained the 12 MP cameras for the front and back of the tablet, but the front-facing one has changed location. Rather than being on the short edge for portrait-orientation video calls, it’s now on the long edge. That might take a little getting used to for those who frequently use their tablets for FaceTime. Credit: Apple Credit: Apple The $699 base price gets you 128GB of storage and Wi-Fi connectivity. It’s $100 more for 256GB and $300 more for 512GB. The nano-texture display is also a $100 upsell, but it’s only available for the 256GB and 512GB versions. The step up to cellular will cost $849 for the 128GB model. Pricing is, sadly, not eye-popping for 2026, but it marks yet another price increase for Apple. The 7th-gen iPad mini launched for $499 in 2024, but it has since risen to $599. The new model adds $100, but it does have some nice upgrades. Preorders for the new iPad mini begin at 10:10 am PT on October 28 (Wednesday), and devices will arrive on doorsteps and in stores on November 6.
Ars Technica - All content
ICE plan to buy robot dogs spurs protest from hundreds of Boston Dynamics employees
2026-10-26

ICE plan to buy robot dogs spurs protest from hundreds of Boston Dynamics employees

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-26

Visit Original Website
Hundreds of employees at the US robotics firm Boston Dynamics signed a petition calling for executives to halt a sale of the company’s robot dogs to US Immigration and Customs Enforcement—but executives stood by the deal, while saying the robots would not be weaponized. On September 25, the Department of Homeland Security contract awarded $1.3 million to a Connecticut company called Radeco to provide ICE with modified versions of Boston Dynamics’ quadruped robots. But Boston Dynamics workers sent the petition letter with more than 300 signatures—half of them anonymous—to the company’s executives in early September and shared copies with The Boston Globe and WBUR public radio station. “Many of us have gotten messages from friends and family, asking us in disbelief and horror if this news is true, and how we can live with ourselves if it is,” according to the petition. “The risk for Boston Dynamics is simply too great to continue with this deal.” The petition described the signatories as employees who normally “love showing off what we do to our friends, family and the world” and who “love challenging ourselves to innovate and change the perception of what robots can do.” The 300 signatures come out of a total Boston Dynamics workforce of approximately 1,400 people. The ICE contract covers the purchase of four Boston Dynamics Spot robots that were modified by Radeco to be marketed as a robot platform named Cerberus. The contract describes these Cerberus robots as being intended to “detect radiation, toxic gas leaks and explosive threats in urban environments or disaster zones,” along with enabling other remote observation and inspection missions. A centerpiece of the upgraded robot is an oxygen sensor that is intended to “ensure the air quality is breathable when personnel are placed in confined spaces such as tunnels.” The Cerberus robot dog also has a camera equipped with 30-times zoom capability, 360-degree cameras, and infrared thermal imaging. The Cerberus robot dog would come with an optional Spot robotic arm that enables it to manipulate objects and open doors, along with providing additional camera views when extended. The ICE contract describes the robot as being generally capable of navigating complex industrial environments on its own and executing “repeatable autonomous missions with minimal human intervention.” Such a purchase was justified in the ICE contract as supporting the Homeland Security Task Forces’ mission to carry out “counter-illicit cross-border tunnel operations” and dismantling “cross-border human smuggling and trafficking networks” being conducted by “criminal cartels, foreign gangs and transnational criminal organizations throughout the United States.” “Spot robots are tools that help keep people out of harm’s way,” a Boston Dynamics spokesperson told Ars in a statement. “Regardless of any political implications or personal feelings about the organizations involved, if our robot can help keep people safe, we believe it should.” Boston Dynamics has also emphasized that the robots would primarily be used by ICE’s Homeland Security Investigations unit that is focused on combating transnational criminal organizations, according to WBUR. However, the Brennan Center for Justice has documented the Homeland Security Investigations unit as frequently being involved in deporting undocumented workers and conducting workplace raids during the first administration of President Donald Trump. These operations occurred between 2018 and 2021, before being halted by US Secretary of Homeland Security Alejandro Mayorkas early in the term of President Joe Biden. During Trump’s second term that began in January 2025, the Homeland Security Investigations unit once again diverted thousands of agents to immigration enforcement and deportations after one of Trump’s executive orders redefined the unit’s primary mission, according to the Immigration Policy Tracking Project. The Boston Dynamics employee petition specifically referenced an ICE and Homeland Security Investigations workplace raid that detained 475 primarily South Korean workers at the Hyundai Motor Group Metaplant America facility in Georgia on September 4, 2025. Hyundai Motor Group became the parent company of Boston Dynamics after acquiring the US robotics company in June 2021. “We cannot be proud of working at Boston Dynamics when that work is being used by a government agency that brutalizes civilians, including some of our own colleagues at Hyundai,” according to the employee petition. “All elements of the ICE organization, not just Enforcement & Removal Operations, have worked to sow fear among all people living in this country.” That single largest immigration enforcement operation of its kind sparked public outrage in South Korea and led to South Korean leaders questioning the country’s business investments in the United States. However, Hyundai has maintained its operations at the US electric vehicle production at the Metaplant America factory and still plans to deploy thousands of Boston Dynamics’ Atlas humanoid robots starting at its US facilities. The Boston Dynamics leadership team has stood by the ICE contract with Radeco, which would mainly involve Boston Dynamics providing technical support for the modified robots, according to The Boston Globe. The company also responded to the employee petition via an email from Amanda McMaster, former interim CEO for Boston Dynamics. That email was obtained and quoted in part by both The Boston Globe and WBUR. “We believe this decision is in line with our purpose of ethically advancing intelligent machines in service of humanity,” McMaster wrote in her email. She urged workers to “respect and accept this decision, even if you do not agree with it.” McMaster also pointed to the company’s terms and conditions for sale of the Spot robots that prohibit “intentional use of the Product to harm or intimidate any person or animal, as a weapon, or to enable any weapon.” The company previously signed an open letter opposing the weaponization of “advanced-mobility general-purpose robots.” The Boston Dynamics spokesperson told Ars that the company has “worked productively” with the Department of Homeland Security and Homeland Security Investigations unit for years, and said the latter have abided by the company’s terms and conditions of sale. The robotics company said it would also “take immediate action” if any customer misused Spot, although it said “there have been no cases of a customer violating those terms and conditions” while working with the Department of Homeland Security and other “public safety agencies.” “That would include specific steps like voiding the warranty, refusing all future software updates, terminating their license, taking any appropriate legal action available to us, and likely never selling to that customer again,” said the Boston Dynamics spokesperson. The Boston Dynamics employee backlash to the ICE contract comes as the company is developing a new advanced robotics and AI facility in Waltham, Massachusetts, near its headquarters. The $100 million project includes creating 1,250 new jobs by 2033, according to a company press release. The ICE contract represents “a complicated and emotionally charged topic for many people, including some of our own employees,” said the Boston Dynamics spokesperson. “As our company continues to grow at a rapid pace, we will continue welcoming a highly diverse group of employees with views on every side of the social and political spectrum.” But as Boston Dynamics ramps up its production capabilities and deployment of robots such as Spot and Atlas, the employees who signed the petition expressed concerns that the company’s association with the ICE contract will threaten both workforce hiring and retention. “We fear we will lose teammates over this decision, and worry about the risk it creates to achieve our shared company goals,” according to the petition. “This decision makes Boston Dynamics a less attractive employer—it will make it more challenging to build the team we need as we scale.”
Fortune | FORTUNE
Anthropic’s S-1, by the numbers: A thriving business—with a $441 billion asterisk
2026-10-27

Anthropic’s S-1, by the numbers: A thriving business—with a $441 billion asterisk

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Around 9 AM on October 26, Anthropic unveiled one of the most eagerly awaited documents in Big Tech history, its prospectus for an initial public offering. Now that the Claude purveyor has filed its S-1 with the SEC, it’s free to begin a roadshow in 15 days, and pursue a timetable that would see its shares trading on the Nasdaq, under the symbol ANT, as early as mid-November. At an anticipated valuation in the $1.8 to $2.0 trillion range, Anthropic would rank second only to SpaceX as the largest market debut of all time. Given its central importance both as a potential investment and bellwether for the future of AI, Wall Street research crews and everyday investors alike are carefully parsing the over 400 page document. And one big concern dwarfs all others: Can Anthropic generate revenues so gigantic that they far outpace the huge spending on compute its leaders already acknowledge is coming? Evidence that’s happening would provide much comfort before folks and funds wrestle with the next and more vexing head-spinner: Will any reasonable projections for its growth in sales and operating leverage prove great enough to make Anthropic worth anything like $2 trillion? So far, the S-1 shows surprisingly excellent bedrock performance, and boasts great financial strength. On the potential downside, it’s committed to big spending on AI capacity that it can’t cancel, meaning that the expenses are locked in while the sales are far less certain. Let’s start with the positive operating news. For the first six months of 2026, revenues exploded 14x from $1.16 to $16.3 billion over the same period last year. But a big surprise comes from disentangling the income statement. Under GAAP, Anthropic suffered a staggering loss of $80 billion in the first half of 2026. But the vast bulk of that paper deficit arose from changes in fair value of convertible notes. Back out that non-cash charge and several others including equity compensation, and Anthropic actually garnered adjusted operating income of $1.62 billion for the first six months of 2026, for a 10% margin. So far, the cash flow scenario also looks favorable. For first half 2026, Anthropic collected $3.93 billion in cash from operations. Its capex, the item that’s mushrooming at SpaceX, was so tiny this writer did a double take: Just $482 million. As a result, Anthropic registered positive free cash flow of around $3.45 billion. Operating leverage, the ability to expand margins by growing revenues faster than costs, is also pointing the right way. Antrhopic defines the metric as “compute efficiency.” It measures the extent to which top-line growth is outpacing its largest expense item, the data center capacity that runs Claude, Fable, and Mythos. In Q4 of 2025, Anthopic collected just 67 cents in sales for every dollar in infrastructure costs, for an efficiency ratio of 0.69. The number went strongly positive in Q1, and in Q2, Anthropic garnered $11.6 in revenues versus $5.6 billion in costs of compute, for an efficiency ratio of 2.08. Customer stats are also impressive. The S-1 disclosed that Anthropic now harbors a base of 11,900 enterprises featuring over $100,000 a year in revenue, up from 950 as of September 30, 2025. The balance sheet’s super-solid: Anthropic’s holding $131.7 billion in cash and short-term investments, following a round that raised $65 billion in late May. That’s more than for Apple ($55 billion), Microsoft ($77 billion), Amazon ($123 billion), and Alphabet $126 billion). The IPO’s expected to amass another $100 billion, hoisting its reserves by late this year to well over $200 billion. Anthropic will need that war chest, and more. The S-1 details eight contracts listed under “infrastructure service agreements” and “infrastructure equipment leases,” the roster of partners comprising the likes of Microsoft, Amazon, and SpaceX. The grand total for these deals comes to $657 billion. The key takeaway: $498 billion or 76% of the dollar value of those arrangements is “non-cancellable.” Anthropic’s obliged to pay whether or not it uses the capacity it’s reserved. In fact, Anthropic’s widening its strategy for attaining new compute. Instead of leasing from the likes of Microsoft where their partner pays for and installs the GPUs and other gear that account for around 80% of a data center’s cost, Anthropic is now going “hybrid” by in some cases buying the chips and assuming the cost of equipping “dedicated” centers operated by others. That’s a highly capital-intensive approach that, judging by its tiny capital spending so far, is totally new. Going forward, it’s clear that Anthropic’s cash outlays for capacity will explode. The hope is that we’ll see a continuation of the current trend where revenues jump much faster. But the approximately $441 billion in guaranteed commitments is something investors should pay attention to. Most of the contracts appear to run seven years or less. So that’s at least $60 billion annually that’s got to be covered by jackrabbit sales. Plus, Anthropic acknowledges that the capacity budget will grow from here, stating on page 197 its intention “to secure further compute beyond our existing commitments, [and] expand our own data center footprint to secure additional long-term compute.” Other items may rankle potential fans. One of them is possible dilution in the name of doing good. The board can issue an additional 2% of the outstanding shares each time the stock appreciates 50% over a “benchmark,” presumably starting at the offering price, to the “Anthropic Public Benefit Fund,” an institution dedicated to philanthropy. So if the stock rockets, each shareholder owns 2% less of the business. And if the stock price makes a round trip, they’re worse off. On balance, the S-1 showed that a basic business is thriving, and for now, Anthropic’s passing the main test by growing revenues much faster than the costs of compute. On the downside, it’s taking a huge, leap-of-faith wager that those sales will absolutely rampage from here. If they don’t, Anthropic will encounter the opposite problem, baked-in costs that ramp faster than revenues, a formula for disaster. Investors also need to decide if even in the best case, Anthropic can race at the virtually never-before-seen pace that would make it worth $2 trillion out of the starting gate. As the S-1 shows, so far the vision’s inspiring, and the numbers are good. It’s the size they’ve got to grow to that looks scary. As Anthropic’s IPO approaches, CFO Krishna Rao faces a defining test

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Good morning. Anthropic CFO Krishna Rao faces a defining question as the AI company prepares to go public: Can extraordinary revenue growth translate into sustainable profitability as the cost of advanced AI systems mount? Anthropic filed its Form S-1 registration statement with the U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission on Monday for a proposed Nasdaq listing under the ticker ANT. Revenue reached $16.29 billion for the six months ended June 30, up from $1.16 billion a year earlier. Net loss totaled $80.75 billion, largely reflecting a $72.39 billion accounting charge tied to changes in the fair value of financing instruments, Fortune reported. Operating loss narrowed to $693 million from $2.14 billion. Founded in 2021 and led by CEO Dario Amodei, Anthropic develops the Claude family of AI models and products, including Claude Code and Claude Cowork. In May, the company raised $65 billion in a funding round that valued it at $965 billion post-money.Growth raises infrastructure stakes Anthropic’s enterprise business is expanding rapidly. As of Sept. 30, more than 11,900 customers generated at least $100,000 in revenue for the company over the trailing 12 months, including approximately 250 that generated at least $10 million. For Rao, Anthropic’s first CFO, appointed in 2024, financing that growth means securing the computing capacity needed to sustain it without allowing infrastructure commitments to overwhelm the business. On the Invest Like the Best podcast in May, Rao said he devotes 30% to 40% of his time to compute infrastructure. “The compute that we procure is the lifeblood of our business,” he said. Anthropic uses Amazon’s Trainium chips, Google’s tensor processing units and Nvidia’s graphics processing units. Compute and infrastructure expenses reached $8.95 billion in the first half of 2026, up from $2.33 billion a year earlier. Anthropic says it currently makes more than two dollars in revenue for every dollar it spends on computing infrastructure; but that could change if the AI lab is forced to reduce token prices, a risk it warns about in its S-1 filing, Fortune reported. Although revenue growth has outpaced the increase in this major cost category, that alone doesn’t establish improving unit economics or a clear path to profitability. Other expenses, contractual commitments and future capacity needs will also shape returns. Investors weigh returns and risksDan Ives, a partner and senior managing director at Yorkville Ives & Co., told me the filing is “a watershed event for the tech sector and AI Revolution.” Analysts at the firm highlighted compute constraints, limited shareholder influence and potential conflicts between Anthropic’s public-benefit mandate and financial returns. “The near term losses are massive, but investors on the IPO will be laser focused on the growth trajectory into 2027 and 2028,” the analysts wrote in a note. Raj Joshi, senior vice president of Moody’s Ratings Corporate Finance Group, told me in an email that the filing would “shine a brighter light on the AI labs’ finances, as well as contracts with Big Tech companies, enhancing investors’ ability to assess risk across the sector.” Anthropic also warns that falling behind competitors in developing advanced AI models could weaken demand even as infrastructure costs remain substantial. For Rao, the test is whether Anthropic can sustain its extraordinary growth while improving operating performance and managing its computing commitments. Investors must weigh that opportunity against competitive pressures and governance risks. Jamere Jackson was appointed CFO of FedEx Corp. (No. 50), effective Nov. 30. He succeeds Claude Russ, who has served as interim CFO since June 1. Jackson brings extensive experience leading the finance function at several public companies. Most recently, he led both finance and store development at AutoZone, where he has served as CFO since January 2021. Before AutoZone, Jackson served as executive vice president and chief financial officer of Hertz Global Holdings and as chief financial officer of Nielsen Holdings. Earlier in his career, he held a variety of leadership roles at General Electric, including chief financial officer of a division of GE Oil and Gas. Together, these trends point to sustained support for corporate leadership, more selective shareholder-proposal activity, and an expanding range of tools for shareholder engagement. Samsung Electronics exec: the investments that endure build more than factories
2026-10-27

Samsung Electronics exec: the investments that endure build more than factories

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Paul Cho is President and Head of Device Solutions Americas, Samsung Electronics. In Taylor, Texas, Samsung is preparing to open a new advanced semiconductor manufacturing facility. It began as the largest investment we had ever made in the United States. But the most important thing about Taylor is not the size of the facility or the dollar figure attached to it. It is what is being built around it. This approach reflects a lesson Samsung has learned over nearly five decades of investing, manufacturing and hiring in the United States: putting up capital is not the same as putting down roots. Samsung has invested more than $100 billion in the U.S. during that time and has been manufacturing semiconductors in Central Texas for more than 30 years, with the total investment in Texas expected to reach around $40 billion by the end of this year. Our U.S. operations, anchored by our semiconductor operations in Central Texas and Silicon Valley, generate more than $40 billion in economic impact to the local economies and support more than 175,000 jobs. In 2025 alone, our Austin and Taylor campuses contributed $10.9 billion to the Central Texas economy. Many of our supply chain partners are following suit by relocating or expanding their existing presence in the area. A semiconductor fab can be built in a few years, but becoming part of a local economy takes much longer. It requires skilled workers, suppliers equipped to grow alongside new investments, and educational institutions that prepare people for emerging opportunities. That commitment is reflected in Samsung’s new $22.84 million investment in Central Texas this year that will help build a sustainable pipeline of semiconductor talent. The investment includes a new community engagement program, Samsung’s 5-Star Workforce Development partnership plan, a new industrial trades training program, and an expansion of our paid internship program with Taylor Independent School District (ISD). Samsung is also deepening its partnerships with The University of Texas at Austin, Texas A&M University, local community colleges, and high schools across Central Texas. These partnerships support scholarships, fellowships, curriculum development, training programs, and educational resources. That commitment extends to Silicon Valley, where our semiconductor headquarters has been based since 1983. Fabs depend on skilled people, so we invest in the educators and mentors who shape young people’s paths, reaching more than 50,000 students and 800 educators locally each year. We equip teachers with real-world semiconductor skills, including a fellowship that embeds an educator with our R&D engineers to build classroom-ready STEM lessons. Whether in Texas or California, the goal is the same: a workforce ready for the opportunities this industry will create. Our efforts to lift up the next generation of talent go beyond semiconductors. Our experience across the U.S. — from home appliance manufacturing in Newberry, South Carolina, to community STEM programs like Solve for Tomorrow — has shown that long-term value depends on equipping local talent to innovate. Companies cannot not wait until a position needs to be filled to invest in that capacity. As the skills required across manufacturing and technology change, workers will need more accessible pathways into new careers — and young people will need earlier opportunities to see themselves as innovators and problem-solvers. Since 2010, more than 620,000 students have participated in Solve for Tomorrow, with Samsung providing technology and classroom resources to more than 4,300 schools nationwide. The projects vary widely, but they begin with the same question: What is a problem in my community, and how can I help solve it? Giving students the tools to answer that question is different from building a manufacturing facility, but both can leave a community better prepared for what comes next. This is why global scale and deep local roots are not competing ideas. The strongest global companies measure success not only by what an investment produces today, but by what workers, institutions, and communities are equipped to do tomorrow. As the United States marks its 250th anniversary, much of the conversation about its economic future is focused on what the country must build next. Communities receiving new investments have every right to ask a related question: What will remain after the announcement is made and the ribbon is cut? The answer should be more than a facility or a dollar figure. It should be people with new skills, stronger pathways into emerging industries, suppliers prepared to grow, and institutions equipped to support future opportunities. Capital can build a factory. Durable investment builds the capabilities that allow companies and communities to keep moving forward together. If you block an AI agent, you are teaching it to recommend someone else
2026-10-27

If you block an AI agent, you are teaching it to recommend someone else

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Dvir Ginzburg is the founder and CEO of Encore AI, which builds AI agents for revenue-generating interactions. Earlier in his career, Ginzburg built AI recommendation systems at Facebook and Microsoft. He holds a PhD in deep learning. Encore publishes the Agent Readiness Index cited in this piece. For about 20 years, the smart engineering decision was to keep automated traffic off your website. CAPTCHAs, bot walls, rate limits, and phone trees were all built on a sound assumption: that software arriving without a person behind it was probably there to scrape, defraud, or overload you. For the most part, this assumption was correct, but it’s the wrong approach for today. Both technology and the people using it have changed. A growing share of the automated traffic hitting a business now belongs to their own customers, who have started sending personal AI assistants to book flights, pay bills, compare policies, and sit through hold music on their behalf. Traditional website defenses cannot tell the difference, and end up treating the customer as the threat. Most of the discussion about this has centered around access, which is the wrong frame. The better question is not whether an agent gets in today, but what the agent learns when it does not. An agent does not experience a block the same way a person experiences a locked door. There is no moment of indignation and no complaint to customer service. What the agent records is a failed trajectory, or a sequence of attempted steps that did not reach its goal. That record does not disappear when the session ends, and it compounds in three places: I spent years building AI recommendation systems, and the pattern is familiar. In a recommender, an item that never converts gets down-ranked. There is no judgment nor intent in that. The system is doing what it was designed to do, which is to stop spending attention on things that do not work. The asymmetry here is what makes it dangerous. When you frustrate a human customer, you usually find out. They call, they email, they post. When you turn away an agent, there is no signal at all. The traffic does not appear in your funnel, the loss does not appear in your reporting, and the customer never learns that you were an option. You are removed from consideration sets you cannot see by software your customers trust. At Encore, we build AI agents for revenue-generating interactions, and we wanted to know how widespread this is. So rather than survey companies, we modeled the most popular personal AI assistants today, and sent them to visit more than 1,500 organizations across 25 industries. They identified themselves on arrival, read only public pages, and never signed in. We publish the results as the Agent Readiness Index, including every check and weight, so anyone can recompute a score. As of Oct. 23, 94% of sites could not accept an agent connection at all, and 26% blocked agents that declared themselves. What gets rejected there is a label, not a capability. Most personal assistants run an ordinary browser on a machine of their own. They can open Chrome and move through a login page much as a person would, and by getting login credentials from their users they pass right through. That is the real failure. The defenses now in place filter for honesty rather than for risk. An agent that announces what it is gets stopped at the door. The ones using a customer’s password have to be identified afterwards, from the trail they leave. Banks do that by reading the markers on every request, the address it came from, the device and browser it claims, and behavioral signals such as where a visitor clicked, how long they looked and where they paused. That is how banks have spent the past two weeks finding agents operating inside active customer accounts. Only 1% of the organizations we scanned offer a customer any way to approve an agent for a limited task with access they can withdraw. The fix is not to let everything in, it’s to become legible. State in machine-readable terms what you accept and what you do not. Offer agents a direct connection rather than an interface built for someone with eyes and a mouse. Give customers a way to delegate a narrow task for a limited time, using the existing open standards. Legibility is better than permissiveness, because a business that declares its rules can enforce them, while a business that blocks indiscriminately can only hope. What does the experience look like after this is fixed? An agent arrives carrying structured intent, which is a precise statement of what the customer wants, before the customer gets there. No channel in the history of commerce has handed a business that. Once you can work with agents safely, you can build offers they can act upon and products they can complete in a single step. Every company I talk to has a mobile strategy and a social strategy. Almost none has an agent strategy, and in our scan only 6% had published any position on AI agents at all. That is not a considered stance. It is the absence of one, enforced by infrastructure that was built for a web that no longer exists. The Netflix layoffs reveal what corporate America is getting wrong
2026-10-27

The Netflix layoffs reveal what corporate America is getting wrong

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Paul Hardart is a professor at NYU Stern School of Business. Last week, Netflix eliminated roughly 1,000 jobs and replaced its chief content officer, Bela Bajaria, who had spent years helping transform the streaming service into a global entertainment powerhouse. Netflix said Bajaria was “leaving” after nearly a decade. She wasn’t departing to “spend more time with her family” or to “pursue other interests.” Whatever the circumstances of her departure, she was out. Donald Trump turned firing people into a catchphrase on The Apprentice. But somewhere along the way, corporate America seems to have turned that sentiment into a management philosophy. Her replacement, Peter Rice, knows the experience firsthand. In 2022, Rice was abruptly dismissed from his senior position at Disney by then-CEO Bob Chapek, reportedly catching himself and much of Hollywood by surprise. Only months later, Chapek himself was fired, learning of his own dismissal via a phone call at home hours before he was scheduled to introduce Elton John at a concert at Dodger Stadium that was being live streamed on Disney+. In his recent memoir, Behind the Castle Walls, Chapek reflects on the painful end to his decades-long Disney career, including the absence of an opportunity to say goodbye to the employees he had spent years working alongside. Why, he wondered, did they have to be so cold? Hollywood has always been a competitive, sometimes Machiavellian ecosystem. Executives come and go. Careers rise and fall. And nobody, regardless of their accomplishments, is entitled to lifetime employment. But when did treating people with dignity on their way out become such an inconvenience? To be fair, Netflix co-CEO Ted Sarandos publicly acknowledged Bajaria’s contributions. And companies have every right to change leadership when performance or strategy demands it. But there’s a difference between making a tough decision and making an unnecessarily harsh one. Bajaria, of course, will be fine. But what about the roughly thousand other employees who lost their jobs? Perhaps corporate boards believe that decisive dismissals send a reassuring message to Wall Street. There’s something almost George Steinbrenner-like about it: We’re steely, resolute and not afraid to make tough decisions. Perhaps we’ve become so enamored with the notion of appearing tough that we’ve forgotten the value of being decent. And to what end? Wall Street may reward companies for cutting costs. But there’s no market bump for being callous. And what about the longer-term implications for the businesses themselves? Think about the message an abrupt firing sends to the employees who remain. They watch someone who has dedicated years, sometimes decades, to an organization disappear almost overnight. What are they supposed to conclude? That loyalty matters? That their contributions will be remembered? That the company values them as individuals? Or that they, too, are ultimately completely disposable and that it’s just a matter of time. Multiple studies suggest that treating employees poorly during layoffs can have lasting business consequences. Columbia Business School research has found that layoffs perceived as unfair can undermine the commitment of employees who remain. Similarly, a 2023 study examining Airbnb’s layoffs found that employees who perceived the process as unfair reported lower levels of trust in management, reduced job satisfaction and greater cynicism. The researchers found that respect, dignity and honest communication were important in shaping employees’ perceptions of the layoff process. Not surprisingly, how you treat the people you fire impacts the people you retain. Companies spend enormous amounts of money recruiting, training and retaining talented employees. They hire consultants to study organizational culture and facilitate retreats, team-building exercises and trust falls. Organizations proclaim that people are their greatest assets—calling employees team members, cast members or even family. And yet, when it’s time to part ways, they sometimes treat those same great assets no better than obsolete office furniture or outdated technology. The contradiction is remarkable. Consider a remark Russell Vought, now director of the Office of Management and Budget, made in 2023. He said he wanted federal bureaucrats to be “traumatically affected”—to wake up not wanting to go to work. Whatever one’s political views about the size of government, surely there’s a distinction between eliminating unnecessary positions and deliberately demoralizing the people occupying them. Why should disagreement over the value of someone’s job translate into disrespect for the person doing it? One reason unions can be appealing is that they provide employees with protections against arbitrary or unfair treatment. Companies that resist unionization often argue that they can foster better relationships with employees without third-party intervention. Fair enough. But if you’re anti-union, shouldn’t you at least be pro-human? And this question is becoming more urgent. We’re entering an era in which artificial intelligence threatens to fundamentally reshape employment. Millions of workers, young and old, are understandably anxious about whether the skills they’ve spent years developing will remain valuable. Some positions will disappear. Others will change beyond recognition. Companies will face difficult decisions about their workforces, often for legitimate economic reasons. But if A.I. agents and technology are going to make employment increasingly uncertain, shouldn’t we at least get the human part right? I’m not suggesting that firing someone should become a prolonged ceremony or that companies should retain employees who are no longer contributing. Sometimes speed, confidentiality and decisive action are necessary. But what’s the cost of a little consideration? An honest conversation. A meaningful acknowledgment of someone’s contributions. A reasonable opportunity to say goodbye. Assistance in finding the next opportunity. A departure that doesn’t leave someone feeling humiliated. These aren’t extravagant benefits. They’re basic expressions of respect. And they may pay substantial dividends. Former employees can become future customers, business partners, advocates or even employees again. Remaining employees are more likely to trust leaders who demonstrate compassion when making difficult decisions. And prospective employees, and even customers, are paying attention as well. Reputations, after all, are built not simply on how organizations celebrate success, but how they handle uncomfortable moments. Employment is, at its heart, a social contract. Organizations provide compensation and opportunity. Employees contribute their time, energy, creativity and, often, a considerable portion of their identities. We all know that the contract won’t always last forever. But its conclusion shouldn’t nullify the value, the camaraderie and the humanity that came before. Most of us will experience some form of involuntary professional separation during our careers. And those who haven’t should remember that today’s person doing the firing may well be tomorrow’s person receiving the call. Just ask Bob Chapek. Or Peter Rice. As we hurtle toward a future increasingly shaped by artificial intelligence, perhaps the most important competitive advantage a company can cultivate is something distinctly human—a little dignity. It costs so little. And it may pay dividends for years to come. Current price of oil as of October 27, 2026
2026-10-27

Current price of oil as of October 27, 2026

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
As of 8 a.m. Eastern Time today, oil is trading at $115.13 per barrel, based on the Brent benchmark we’ll explain in a bit. That’s 28 cents below yesterday morning’s level and about $49 higher than where it stood a year ago. No one can say for sure where oil prices will go next. Many forces shape the market—but at the core, it’s still about supply and demand. When risks like a potential recession or war ramp up, oil prices can change direction quickly. When you buy gas at the pump, you’re covering more than the cost of crude oil. You’re also paying for every step in the process, including refineries, wholesalers, taxes, and the markup your local gas station adds. Even so, crude oil has the biggest influence on what you pay, often making up more than half the cost per gallon. When oil prices jump, gas prices usually climb right along with them. But when oil falls, gas prices often slip much more slowly—a pattern sometimes called “rockets and feathers.” If an emergency hits, the U.S. keeps a backup supply of crude oil called the Strategic Petroleum Reserve. It’s mainly there to protect energy security during crises, such as sanctions, catastrophic storm damage, even war. It can also help cushion the blow when supply shocks send prices soaring. It’s not meant to solve long-term problems. Instead, it provides quick relief for consumers and helps keep vital parts of the economy moving, like essential industries, emergency services, and public transit. Oil and natural gas are two of the world’s primary energy sources. A big change in oil prices can affect natural gas by extension. For example, if oil prices increase, some industries may swap natural gas for some segments of their operations where possible, which which increases demand for natural gas. When looking at how oil performs, two main benchmarks stand out: Of the two, Brent gives a better picture of global oil performance because it prices a large share of the world’s traded crude. It’s also the go-to for tracking oil’s historical trends. In fact, even the U.S. Energy Information Administration now relies on Brent as its primary reference in its Annual Energy Outlook. If you look at the Brent benchmark over several decades, oil has been far from stable. It has experienced sharp rises tied to wars and supply cuts, along with steep drops linked to global recessions and oversupply (called a “glut”). For example: To sum up, oil’s historical performance has been anything but smooth. Again, it’s heavily influenced by wars, recessions, OPEC whims, shifting energy policies, and much more. Looking to stay up-to-date regarding the latest energy developments? Check out our recent coverage: The current price of oil per barrel depends largely on supply and demand, including news about potential future supply and demand (geopolitics, decisions made by OPEC+, etc.). In the U.S., prices also move based on how friendly an administration is to drilling, as it can affect future supply. For example, 2025 saw the Trump administration move to reopen more than 1.5 million acres in the Coastal Plain of the Arctic National Wildlife Refuge for oil and gas leasing, reversing the Biden administration’s policy of limiting oil drilling in the Arctic. The price of oil updates constantly when the “futures” markets are open. A futures market is effectively an auction where people agree to buy or sell oil in the future. As long as people and companies are trading contracts, the oil price is changing. In short, shale is rock that contains oil and natural gas. Think of shale as energy yet to be tapped. The more shale the U.S. accesses, the more energy we’ll have—and the more easily oil prices can keep from spiking as much thanks to a greater supply. When oil is expensive, it tends to make everyday items cost more. This can be related to energy (your heating, gas utilities, etc.), but it’s also due to the logistics involved with making those items accessible to you. Shipping, for example, can affect the price of things at the grocery store, as it’s more expensive to get those products from warehouses and farms onto the shelf. Joseph is a staff writer on Fortune's personal finance commerce team. He's covered personal finance since 2016, previously serving as a reporter and editor at sites like Business Insider and The Points Guy. How might you build a career on a combination of interests?
2026-10-27

How might you build a career on a combination of interests?

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
TCS’ Virginia Lee explores her career in cybersecurity, the many ways you can find your calling and the cyber changing landscape. The head of cybersecurity Ireland at Tata Consultancy Services (TCS), Virginia Lee told SiliconRepublic.com that she accidentally found her way into a career in the cybersecurity sector. She said, “When I joined TCS as an automation engineer, I had no grand plan to build a career in cyber. I got the opportunity to work on a security-related product and quickly realised it combined many things I enjoyed, technology, problem-solving, continuous learning and working with diverse teams.” In time, the work became more interesting, she gained more exposure and the role often brought unique challenges. She said, “Before long, I moved into dedicated cybersecurity roles. Looking back, it is one of the happiest career accidents I’ve had. One thing I’ve learned since then is that not everyone follows a perfectly planned path into cybersecurity. “Many of the best professionals I know arrived through unexpected routes and that keeps the industry interesting.” Cybersecurity has moved from a specialist IT function to a board-level priority. When I started, conversations focused on technical controls and compliance. Today, cyber risk is recognised as a business risk that can impact operations, reputation, customer trust and overall business resilience. In the past, Cybersecurity was seen as an add-on with professionals reluctant to embrace new digital initiatives. Today, our job is to help the business evolve quickly and safely. When security and governance is built into cloud and AI projects from day one, organisations innovate with confidence rather than constantly stopping to test vulnerabilities later. The threat landscape has also become far more sophisticated over the course of my career. Organisations are managing increasingly complex environments including cloud adoption, digital transformation programmes and now the rapid emergence of AI. At the same time, the role of cybersecurity professionals has evolved. Technical expertise remains essential, but teams at TCS and across the industry need people who can communicate risk clearly, influence decision-making and work across different business units. Demand outpaces supply in specialist areas like threat hunting and incident management. However, I think the conversation has moved beyond simply talking about a skills shortage generally. Many organisations need experienced professionals with broad skillsets, while also needing clearer pathways for early-career talent. Misconceptions about the work create barriers too. Cybersecurity is still not fully understood by many students and career changers. People often assume it is a highly technical profession where every role requires deep coding knowledge. In reality, cybersecurity offers opportunities across risk management, governance, engineering, architecture, threat intelligence, digital forensics and many other disciplines. We need to challenge traditional hiring norms. Curiosity and willingness to learn determine success in cybersecurity far more than an individual’s academic background. We should not assume talent only comes from computer science degrees. Some of the best cybersecurity professionals I work with at TCS started in linguistics, business operations, or non-technical roles. They succeed because they bring strong problem-solving abilities and a genuine interest in how technology and risk intersect. Cybersecurity encompasses a wide variety of roles and our hiring needs to reflect that. To fix the talent bottleneck, the industry needs accessible entry points, expanded graduate schemes, apprenticeships and structured retraining programmes to support career changers. Retention isn’t an issue for us currently, but this is a competitive industry so retention requires constant attention. Cybersecurity is a fast-moving profession and security professionals want clear career pathways, continuous learning, and exposure to new technologies, industries, and experiences. Keeping talent comes down to offering opportunities across projects, clear progression and structured mentorship. From my experience, people stay when they see how their work contributes to a wider purpose. People join organisations for an opportunity, but they stay because they believe they can continue to grow. Future talent pipelines depend on adaptability. The skills we need in five or 10 years will differ from those we use today. Therefore, leaders need to develop people who can comfortably move across disciplines. Someone might start in a security operations centre and later transition into cloud security, architecture, governance, or advisory roles. AI is changing how cybersecurity teams operate by assisting with threat detection, security monitoring, incident response and data analysis. This efficiency enables teams to focus on higher-value, complex tasks. AI will not replace security teams, but it shifts the required skills. Demand is growing for specialists who understand AI governance, AI security, responsible adoption and risk management. With continued advancement, many roles can be supported by AI, but it does not negate the need for strong human technical talent. We see AI as a catalyst for new opportunities, and we are committed to building a future-ready workforce by developing AI-native talent. Some of the most valuable learning in cybersecurity comes from cross-sector collaboration. I work across multiple sectors myself and I often see organisations faced with similar challenges. The reality is organisations in financial services, healthcare, critical infrastructure and government all face similar threat patterns and operational hurdles. There is significant value in sharing knowledge, whether that is experiences or lessons learned. Stronger collaboration creates opportunities for professionals to learn from different environments, build broader networks and gain exposure to new ideas and approaches. Ireland has a strong cybersecurity community and continued collaboration between industry, academia and government will be important to build resilience across the sector and develop strong talent. Stay curious. Technology and threats change constantly and the professionals who thrive are those who never stop learning. Avoid narrowing your focus too early. Explore different areas of cybersecurity, gain exposure to multiple industries and focus on understanding the business context behind the technology. Build your network early, seek out mentors and engage with the wider cybersecurity community. Some of my best opportunities have come through collaboration and industry relationships. Strong networks and continuous learning build a durable career in cybersecurity. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Data intelligence platform Tracxn Technologies has published the Europe Tech 9m report, which explores funding, investors, exits, unicorns and city-level activity across Europe’s technology ecosystem for the first nine months of the year. A standout detail of the report is the figures showing that for the first nine months of 2026, between January and September, late-stage deals accounted for 53pc of all funding. The average late-stage deal more than doubled from around $68m in 2025 to roughly $153m in 2026. Another key figure from the report shows that Europe’s number of Unicorns, that is companies valued at $1bn or more, has risen. European technology companies produced 41 new Unicorns during the documented period this year, which is up from 16 in the first nine months of 2025. According to Tracxn, this is representative of around 21pc of the 200 new unicorns worldwide. The city of London accounted for 15 of the 41. Broken down geographically, London was responsible for raising $21.6bn in 2026, between January and September, which was up from $10.2bn in 2025. Paris ranked second, raising $7.4bn in 2026, against $4.1bn the previous year. Stockholm raised $4.1bn against $1.1bn and Munich followed with $3.4bn and Berlin with $2.0bn, against $1.8bn and $1.7bn in 2025. Across the report, the type of organisations raising large sums of money included AI companies, which raised the most of any group ($15.7bn), high performance computing groups ($7.7bn), military technology companies ($5.8bn), data centre infrastructure groups ($5.7bn) and companies operating in drones ($2.7bn).
