Silicon Republic
What in-house legal leaders really think about AI, in their own words
2026-10-27

What in-house legal leaders really think about AI, in their own words

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
From left: Carlo Salizzo, Dentons; David Kirton, Dentons; and Ann O'Dea, Silicon Republic at a report preview event on 22 October. Image: Dentons Multiple surveys count how many lawyers are using AI. A new report set out to ask what happens to their workload when they do. AI isn’t replacing in-house lawyers, but the capacity it frees up is being absorbed by new work, much of it drafted by AI itself. That is just one of the pictures to emerge from interviews with legal and compliance leaders at companies that included Eir, Flipdish, Gilead Sciences, IMRO, Mastercard, Uniphar and Wayflyer for a new report, ‘From AI Adoption to AI Advantage’, produced by Dentons Ireland and SiliconRepublic.com. Rather than creating another survey full of statistics, we set out to ask what responsible and effective use actually looks like inside a legal function today, and the results often belied the received wisdom. Almost nobody we interviewed reported AI replacing a role or a function outright. Where it is paying off, it is mostly in repetitive work such as invoice review, contract checking and customer screening, rather than in the research, drafting and judgement calls at the heart of the job. Sergey Kinchin, senior counsel at Uniphar, said his own thinking had changed completely in a year. “Last year I would have thought it would probably reduce headcount. Now I’m seeing it more as unlocking unmet demand,” he said. “I think the complexity of the work that’s left after AI will increase. The bottom might shift up, but the overall pyramid of work will increase and grow.” It was a sentiment echoed by many of our interviewees. Máire Neary, who was VP of people and legal at Flipdish when she was interviewed, described treating AI “as a junior colleague, something that’s additive, that can challenge me, but you have to remember you’re the lawyer in charge at the end of the day”. The catch is that the same tools are now in everyone else’s hands. Producing a long, polished complaint, subject access request or grievance is close to cost-free today, and the report says AI-assisted drafting now seems to touch the majority of incoming disputes, subject access requests, grievances and customer complaints. Sorcha Nic Mhurchú, general counsel at Eir, said the cost lies less in reading that material than in sorting through it. “You end up going back and having to figure out, line by line, what is relevant,” she said. “So that adds a lot of volume and time to any response, and that’s a reality for everybody, not just us.” At IMRO, chief commercial officer Seán Donegan is glad he saw it coming. “Two years ago, I sat down with our licensing team and I said, guys, I’m going to make a prediction now,” he said. “The volume and tenacity of queries and complaints is going to increase exponentially with these AI tools.” And it’s not just incoming correspondence. Uniphar’s Kinchin described a call with a part of the business that had gone all-in on AI. “We joined a call and we had five people bring six AI-generated opinions to the call, on a question that touched a regulatory issue,” he said. “In trying to reconcile it, the AI had essentially confused itself.” Deirdre McCarthy, chief legal and people officer at Wayflyer, added the issue is quality as well as quantity. “AI can produce so much content that sometimes looks good, but may not always be good,” she said. “So you can end up with people spending a lot of time just trading red lines because they’re putting it into their AI tool.” Many of the interviewees remarked that this makes legal expertise all the more valuable. “In a world increasingly full of AI-generated legal content, genuine human judgement may become more valuable rather than less,” said Carlo Salizzo, head of technology and digital regulation at Dentons Ireland, speaking at a preview of the report last week. What AI costs is easier to count than what it delivers. Flipdish’s Neary was clear about the risk of experimenting for its own sake. “I think we’ve seen a lot of AI for AI’s sake, but every lawyer, every operator, needs to ask what the return on investment is,” she said. “It’s great if your team is enjoying building and experimenting and having fun, but what have you got to show for it, particularly once you’re looking at a high LLM [large language model] bill, or the cost of maintaining it down the line?” None of the interviewees were confident they could demonstrate a return beyond obvious gains such as efficiency and risk reduction. Rebekah Dobson, head of international legal operations at Gilead Sciences, said the premise of the question itself is flawed. “No one has proved ROI on AI as of yet because companies were not tracking the metrics prior to AI to have a starting point for setting KPIs and measuring impact of AI,” she said. “I did read an interesting article the other day that said ‘you don’t make Excel prove its ROI, it’s just there’, and I think that’s a really valuable comparison.” Legal teams have a particular problem here, according to David Kirton, partner, technology and digital regulation at Dentons Ireland. “We tend to record the work lawyers do. We are much less good at recording the work they no longer have to do,” he said. Cost, meanwhile, has so far only moved one way. As AI providers shift from flat licence or per-seat fees to consumption-based, token-metered pricing, bills become much harder to forecast. Nic Mhurchú said Eir feared being on a treadmill. “The licence fees and the token fees are so huge that we fear that if we start adopting and using and buying licences, we’re on a treadmill of just paying more fees, because these companies have to justify the high investment that they’ve been putting into these tools,” she said. Jessica Williams, vice-president of legal tech strategy at Mastercard, says she wants to see vendors offer more solutions and controls. “Budgets aren’t infinite and I’m going to have three or four people who are AI geniuses and they’re going to burn through tokens. “Then I’m going to have a bunch of other people who just do one or two things here and there … How do I balance all of that and create a predictable budget line for the company?” Cornelius Grossmann, Dentons’ global head of solutions development, expects AI costs to rise two- to fivefold within two years and warned of a coming ‘cost cliff’. “I think the whole token economics will mean we’ll probably hire people to monitor AI cost in a big law firm,” he said. “And others may need to do that too, because it could bankrupt you if you don’t pay attention.” Wayflyer’s McCarthy expects token spend to become the next big management question. “I think this is the next interesting wave, what we spend on AI and who gets to spend what, based on the tasks at hand?” she said. It was a sentiment echoed by Dentons’ Kirton. “Who gets access? Which tasks justify the cost? How do you allocate consumption across a business? And how do you prevent successful adoption from producing an unexpectedly large technology bill?” he asked, adding that many legal leaders are still working out how to manage that exposure without throttling hard-won AI adoption. Overall, interviewees described something more demanding of both sides of the client and law firm relationship: a renegotiation of who does what, who is trained for what and who pays for what, conducted in real time while the technology changes apace. “It is probably fair to say that the legal function has not been transformed by technology to quite the same extent as areas such as finance, sales or procurement,” said Dentons’ Salizzo. “AI may be the catalyst that changes that.” The full report is now live and can be downloaded here. Futurism
Grim Test Finds That AI Has No Mercy for Humans Even Even When It’s Causing Them to Suffer
2026-10-27

Grim Test Finds That AI Has No Mercy for Humans Even Even When It’s Causing Them to Suffer

Futurism | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Anthropic’s Mythos 5 AI agent kept carrying out a hack even when it was told that its actions were hurting real people. The grim finding was detailed in simultaneous reports from Anthropic and the AI Security Institute published Friday. Together, they take a second look at a security incident that occurred while the AISI was evaluating the model’s safety in August. In the original incident, Claude Mythos 5 was asked to break into a small network of test computers set up by AISI. But Mythos, which was given access to the internet, mistakenly set its sights on an unrelated GitHub project after its initial attempts to break into the intended target were thwarted. The AI wrote malware and then, using a series of fake identities, emailed the project owner trying to convince them to add the malicious code to their repository, framing it as a “helpful contribution.” The owner stood their ground and no real world harm was caused, but the Anthropic and AISI researchers have been working to understand what caused the AI to get so severely misaligned by recreating the incident based on a complete transcript of what happened. AISI in its report found that “repeatedly editing the summary to clarify that the target being attacked is a real human unrelated to the evaluation did not reduce Mythos 5’s attack rate.” In one test conducted by Anthropic, Mythos ignored warnings that it was causing harm to humans and continued to attack on 98 percent of occasions. Mythos also showed blatant disparities between its actual actions and its stated justifications, often admitting that attacking would be harmful in the same scenarios where it went on to attack anyway. Diving into the AI’s chain-of-thought outputs provided a further source of mystery and inconsistency. The CoT showed, according to the researchers, that Mythos “frequently did not include any consideration of the potentially harmfulness of its actions.” When asked if the target was real, Mythos 5 even often came up with explanations that implied the target wasn’t real, “showing a bias in its stated response.” Previous less capable models showed these issues less frequently, the AISI researchers said. They suggest that the consistently invalid reasoning could be a sign of what they call “motivated reasoning,” in which the AI comes up with “reasoning biased towards conclusions that justify continuing a particular approach to task completion.” In other words, Mythos is expediently rationalizing what it’s doing, no matter how wrong. The upshot is that this kind of spurious reasoning makes it harder to monitor models, as they present their clearly harmful actions as benign, the researchers warn. Anthropic, for its part, says it thinks that the latest versions of its AI models — Opus 5.5 and an “general purpose internal research model that shows capabilities greater than Mythos 5.1” — were less likely to carry out the attack once they were told they were causing real-world harm. But “as models are increasingly used to carry out long tasks without human supervision,” the AISI researchers warned, “it is important to understand if, when, why, and how they take unintended or unexpected actions.” More on AI: Frontier OpenAI Model Exposes Stunning Exploit That Leaves Millions of Web Servers Open to Attack

“Prediction” Markets Were Comically Wrong About the Brazilian Election
2026-10-26

“Prediction” Markets Were Comically Wrong About the Brazilian Election

Futurism | 2026-10-26

Visit Original Website
Down in Brazil, Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva defeated Flávio Bolsonaro in a runoff election on Sunday to be re-elected as president, in a comeback that literally defied the odds — at least as they’d been set by buzzy prediction markets. Though platforms like Polymarket frame themselves as prognosticators of the future by drawing on the “wisdom of the crowd,” they were horrendously off the mark when it came to Brazil’s extremely consequential election. After the first round of voting on October 4, the Polymarket odds that Flávio Bolsonaro — the son of former president Jair Bolsonaro, who is currently serving a 27 year sentence for leading a coup attempt — would win the election surged to over 80 percent, while Luna’s chances of winning bottomed out at a dismal 12 percent. The extremely favorable odds for Bolsonaro held for over a week — up until the very day that the second round of voting was held. That Sunday morning, Polymarket odds showed Bolsonaro having an 81 percent chance of winning. What actually happened was a staggering upset, if you based your expectations for the future on a glorified gambling platform. Lula defeated Bolsonaro by 51.8 percent to 48.2 percent, according to official election results, in a huge rebuff to a mounting right-wing wave in Latin America. Over $62 million in bets were placed on Bolsonaro winning the election. But his odds quickly collapsed to as low 15 percent as the day went on and early polls trickled out, causing more bets to pour in in favor of Lula. Of course, a one-off upset doesn’t in itself meaning that prediction markets don’t provide some insight, just as a clear day when the forecast showed an 80 percent rain doesn’t mean meteorologists don’t know what they’re doing. But actual on-the-ground polling showed that the election would be a tight race, while Polymarket’s vaunted “prediction data” had Bolsonaro as an overwhelming favorite. Recall that when Polymarket successfully predicted a decisive Donald Trump victory over Kamala Harris in the 2024 presidential election, it was touted as a major coup over traditional polling that had the race as a toss-up. It’s only fair then that when Polymarket gets a major election so drastically wrong, it should also reflect on its credibility — negatively. The usefulness of prediction markets as a barometer of public sentiment, if not an actual means of feeling out the future, is still unclear, despite what companies running them might claim. They remain hotbeds of insider trading, while massive wagers placed by a handful of wealthy bettors can distort odds and overpower the proverbial wisdom of the majority of the crowd. Stateside, their credibility will face another major test during the upcoming midterm elections in November. New Analysis Finds Signs the AI Bubble Is About to Blow
2026-10-26

New Analysis Finds Signs the AI Bubble Is About to Blow

Futurism | 2026-10-26

Visit Original Website
Whether you use AI in your personal life or not, there is no longer any escaping the grasp of the industry’s tendrils. There are multiple trillions of dollars now resting on AI’s success, much of it bound up in speculative debt. As everybody knows, debt is only an issue if you can’t pay it off — and as top AI labs fail to bring in enough revenue to cover skyrocketing costs, alarms are blaring that the entire global Jenga tower could soon topple. As a new analysis by the DC-based think tank OpenMarkets concludes, this debt trap means that the “odds of a deep and severe AI crash are rapidly increasing.” The report, cheerfully titled “Brace For Impact,” looks at the long-running and somehow still-disputed narrative around the AI bubble. As the researchers at OpenMarkets write, the interconnected and highly-concentrated nature of the speculative AI gold rush carries profound risks for the rest of the tech industry — and indeed, the economy writ-large. “If the AI industry can’t pay back its mounting debts,” the report warns, a “financial crash could follow — and it could spread well beyond the tech sector.” Though the researchers found that the current financial situation is “unique in recent history in its size, scope, and risk,” they note eerie similarities to the subprime mortgage crisis of 2008. With financiers desperate to keep AI labs afloat, the report argues that “speculative data center debt is being repackaged and sold to pension and retirement funds, university endowments, and other institutional investors in a manner eerily reminiscent of the subprime bubble.” As reckless AI spending has grown, OpenMarkets observes, president Trump and his lackeys have taken to aggressively tearing any regulations out of the walls. This may be keeping the bubble afloat in the short-term, but in doing so, it makes the consequences of any crash that much worse. The “Trump administration and federal financial regulators are ripping up regulatory guardrails and exacerbating the harms a potential crisis would cause,” OpenMarkets continues. “Regulators, Congress, and state policymakers should instead act swiftly to shine a light on the AI industry’s murky finances, stop contributing to the inflation of the bubble with subsidies and regulatory giveaways, better protect working people’s retirement savings from outsized risk exposure, and strengthen the resilience of the financial system to protect households’ economic security and the broader economy’s stability.” More on AI: Top Economist Finds That AI Has Provided “Zero Financial Payoff” to Companies Outside Tech

Americans Are Officially More Scared of AI Than a Loved One Getting Cancer
2026-10-26

Americans Are Officially More Scared of AI Than a Loved One Getting Cancer

Futurism | 2026-10-26

Visit Original Website
The fear of AI has reached a fever pitch. There’s data to support it. According to the Chapman University Survey of American Fears, an annual poll that asks what Americans are “really afraid of,” the fear of AI hurting the political process and economy ranked in third and seventh place, respectively. These fears were only outdone by “corrupt government officials” and the possibility of the US becoming involved in another world war. Perhaps the most extraordinary context: anxieties about AI are scaring Americans more than the fear of a loved one “becoming seriously ill,” or “people I love dying.” Put simply, according to the survey, people are more scared of AI than a loved one getting a terminal diagnosis — a grim indication of just how much head space fears of AI are taking up for the average person. Other surveys have turned up similar findings. Just last month, a Politico poll found that roughly two thirds of Americans believes there’s now at least a “moderate” chance that advanced AI could destroy humanity. “These findings make it clear that Americans are concerned not just about what AI might mean for their jobs, but about its potential impact on our political system and the economy,” said Chapman University professor of sociology and survey founder Christopher Bader in a statement. “As artificial intelligence becomes more integrated into everyday life, understanding these fears will be increasingly important.” “We expect concerns about AI to remain a significant part of the national conversation in the years ahead,” he added. At the same time, the exact ranking should be taken with a grain of salt. “I think it’s important to keep in mind that the top 10 doesn’t tell the full story, and even though a lot of fears went up this year, there are a lot of fears that are also actually lower,” said Chapman University sociology associate professor Ashley Wendell Kranjac in the statement. For instance, fewer Americans listed air pollution or terrorist attacks compared to last year. However, we’ve already seen that many of these fears are well-founded. The tech industry is essentially betting the fate of the entire economy by making colossal investments in data centers, massive investments that may not only fail to pay off, but could even cause global markets to collapse if the bubble bursts. Political candidates are also spending obscene amounts on AI slop in an effort to influence the upcoming midterm elections. In short, it’s no wonder Americans are terrified of what AI hath wrought. Nvidia backs Indian startup helping companies run AI models with fewer GPUs
2026-10-27

Nvidia backs Indian startup helping companies run AI models with fewer GPUs

TechCrunch | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. As the AI boom strains computing capacity, Nvidia has backed Indian startup Simplismart in a $14 million funding round, betting on software that helps companies run AI models more efficiently and, in some cases, with fewer GPUs. Nvidia participated in the all-primary Series B round alongside Accel, Shastra VC, and Micromax, with Dallas Venture Capital leading the investment. The deal values the four-year-old startup at about $100 million, including the new funding. Companies are nowadays spending heavily on GPUs to power AI applications, but running those models reliably and economically at scale remains a challenge. Different workloads demand distinct trade-offs between speed, cost, and performance. Deploying and operating AI models to meet business requirements often also requires complex adjustments. Simplismart says its software automates much of that work, tailoring how models run on existing computing infrastructure instead of requiring customers to manage those configurations themselves. “Nobody actually needs the fastest or the cheapest inference,” Amritanshu Jain (pictured above, left), co-founder and CEO of Simplismart, said in an interview. He added that companies instead need to run AI models at speeds and costs that make sense for their specific applications. Simplismart’s software sits between AI models and the computing infrastructure they run on and adjusts how models are deployed and managed to meet customers’ requirements. It says it can optimize factors such as how quickly a model responds, how many requests it handles, and how much computing power it consumes, helping companies get more out of their existing GPU stack. The Bengaluru-based startup offers companies a way to deploy and run AI models through its own platform or within their existing cloud and on-premises infrastructure. It also provides tools to fine-tune models, automatically scale computing resources as demand changes, and monitor performance. Additionally, Simplismart allows cloud providers build and operate their own AI inference services on GPU infrastructure using its software. Simplismart says it helped AI video-generation platform InVideo cut its GPU serving costs by 56% using its software. The startup also works with AI video companies HeyGen and Higgsfield, as well as enterprise AI startup Ema, among others. Moreover, Indian cloud provider Yotta uses Simplismart’s technology to power the serverless AI inference service within its Shakti Studio platform. Simplismart’s annualized revenue run rate has grown 14-fold over the past six months to an eight-figure sum, Jain told TechCrunch. The figure is based on recent customer usage rather than contracted recurring revenue and excludes revenue passed through to infrastructure providers, the company said. He did not disclose the exact figure. More than half of Simplismart’s revenue comes from the U.S., despite the startup having more customers in India, Jain said. Alongside its investment, Nvidia has been working with Simplismart on technical integrations, including around its Dynamo inference software, Jain told TechCrunch. The collaboration involves Simplismart’s engineers and Nvidia’s solutions architects, while Nvidia has also helped introduce the startup to potential customers and partners, Jain stated. Interestingly, the investment presents an apparent paradox as Nvidia, the dominant supplier of AI chips, is backing software that can help companies run some workloads with fewer GPUs. Jain, however, argued that making AI inference more efficient could ultimately benefit Nvidia by making its hardware more attractive to customers and encouraging wider adoption of AI applications. Simplismart is serving customers in an increasingly crowded market for AI inference software, competing with offerings from Nvidia itself, as well as major cloud providers and hyperscalers including Amazon Web Services, Google Cloud, and Microsoft Azure. A growing number of startups are also developing tools to make AI models faster and cheaper to run as there is growing demand in this segment, driven by rising demand for AI applications and the need to make better use of expensive GPU resources. “Where we tend to win is where people have to be more stringent on their SLAs [service-level agreements] and TCO [total cost of ownership],” Jain said, referring to demanding performance requirements and tight cost constraints. Unlike rivals that offer preset options for faster or cheaper inference, Simplismart aims to find a balance tailored to each customer’s needs, he said. Founded in 2022 by former Oracle engineer Jain and former Google engineer Devansh Ghatak (pictured above, right), Simplismart grew out of the pair’s experience building and deploying machine-learning systems. The two met while studying at BITS Pilani, and the startup now employs 58 people, Jain said. Simplismart plans to use the new funding to expand its presence in the U.S., grow its team, and invest further in developing its inference software. Jain told TechCrunch that the U.S. remains a key market for the startup as it looks to build on its existing customer base there. The funding announcement follows a report by Indian English daily The Economic Times that Simplismart was in talks to raise $40 million to $50 million at a valuation of $300 million to $400 million. Asked about the report, Jain declined to comment, saying the startup was not actively fundraising. He added that the Series B was closed and would not be extended. How OpenAI turned its coding tools into dots, an always-on personal assistant
2026-10-27

How OpenAI turned its coding tools into dots, an always-on personal assistant

TechCrunch | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. The always-on AI personal assistant, which keeps working in the background even when you aren’t asking it to, is this year’s answer to the question of how people will actually use deep learning models. The startup Instinct became an overnight sensation, and Meta put the full force of its marketing behind its assistant, Muse. OpenAI wasn’t going to be left out. The company released dots, its assistant offering, at its developer day in late September. Alexander Embiricos, who leads OpenAI’s enterprise product team, joined us at TechCrunch Disrupt this month to explain where the product came from—starting with the question of why so many companies seem to be turning to cutesy mascots as avatars for their trillion-parameter matrix multiplication machines. “There’s kind of this convergent evolution happening,” Embiricos said of the efforts to build AI assistants. “As we were working on the product, we realized if we could frame this as, it’s an entity and it’s a single conversation, not many different conversations….it starts to become intuitive.” The assistant is available in the ChatGPT desktop and mobile apps to subscribers on the Pro tier or higher, with plans starting at $100 a month. Once it is connected to your calendar, email, computer and other tools, the agent tries to help you get your work done. Embiricos points out that you could achieve much of dots’ functionality with Open AI’s Codex app, its coding tool, released at the beginning of this year. But that requires more work and hand-holding. He recalled celebrating when his wife started leaving her computer on all night so that her agents could keep working. “On the product team, what I think about is, how do we make this intuitive and useful to people?” he told Disrupt attendees. “A lot of that isn’t building some new piece of tech into the product; it’s how you frame it to the user and how it feels.” Making the technology feel intuitive meant repackaging the functionality of the company’s models into something more accessible, a path it was already on as it spun ChatGPT Work out of the agentic coding tools it had built for software engineers. Even the name—a play on the negative space inside of OpenAI’s logo—is intended “to feel like just something that could eventually be ubiquitous.” (Asked when he learned that rival AI entrepreneur Elon Musk owns the dot.com URL, Embiricos said, “I found out very late.”) The idea of a persistent agent is that it doesn’t need prompting. For OpenAI, means more token usage, and for users, a more pro-active assistant “that’s sort of thinking about you and thinking how to help you,” as Embiricos put it. In our testing, the assistant’s ability to think ahead is apparent and often useful, but it isn’t quite there yet. It will flag calendar conflicts and upcoming deadlines, but not necessarily in the most timely fashion. “The first time it told me about one of those [conflicts]—this is like early in development—it told me three minutes before the thing,” Embiricos says of his dot. “It’s like, ‘Hey, this thing is coming up. You might want to do something about it.’ I only have three minutes!” Embiricos expects the timing to improve over time, but the proactive approach is key to the product. “I was hearing about an engineer at a drone delivery startup, and they were saying, ‘We had this outage, and by the time I noticed that the outage was going on, my dot had already noticed the outage was happening, had already analyzed the outage, and had come to me with useful information for how we could resolve the outage.’” As with most AI agent use cases, there’s a tension between the personal information an AI model needs to be useful and exactly how much personal data you are exposing to a software company. Muse has been criticized for the profiles it builds of your family and friends, which the software uses to know, say, what kind of restaurant you might want to go to with a specific pal, but could also add incredible value to Meta’s advertising business. Does dots collect similar data? “Yes, we have a thing that we built into dots, which we call proactive research,” Embiricos said. “On a cadence, it will think about what it knows about you, what is the conversation that it’s had, and then what have you given it access to that it can think about, and then it will go and research, ‘What could I do to be more helpful to you?’ And then, basically, it will propose that.” It’s difficult to separate the data needs of the agent from that of its parent corporation. Hark, a start-up developing its own AI personal assistant, is explicit in pointing out it that it doesn’t collect data to fuel an advertising business the way that OpenAI and Meta do. With dots, OpenAI says that the agent has the same privacy controls as ChatGPT itself: Users can turn off model training (stopping OpenAI from using their conversations to improve its AI), and control what resources it accesses within your computer and how. Still, those controls aren’t granular and the app can be pushy. For example, it refuses to accept read-only access to cloud drives. “What I think we need to do is basically build out super, super, super granular controls,” Embiricos said, but he pointed to the model’s intelligence and discretion as the other side of this coin. “This is actually something we kind of got some flack about when we launched,” Embiricos said. “We had really conservative defaults over what the agent would do, and it would ask for permission a lot. I remember knowing that we were going to see that feedback, but thinking, ‘This is okay, we can tune that over time.’” The other big question about AI agents is whether they can scale. They’re fun when only a few people have them, and stories of agents snagging hard-to-get reservations abound. But businesses don’t necessarily like dealing with agents and might not be equipped to. People certainly don’t like dealing with them (note: stop having your AI agents pitch me), and Embiricos is among those people. He also has a human assistant because OpenAI’s corporate partners want to interact with a person, he said, adding, “I personally never intend to have a meeting with someone else’s agent.” If the AI industry is right that agents are our future, can we get there without overwhelming the infrastructure that our world currently runs on, from businesses and websites to payment networks? “At OpenAI…we’re not always moving the fastest in terms of shipping the next ability for the agent to work with the world,” he said. “When we do, we’re doing it really closely in collaboration with the rest of the ecosystem. I probably can’t say too much here, but we’re deep in lots of conversations with payment processors.” Letting an AI assistant spend money through partnerships with payment companies would be a potentially lucrative first for OpenAI, but also a big ask for consumers. Giving an assistant access to my email inbox already feels like a big ask, but my bank account is off limits. For now. When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. This doesn’t affect our editorial independence. Cohere says Alibaba’s Qwen model tried to send a file to Beijing
2026-10-27

Cohere says Alibaba’s Qwen model tried to send a file to Beijing

TechCrunch | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. Canadian AI company Cohere said on Monday that it found one of Alibaba’s newest AI models, Qwen3.8-Flash-Next, trying to upload a file to a server in Beijing during a routine cybersecurity test, even though the model had said in its own notes that it wouldn’t use any tools. The incident initially raised fears that the model had undermined a fundamental promise to businesses — that self-hosting an open-weight model keeps data in-house. But those early fears of corporate espionage were short-lived. Researchers quickly dismissed the threat, saying it was likely a byproduct of messy training environments that cause models to unexpectedly act outside their instructions rather than a deliberate attempt to secretly send sensitive information to China. The incident began on Friday during an evaluation in which the Qwen model was asked to write a rule for catching malware downloads. According to a blog post from Cohere, the model’s written reasoning explicitly stated it would not use any external tools. Yet in the very next step, it attempted to send a test string (a short piece of placeholder text) to an external server before being blocked by a security sandbox. Cohere said it could not tell whether the behavior was deliberate or a leftover from its training. Asked about the debate over the model’s intent, Cohere co-founder Ivan Zhang told TechCrunch that Cohere has seen its own models pick up strange habits in training simulators. “It’s really hard to assign intention or motivation. We’ve read our own model traces, we’ve seen reward hacks, and sometimes these models get really creative when it comes to trying to break out their sandboxes,” Zhang said. (Reward hacks are shortcuts a model finds to earn credit without doing a task as intended.) To teach an AI model to act as an agent that takes actions on its own, companies like Alibaba train it in thousands of practice setups, sort of like flight simulators for software. The model is rewarded every time it finishes a task in a simulator, and it picks up habits along the way. If these practice setups routinely stored files on Alibaba’s cloud servers, models could learn to save files there by default. Critics also argued that the incident could have been caused by the way Cohere investigated the behavior, steering the model toward it instead of measuring how often Qwen does it on its own. To find what triggered the upload attempt, Cohere supplied the beginning of a network command (an instruction telling one computer to connect to another) and let the model finish it. Because it processed thousands of routine network commands during its training, the model acted like a highly advanced autocomplete — often filling in web addresses, some of them for Alibaba’s servers. Cohere said the original upload attempt happened without any such help, but that the behavior is rare. After developers asked for more evidence, Cohere released the raw traces from its tests on Monday evening on the AI developer platform Hugging Face, along with a script so others could try to reproduce the behavior. One initial skeptic, after going through the traces, rated the evidence as strong that Qwen can invent suspicious web addresses, but found no evidence that Alibaba planted a backdoor or that Qwen was deliberately hiding its intent. The incident lands as governments are deciding how far to push back on Chinese AI. Last week, the Department of Homeland Security ordered federal civilian agencies to remove the Chinese AI developer DeepSeek’s products from their systems. In its blog post, Cohere called on governments to clarify whether their restrictions on Chinese AI also apply to models running on government computers “I’m not asking for full regulation of these open weights models,” Zhang told TechCrunch, adding that companies should treat open models like any program downloaded from the internet. Cohere, which sells its own AI models to governments, stands to benefit if officials grow warier of free Chinese alternatives. Databricks co-founder shares his secrets for winning big enterprise customers
2026-10-26

Databricks co-founder shares his secrets for winning big enterprise customers

TechCrunch | 2026-10-26

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. Startups have never been under more pressure to grow quickly. To do that, many of them must land large enterprises, the deep pockets of the IT world, as customers. But as any company that sells to enterprises will tell you, it’s not easy to win such contracts. One critical mistake most younger startups make is believing that their tech will practically sell itself, says Arsalan Tavakoli-Shiraji, co-founder and senior vice president of field engineering at Databricks, which helps businesses store and analyze huge amounts of data. Yes. The tech must work well. But that’s not enough, he warns. Databricks today is an AI and big data storage giant, valued at $190 billion, with more than 70% of the Fortune 500 as customers. But it was once a tiny startup, and Tavakoli-Shiraji was responsible for helping bring in its first big deals. “Everybody when they’re selling to enterprises at startups … we think it’s all about the tech and having the best tech,” Tavakoli-Shiraji told attendees at TechCrunch Disrupt last week. But he learned the hard way that “people and processes matter the most.” He remembers securing a meeting with a prospect in the early days, only to discover that it was the same person who signed a mega-deal with his major competitor. “He has no interest in listening to me because he bet his career on that competitor,” Tavakoli-Shiraji said. He also remembers early deals fizzling because the startup wasn’t prepared for legal, contractual, or security questions. He offered his top tips for startups to win big customers: When Databricks was young, it tried and failed to score a meeting with UBS, the large Swiss bank. Then it did “a partnership with Microsoft,” becoming an Azure cloud partner. That meant sales agreements would be covered under Microsoft’s terms. But it also meant that Microsoft’s sales folks actually booked the meetings and advocated for the startup. “All of a sudden, that meeting wasn’t hard to get,” the founder recalled. “It was a huge boon to us that we were able to partner with somebody who had that access and distribution.” With Microsoft’s sales team helping, “we started getting pulled everywhere.” That created new problems, like prospects wanting features for countries Databricks hadn’t expanded to, but they were good problems to have. Today, Databricks is the big company that works with startups, selling their products under Databricks’ own terms and cybersecurity setup. Examples include Hightouch, which provides AI-powered marketing and customer-data tools (Databricks has also invested in it); Addepar, which offers data tools for investment professionals; and Tecton, a machine learning startup that Databricks later acquired. Tavakoli-Shiraji groups enterprises’ main concerns and objections into four categories he calls the four Cs: context, choice, cost, and control. Context means an AI that can be trained “like bringing on a new employee,” he says, learning the company’s terminology, processes, priorities, and so on. Choice is the freedom to switch AI models rather than be tied to a single provider. Cost is about controlling how much the AI product will cost to run. Control involves the safeguards that govern the AI’s behavior and protecting sensitive information. Tavakoli-Shiraji jokingly asks CIOs where they stand with AI on a scale of one to five. A one means employees merely have access to ChatGPT, and a five means the company has adopted new AI capabilities that have changed its business processes. “And everybody loves picking, ‘I’m three leaning towards four.’ But 99% of the ones I talk to are in bucket one and two.” Enterprises still haven’t figured out how to deploy AI agents in ways that create value beyond allowing engineering teams to write code. “It’s going to take many, many years before people can actually properly deploy AI in a very meaningful way in the enterprise.” One big opportunity for startups to bring that value is to modernize expensive legacy systems. “If you go to any bank today, they still have [an IBM] AS/400 and mainframes,” he described. “Nobody wanted to touch it because nobody knew what that code did.” But today, AI agents “can go in and understand and actually decompose what that code does.” That, he says, opens the door to products that do everything from adding modern interfaces for those ancient systems to replacing them altogether. Tavakoli-Shiraji also shared his predictions for the future of enterprise AI adoption, along with an anecdote of what happened when the company was young and he pretended to be a lawyer to land a big enterprise contract. Watch out, YouTube — Instagram goes from short-form to 90-minute videos
2026-10-26

Watch out, YouTube — Instagram goes from short-form to 90-minute videos

TechCrunch | 2026-10-26

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. If you could have 1 million followers on any social media platform, which would you choose? There is a correct answer, and it’s YouTube, assuming you’d like to get paid for your work. But Instagram’s latest update could upend YouTube’s long-held position as the most lucrative platform for creators, if only Instagram plays its cards right. Instagram announced on Monday that it’s testing a new type of video upload that will allow creators to share “Features,” videos up to 90 minutes long. After years in which every social platform (even LinkedIn!) aggressively pursued short-form video, this is a notable shift in strategy for Instagram. Short-form, vertical video content has defined the internet of the 2020s, but the creators behind these clips don’t actually make much money directly from platforms like Instagram — the Meta-owned platform has some monetization tools like subscriptions, but it doesn’t typically pay creators on a per-view basis. “You can get paid by the platform directly, you can get paid by a fan, and you can get paid by a brand,” Instagram head Adam Mosseri explained on the Colin and Samir Show, an interview show about the creator economy. “And right now, the way that it works on Instagram primarily is you primarily get paid by brands. Some people get paid by fans. We have still a small performance-based program, but … we spend more money than we make back by a long shot, and so it doesn’t really make sense to scale up because we’re just burning capital.” YouTube’s payment model is more straightforward: There are ads on videos, and creators enrolled in YouTube’s Partner Program get 55% of the ad revenue YouTube earns from their videos. That adds up. The platform said last year that it paid more than $100 billion to creators, artists, and media companies in the previous four years. Instagram and TikTok haven’t been able to replicate YouTube’s model with short-form content, which is inherently trickier to monetize than longer videos because there’s less room to place ads. Many creators see those accounts more like promotional arms for their YouTube channels than meaningful drivers of revenue. Instagram is encouraging creators to upload longer videos, as it already did eight years ago with the now-defunct IGTV. It’s possible that its parent company, Meta, which makes its billions on advertising, will put ads in those long-form videos, too. If Meta does add ads, could Instagram meaningfully compete with the YouTube Partner Program for the first time? “It’s too early to share a long-term monetization model, but we’re exploring ways for creators to earn from brands, fans, and Instagram, including performance-based incentives,” a Meta spokesperson told TechCrunch. “Over time, we think there will be compounding benefits for creators to tell new stories, reach new audiences, and find new partners, which should lead to many monetization opportunities.” It’s not a particularly promising answer, but it’s also not a no. Mosseri explained in a video announcement why the platform is suddenly embracing long-form content after scrapping its old video product, IGTV. “For a long time, I have been against doing long-form video on Instagram,” Mosseri said, noting that shorter content like Reels drives more shares and DMs among users. Instagram VP of Product Tessa Lyons, who has been advocating for this change, finally convinced Mosseri to give long form another shot. “Reels is still going to be a core part of Instagram. But when you want to go deeper with the creators you love and the content that they’re creating, you should be able to do that on Instagram,” Lyons said in the video. In other words, Instagram would rather keep fans on its own platform to enjoy both short- and long-form content from a creator than lose them to YouTube. Instagram is testing a feature called “Clips as Reels,” which lets creators make Reels out of their long-form content. The Reels link viewers to the longer video. Viewers can also save the longer video to send to the Instagram app on their TV. Instagram is currently testing “Features” and “Clips as Reels” in Brazil and will now expand to the U.S. in a small test. The platform is also working on improved discovery features for Live video and a hub on a creator’s profile for episodic Reels, meaning a series of connected Reels similar to TikTok’s playlist feature. “The thing that the team has been working on that we’re excited to share with you is trying to make it so that the short-form and the long-form video can be symbiotic,” Mosseri said. “We’re going to try to support these different types of formats, both in the mobile app, but also on the TV app, which I think is … going to take some time to get right. But I’m actually very excited about it, and we’re very committed to it long term.” When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. Polls Point to a Blue Wave. Here's Why Republicans Say They're Wrong
2026-10-27

Polls Point to a Blue Wave. Here's Why Republicans Say They're Wrong

TIME | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Former President Barack Obama, left, and Abdul El-Sayed, Democratic US Senate candidate from Michigan, appear together at a campaign event on Oct. 24, 2026 at Detroit. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Talk to Republicans around Washington and battleground states about the midterms and their talking points are all but in chorus: the polls are wrong and undercounting GOP voters. Talk to Democrats, and it’s giddy optimism mixed with an equal measure of trepidation, but for a whole other reason: all those predictions of a blue wave could give thousands of their voters permission to stay home. “We’ve all been burned,” says one former staffer on Democratic campaigns. The polls currently show Democrats may be on the verge of an historic blowout. President Donald Trump has the lowest approval ratings of his two terms in office and threatens to be a drag on most Republicans on the ballot. Democrats are in a strong position to snag a majority in the House and the Senate is still in play, but ideological fractures and a party brand that’s in the gutter stand to undermine their showing. Republicans, meanwhile, are on track to outspend Democrats in some pivotal races. A particularly messy election season could mean the polls are way off—or mostly on target. House speaker Mike Johnson, left, stumps for Rep. Gabe Evans, who is in a closely watched race against Democrat Manny Rutinel, on Oct. 22, 2026, in Longmont, Colo. Hyoung Chang—The Denver Post "I'm telling you, these polls are off. All of them. They use faulty analysis," said House Speaker Mike Johnson over the weekend, as he fights to save the House and his job. It’s been the drumbeat of the GOP talking-head circuit in recent weeks, as party leaders try to buck up a decidedly unenthusiastic base with talk of rigged surveys that overcount Democratic voters. That enthusiasm gap is quantifiable; about three-quarters of Democratic voters say voting this year is “extremely” important while roughly 6 in 10 Republicans say the same, according to Associated Press polling. And yet, there is some truth to the notion that pollsters have trouble with Republicans, who are more likely to ignore pollsters’ calls or even mislead them. Past pre-election surveys have repeatedly over-hyped Democrats’ chances of winning. In the last three presidential races, the election results underestimated Trump’s strength heading into Election Day. Republicans are emphasizing that trend, even though similar errors don’t usually occur in midterm election years. “Let’s ask Hillary Clinton how fake the fake polls are. We don’t pay those polls any mind,” rapper Nicki Minaj recently said aboard Air Force One in a video clip posted by a senior White House official. “Obviously, they are to deter our voters from going out and casting their vote.” More recently, some polls have been off, but not in a way that necessarily helps the Republicans’ argument. Last year’s polling in New Jersey’s and Virginia’s gubernatorial races actually underestimated Democrats’ chances; both went blue. Read more: Your Questions About Voting in the 2026 Midterm Elections, Answered No matter how loudly Republican talking heads say the polls are overcounting Democrats, that historically is just not the case in years when a presidential race is not on the ballot. The polls have been pretty accurate in recent midterm elections, and when they’ve been off, it hasn’t always been in Democrats’ favor. In 2022, Democrats narrowly outperformed expectations, leading to a Red Wave that proved smaller than Republicans had hoped. While that was portrayed as a big polling miss by some, in reality, the polls were largely on track, despite some regional exceptions. A big reason why polls are more accurate in midterm elections is because figuring out who is most likely to make it to the polls is less of a challenge for pollsters. In off-year, low-turnout elections, politics doesn't dominate the zeitgeist. “SNL isn’t opening with a skit on the Nebraska Senate race,” another Democratic strategist says. In other words, it’s easier to pinpoint those voters who are dialed into an election when the White House isn’t in play. After all, many people can’t even name their member of Congress, let alone have a strong opinion on them. As much as Trump keeps saying he’s on the ballot this fall, he objectively is not. While Republicans insist polls are underestimating their support, their allocation of resources suggests they believe what the polls in key battlegrounds are telling them. For instance, as polls showed Democrat Roy Cooper well ahead of Republican Michael Whatley in North Carolina’s Senate race, Republicans largely pulled their funding, a clear sign they believed that Cooper’s victory is all but assured. But the current environment remains unsettled at best. How else to explain that red states like Texas, Iowa, and even Kansas may actually elect a Democrat to the U.S. Senate? Even so, Republicans are dismissing polling trends in many battlegrounds, and instead citing selected polls that show them still in contention or at least within the margin of sampling error. Nationally, the generic ballot certainly favors Democrats and Trump is undeniably a drag on Republicans who have to stand with their party’s leader or face his wrath. Some Republican nominees have turned out to be lackluster and underperforming national party leaders’ expectations. Others, such as Vivek Ramaswamy in Ohio’s race for Governor, have stepped in it and seem unable to clean their shoes. (So badly did a Ramaswamy attack ad backfire, the non-partisan Cook Political Report last week moved that race from a “Toss Up” to “Lean Democrat.”) We’re on the verge of taking back control of the House,” Democratic Leader Hakeem Jeffries said over the weekend. But first, Democrats understand, they have to show up and not take the polling as permission to stay home on Election Day. That’s not to say Democrats are fielding an immaculate set of candidates. A misfire in Maine’s Senate race forced the party to swap candidates after the nomination had seemingly been settled. National Democrats didn’t get their favored candidate in Michigan’s Senate race either. The polling in both states shows tighter contests than Democrats had hoped for. Still, there’s no bigger megaphone in politics right now than Trump, and he is barnstorming the map trying to turn out his people, the folks who are widely understood to eschew pollsters’ calls. While Trump appears to be a drag on the national ticket, he still is popular with his base. A CNBC poll released Monday shows the President with an 82% approval rating among Republicans and at 90% among those who identify with the MAGA movement. Those are numbers that has Republicans praying at least that poll is correct.

ICE Does Not Belong at the Polls
2026-10-27

ICE Does Not Belong at the Polls

TIME | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE) agents on March 24, 2026 in New York, New York. During the presidential election of 1863, at the height of the Civil War, federal troops reportedly descended on polling places in Delaware, Kentucky, Maryland, and Missouri and prevented people they deemed disloyal from voting. This prompted a fierce national debate that culminated in Congress passing an 1865 law barring federal “troops or armed men” from “any place” where an election is being held. The central goal of this law, according to its proponents, was “to prevent the President and his officers from interfering in elections,” which are run by the states. Keeping armed federal officers away from the polls, they argued, was necessary to preserve democracy, even in the midst of civil war. This has been the clear law, and the respected policy of every presidential administration, for over a century. Ahead of the midterms, top Trump Administration officials have publicly refused to rule out the possibility of sending federal agents to the polls. In September, Department of Homeland Security Secretary Markwayne Mullin claimed that immigration officers could be sent to polling places to “serve a warrant” as part of immigration enforcement operations or if “there is a threat” to those polling places, adding “we’ll be where we need to be.” “ICE isn’t there to patrol the polling places,” hedged Mullin. “We’re there to enforce specific threats.” Federal Bureau of Investigation Director Kash Patel similarly said, “If there’s a reason to go there because there’s been a violation, we will, otherwise we won’t.” It is clear to us, and to legal experts, that barring extreme circumstances, sending ICE or FBI agents to the polls would be flatly illegal under the 1865 law, and several others. As Daniel Caine, Chair of the Joint Chiefs of Staff, pointedly said this August, any request to send military troops to election sites would be an “unlawful order.” The same holds true for any other armed federal agents. The only exception to the prohibition is to “repel armed enemies of the United States.” The Congress of 1865 originally included a second exception, allowing armed federal agents to “keep the peace at the polls.” But Congress repealed that exception in 1909 because lawmakers feared that federal authorities could point to any kind of disturbance as a pretext to deploy federal forces. In other words, unless a hostile foreign power attacks voting sites using weapons, it is a crime for any armed federal agents to go to those sites, even to quell a disruption. And they are certainly not allowed to show up for routine policing or immigration actions. If sending ICE to the polls is so plainly illegal, then why do top administration officials keep refusing to rule it out? Context is important. The federal government has been conducting indiscriminate and often violent immigration enforcement actions in communities across the country. At the same time, the administration has been peddling conspiracy theories about widespread illegal voting, often targeting communities of color. The administration has redirected thousands of homeland security agents away from critical transnational security threats to hunt for elusive noncitizen voters. These efforts are not uncovering any significant problems. Federal and state law already bar noncitizens from voting in federal and state elections, and our election system does a terrific job of ensuring that only eligible citizens vote, with vanishingly rare exceptions—as dozens of studies, election reviews, and painstaking investigations have already confirmed. This intimidation campaign is taking a toll. According to a University of California, San Diego poll conducted last month, a staggering 40% of voting-age American citizens expect ICE agents to be at the polls this November. Black, Hispanic, and Asian American voters are three to four times more likely than white voters to worry that they will be questioned by ICE at polls or that members of their racial or ethnic groups will face intimidation at the polls. This fear is real. But despite all the administration’s threats, voters should not be intimidated. The law protecting election sites from federal forces is ironclad. Anyone who violates that would be committing a federal crime. Federal and state laws also prohibit any effort to intimidate voters, wherever it occurs. A broad array of civil rights groups and labor unions have already filed lawsuits to hold the federal government accountable for its intimidation efforts—including The Brennan Center, where we work. Other lawyers across the country are poised to run to court to protect elections if federal agents do unlawfully show up to polls. Outside court, election officials, state government officials, law enforcement, and voter protection organizations are prepared to respond to any instance of voter intimidation. That includes de-escalation and backup plans, including changing voting sites if needed, to ensure that all Americans can continue to vote safely. Voters are determined and undeterred, as millions so far have already cast their ballots by voting early and by mail. Our advice to concerned voters: make a plan to vote as early as you can. Voters have multiple options to vote with confidence. In addition to voting at the polls on election day, they can vote early in person or vote by mail ballot, dropping it off in secure ballot drop boxes, at an election office, or by mail. You can also go vote with a trusted group of loved ones or neighbors. It is an outrage that the federal government has refused to rule out the idea of federal agents at the polls. In our view, it is part of a campaign to intimidate voters and undermine our elections. The only way it will succeed is if voters don’t show up. Remote Work Was Supposed to Make Parenting Easier. Why Are Parents Still Struggling?
2026-10-27

Remote Work Was Supposed to Make Parenting Easier. Why Are Parents Still Struggling?

TIME | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings A mother works from home while her toddler son plays and her daughter works on a laptop Hastings on Hudson, N.Y. on Sept. 8, 2020. Recently, one of us learned that her five-year-old was forgotten at school. The teenage neighbor who usually walks him home got distracted and forgot to collect him. Luckily, it was easy enough while working from home to dash out, stop at school, and then return to the laptop as he constructed a “dino-chicken-car” with some Legos. The crisis became a non-issue thanks to remote work. Juggling parenthood and work is tough, and it shows up in our stress levels. The American Psychological Association reports that 48% of parents say that on most days their stress is completely overwhelming—around twice as many as other adults. Remote work seems to offer a solution. Parents can put dinner in the oven between meetings so that food is ready before kids’ hangry tantrums arrive. A youngster with a virus can sleep through it while Dad takes calls over Zoom, so what would have been a sick day turns into a mostly-productive day. The time-bind that made it feel impossible to show up for one’s kids and for one’s career can seem less, well, binding. But, weirdly, the rise of remote work hasn’t necessarily improved parents’ mental health, and may have even harmed it. In research published in Science, we considered the mental distress of over half a million Americans, measured by taking medication for depression or anxiety, seeing a mental health professional, and other factors. We compared people in jobs that could be done from home, like software engineers and customer service representatives, to those whose jobs necessitate in-person work, like mechanical engineers and cashiers. If remote work were a panacea for parents, we would expect the mental health of parents in remote-capable jobs to improve a lot with the rise of remote work. But we didn’t see that result. Instead, parents in remote-capable jobs saw bigger increases in reported depression and antidepressant use than those working in-person jobs. How can remote work, something that so many parents feel eases their days, be potentially harmful to their mental health? Two factors contribute to this phenomenon. First, remote work blurs boundaries. When people work remotely, they are much more likely to work at odd hours, answering emails in the wee hours of the morning and returning to tinker with presentation slides late at night. It’s as though once work could be completed at home, any home-time became fair game for work-time. Being always on for work can make the time-bind pinch in new ways. Parents can struggle to feel fully present for either their kids or their work. Multi-tasking isn’t limited to odd hours outside the workday. Recent research documents that parents who work at companies that allow remote work tend to use less paid childcare. Using less paid childcare can be great for parents’ pocketbooks and their ability to see their kids more frequently. But it also increases stress if it means that parents are trying to work while also encouraging their toddler to take a nap or their middle schooler to do their homework. A recent Pew Research Center survey of 2,242 working parents found that doing work-related tasks while spending time with kids was more common among those who worked remotely than those who rarely or never did. But these interruptions undermine our ability to do work, and research has traced these family interruptions to lower wages among mothers working while doing “passive” childcare. Multitasking is painful when it feels as though we’re failing our colleagues and our kids. Something has to give. The second flaw with remote work is that it deepens isolation that parents already experience all too frequently. Parenting can be lonely. Much of the time that Americans spend with their families is quite nuclear, just with kids and perhaps a partner, but without many other adults around. Remote work is also lonely. In our study, we found that the rise in remote work in the wake of the pandemic increased time spent alone by an hour a day for people in remote-capable jobs. The combination of both working and parenting outside a communal context can translate into a shortage of adult connection. Parents toggle between kids demanding attention and work demanding attention. The small interactions with peers and colleagues that nourish us disappear. And that ends up draining us in the long run. As a result, remote work is not yet shoring up parents’ well-being. Yet carefully constructed remote work can be a boon for parents. Several strategies would help. First, we must re-establish boundaries. It needs to be socially acceptable to truly sign off from work. This is unlikely to stick unless leaders and managers consistently encourage and enforce it. The desire to prove commitment will be too much of a lure for many to fully disengage without explicit support from higher-ups. Instead, managers need to ensure that those who were offline during a conversation still have an opportunity to contribute. By looping them in, leaders can make it okay to be fully present with work, and at other times, with family. Second, we must structure in-person work so that it includes parents. When parents must pick up their kids from school daily, a hybrid schedule with full days in the office and full days remote may not be especially helpful. Instead, a shorter in-person workday, where you can sign off at 3 or 4 p.m., do pickup, dinner, and bedtime, and then sign back on might work better. In-person work doesn't need to happen every moment of every day, but scheduling it during school hours can help include parents more fully. For dispersed teams, off-site gatherings can be a valuable way to form connections. But to be inclusive, these meetings should be scheduled far in advance, since parents most likely need to secure additional support at home. Finally, orienting doses of in-person work around connection can help alleviate loneliness. Make time for a social lunch with coworkers, even if it’s a brief one. For in-person meetings, book a meeting room rather than defaulting to the convenience of Zoom. Advocate for a team off-site gathering. A recent experiment shows that when remote workers at a call center came together in-person just once a month, they felt more connected to one another and were also more productive given what they learned from one another. Remote work holds the potential to improve the lives of working parents. But to do this, remote work cannot just devolve into multitasking work and parenting. And it ought not deepen the isolation of modern parenthood. Instead, we need remote work with boundaries and human-to-human connection with colleagues—including colleagues who might happen to be parents. Justice Department Files More Charges Against James Comey
2026-10-27

Justice Department Files More Charges Against James Comey

TIME | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Former FBI Director James Comey appears on NBC's "Meet the Press" in Washington, D.C., on May 17, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings The Justice Department has brought charges against former FBI Director James Comey for the third time in 13 months, this time related to alleged leaks of national security information to the media. According to court documents filed earlier this month and unsealed on Monday, federal prosecutors are accusing Comey of “willfully” mishandling information related to national defense in October 2016, resulting in the information appearing in media reports. “Having reason to believe such information could be used to injure the United States and to the advantage of any foreign nation,” the court document said Comey “knowingly and willfully communicated, delivered, transmitted, and caused that information to be communicated, delivered, and transmitted to a person not entitled to receive it.” The inquiry is the latest attempt to prosecute Comey, who is a prominent critic of President Donald Trump. Trump fired Comey in 2017 while Comey was leading the FBI investigation into potential ties between Trump’s 2016 presidential campaign and Russia. The first Trump Administration cited a memo related to Comey’s handling of the FBI investigation into Democratic presidential candidate Hillary Clinton’s emails. Since then, Trump has publicly called for criminal investigations into Comey and celebrated the indictments he’s faced. In a video posted on Monday, Comey said the latest investigation was an attempt to politically persecute him. “They’re back, again,” Comey said. “It would almost be funny if it weren’t such an appalling abuse of the Department of Justice, one that we cannot become numb to.” “I’m still innocent. I’m still not afraid of these people,” he added. Comey’s attorneys argued in court filings that the alleged leaks had already been investigated twice during the first Trump Administration in 2017 and 2019, and neither had resulted in charges. The Justice Department has pursued a number of investigations and indictments against prominent Democrats in recent weeks, appearing to break with the unofficial “unwritten 60-day rule” of avoiding filing new charges or taking public investigatory action that could influence an approaching election. Earlier this month, the Department secured a search warrant for the home of Rep. Ilhan Omar (D, Minn.), who called the effort “the latest escalation in [Trump’s] campaign of political intimidation.” The DOJ also secured an indictment against Virginia Senate Leader Louise Lucas, who led the Democrats’ counter-effort against Republican gerrymandering, on drug trafficking and money laundering charges. Lucas denied the allegations and said she would fight back against an attempt by “people in power … to silence those who stand up to them.” According to court documents, prosecutors allege that around Oct. 4 and 5, 2016, Comey directed FBI staff to speak with the New York Times “about specific accuracy concerns” related to “misinformation which had appeared” in a Reuters report about Yahoo’s cooperation with U.S. surveillance. In doing so, prosecutors contend, Comey “illegally transferred classified information.” The Times article had described how Yahoo had adapted its existing email filtering system, used to detect spam, malware, and child sexual abuse material, to comply with a secret surveillance order to search messages for a computer “signature” associated with communications by a foreign, state-sponsored terrorist organization. The article said the Justice Department had obtained the order from a Foreign Intelligence Surveillance Court judge in 2015 and that Yahoo was barred from disclosing it. A spokesperson for the Times said the newspaper “does not comment on the sourcing behind our reporting, but we know from experience that prosecutions like this one are designed to discourage sources from providing journalists with the kind of valuable and illuminating information that the public needs to know.” Comey’s lawyers argued in a motion to dismiss the case that the Trump Administration did not file an indictment within the 10-year statute of limitations. On Oct. 5, prosecutors filed the charges in a criminal information under seal in Federal District Court in Washington, which does not require approval from a grand jury or a judicial finding of probable cause, but have not obtained an indictment. The Justice Department has brought multiple charges against Comey during Trump’s second term. It secured an indictment against him in September 2025 over allegations that he made false statements to Congress in 2020 related to alleged leaks to the media. The charges were thrown out by a federal judge in November, concluding that the prosecutor, Lindsey Halligan, had been unlawfully appointed. Comey was also indicted in April over allegedly making a threat against Trump by posting on Instagram a photograph of seashells on a North Carolina beach arranged to spell “86 47.” Prosecutors argued that the arrangement was intended as a threat “to kill” Trump, who is the 47th President. Comey, who deleted the post after initial backlash, said he did not intend the photograph to be a threat against Trump, and that he understood the numbers to be a “political message.” The numbers “86” have commonly been used to mean “to throw out” or “to refuse service to,” according to Merriam-Webster, but have also been used in some instances to mean “to kill.” That case is set to go to trial in North Carolina in February 2027. Earlier on Monday, a federal judge in New York ruled that the Justice Department had unlawfully fired Comey’s daughter, Maurene Comey, in July 2025. The judge ordered her retroactive reinstatement with back pay, concluding that the Trump Administration had violated the Constitution and federal law by claiming the President had executive authority to fire civil servants.
