Update your preferences in Account Settings Brazil’s Supreme Court has ordered police to confiscate the passports of Flávio Bolsonaro as part of a criminal investigation related to a money laundering scheme. Supreme Court Justice Alexandre de Moraes issued the ruling, which became public on Monday, a day after Bolsonaro, the son of jailed former President Jair Bolsonaro, lost the presidential election to incumbent Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva. Bolsonaro is being investigated for alleged links to a Brazilian lawyer, Willer Tomaz, who is accused of laundering more than $1 billion through a fraud scheme, the New York Times reported. Bolsonaro, who has cast the investigation as political persecution, said in a social media video on Monday afternoon that the seizure of his travel documents was an attempt at intimidating him after he lost the election. The action could inflame political tensions amid claims from Brazil’s right wing and abroad that the election was unfair. Bolsonaro initially said he would seek to annul the vote, although his Liberal Party later conceded defeat. In the video posted Monday, the right-wing senator said he would now seek to have Lula’s ticket disqualified, citing alleged disinformation and “vote buying.” He also said he would organize the opposition in Congress to Lula’s government. “I wasn’t the one who lost,” he said. “The system won.” Donald Trump, an ally of the Bolsonaros, said on Monday that the election appeared “rigged.” The U.S. President did not provide evidence for his claim. “I didn’t take ​sides, but it looked like another rigged election,” Trump said. “I just think it had a very bad look, the election, the ​way it was tabulated,” he added. “The extension given was very strange.” Brazil’s Supreme Court had granted a two-hour extension to voting on Sunday, citing transport issues raised by Lula’s campaign. Bolsonaro’s campaign had separately requested a two-hour extension due to heavy rainfall in certain areas. Lula won the election by more than 4 million votes. Roughly 0.03% of the total votes—or less than 35,000 votes—were recorded in the extra two hours. Vice President Geraldo Alckmin told Rede Vanguarda that Trump’s claims were “completely wrong.” Read More: Why the Trump Administration Is Being Accused of Interfering in Brazil’s Election Bolsonaro is also being investigated over alleged links to a disgraced banker connected to a corruption scheme. Bolsonaro allegedly raised millions of dollars from the banker to fund a biopic about his father. Here’s what to know about the investigation. The inquiry into Bolsonaro stems from a federal probe into unauthorized deductions from pension payments made by Brazil’s social security agency. The broader operation, launched in April 2025, investigated associations suspected of collecting unauthorized membership fees from pension benefits. Evidence from the probe led investigators to examine suspected money laundering involving betting platforms and financial transactions linked to Tomaz, who denies wrongdoing. Police reportedly discovered that Bolsonaro had “master user” access to bank accounts belonging to Alcazar Holdings, a company owned by Tomaz. In May 2025, a person working for Tomaz asked the bank to remove Bolsonaro’s access to the accounts, local media outlets BPMoney and Times Brasil reported earlier this month, citing police findings. Both Bolsonaro and Tomaz deny having a joint account or financial ties. The order required Bolsonaro to surrender his passports and barred him from contacting Tomaz. Bolsonaro said he would comply with the order and that he had “never considered” leaving Brazil. He said that the ruling came after his brother, Eduardo Bolsonaro, had said on a podcast last week that he should leave the country if he lost the election. Bolsonaro has repeatedly criticized Brazil’s judiciary and described the investigations into him as politically motivated. The country’s Supreme Court generally assigns cases through a lottery system, and the money laundering investigation was assigned to de Moraes on Oct. 16. De Moraes was the lead justice in last year’s trial of Jair Bolsonaro, who refused to accept his election loss in 2022. A five-member panel of Supreme Court justices convicted Jair Bolsonaro, by a 4-1 vote, of attempting to overturn his election defeat and sentenced him to 27 years and three months in prison, which he is serving under house arrest due to poor health. Prosecutors alleged that the plot included plans to assassinate Lula. Jair Bolsonaro denies wrongdoing. In June, a Supreme Court panel unanimously convicted Eduardo Bolsonaro of coercion in judicial proceedings. Prosecutors alleged he had attempted to lobby the Trump Administration to use tariffs and sanctions, including on de Moraes, to pressure judicial authorities to drop its prosecution of Jair Bolsonaro. He was sentenced to four years and two months in prison, but remains in Texas, where has lived since 2025. De Moraes has also faced scrutiny over reported ties to Daniel Vorcaro, the former owner of Banco Master, whose financing of a film about Jair Bolsonaro is the subject of a separate inquiry into Bolsonaro. The New York Times reported, citing a police dossier, that Vorcaro sent text messages to de Moraes asking for help to avoid arrest. In messages to other associates, Vorcaro reportedly mentioned paying millions to the law firm of de Moraes’s wife. De Moraes has denied wrongdoing and said he never acted to favor Vorcaro or Banco Master. De Moraes also clashed with tech billionaire Elon Musk in 2024 over court orders to block accounts on Musk’s social media platform X. De Moraes temporarily suspended the platform in Brazil after it failed to comply with judicial requirements, which Musk had described as “the most draconian demands of any country on Earth.”

Israel Heads to the Polls as Netanyahu Battles to Stay in Power
2026-10-27

Israel Heads to the Polls as Netanyahu Battles to Stay in Power

TIME | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Israel's Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu casts his ballot, alongside his wife Sara, at a polling station in Jerusalem on Oct. 27, 2026. Israelis headed to polling stations Tuesday to decide the political fate of Benjamin Netanyahu, the country’s longest-serving Prime Minister who faces one of the most consequential tests of his decadeslong political career. Netanyahu was joined by his wife Sara as he cast his ballot just after midday, local time, at a polling station in a Jerusalem school. “This is a very moving and fateful moment. My wife and I are truly overwhelmed,” Netanyahu told reporters after voting. “We know what is at stake, and since I am not allowed to say things explicitly, I will say: Vote correctly.” From 7 a.m. to 10 p.m. local time (1 a.m. to 4 p.m. EDT), Israelis can cast votes for representatives in the Knesset, in the first election in the country since the Oct. 7, 2023 terrorist attacks by Palestinian militant group Hamas and the subsequent Israeli war in the Gaza Strip. Since then, Israel under Netanyahu has pursued an aggressive military campaign across the Middle East—going to war with Iran, backing settler attacks and displacement in the West Bank, and carrying out strikes against militant groups in Lebanon and Yemen. Tuesday’s election is widely seen as a referendum on Netanyahu’s leadership and Israelis’ willingness to change course amid uncertainty. According to polls, the outcome is far from certain, with a tight race expected as Israel decides upon its future. “I hope this will be a true holiday for democracy,” Isaac Herzog, the Israeli head of state, told reporters from a polling station in Rehavia, where he and his wife voted. “No one can be allowed to spoil or undermine it.” Two main political blocs are contending to form Israel’s next government. The first is the pro-Netanyahu coalition led by Netanyahu’s Likud party. The coalition includes far-right and ultra-Orthodox parties, such as Otzma Yehudit (Jewish Power) led by national security minister Itamar Ben-Gvir and Religious Zionism-Zehut led by finance minister Bezalel Smotrich. Their ultranationalist policies have found an audience in younger right-wing Israelis. On the other side are the Jewish-led opposition movements—with former Israel Defense Forces chief of staff Gadi Eisenkot and centrist Yashar (Straight) at the forefront. Eisenkot, who entered politics in 2022, joined Netanyahu’s emergency war cabinet after the Oct. 7 attack on Israel. But after having lost a son in combat in Israel’s wars, Eisenkot left his post in 2024. Two of his nephews also died fighting in Gaza, a prominent campaign talking point. The opposition also includes a joint ticket led by former Prime Minister Naftali Bennett and Knesset opposition leader Yair Lapid, the secular nationalist party Yisrael Beiteinu (Israel Our Home) led by former finance minister Avigdor Lieberman, and the Democrats led by former IDF deputy chief of staff Yair Golan. Some polls suggest that Eisenkot’s Yashar party may garner slightly more support than Netanyahu’s Likud, but they also project that neither the Prime Minister’s right-wing bloc nor the opposition may win the 61 Knesset seats needed to form a government. Including Arab parties such as United Arab List (Ra’am) and the Joint List, or fringe parties, could be necessary to form a government. Both pro-Netanyahu and anti-Netanyahu coalitions have so far ruled out teaming up with the Arab-led parties. Arab citizens of Israel make up about 20% of the population, though they receive fewer government services and endure higher poverty and unemployment rates. Discrimination and disappointment have historically depressed Arab-Israeli voter turnout. A cyclist rides beneath a pedestrian bridge displaying electronic election billboards showing former IDF chief of staff Gadi Eisenkot (L) and Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu (R) in Tel Aviv, Israel, on Oct. 23, 2026. Erik Marmor—picture alliance/Getty Images Netanyahu hopes to maintain his premiership with the help of his longstanding ally, U.S. President Donald Trump, who only announced his endorsement days before the election as conflict in the Middle East has tested their friendship. In a late-night TV appearance on Oct. 25, Netanyahu boasted of Trump’s endorsement and said that Trump was willing to take action “that would finally, finally, remove the Iranian threat” so long as Netanyahu stayed in power. Netanyahu added that he has already instructed Israel’s national intelligence agency Mossad as well as the IDF to “bring down” the Iranian regime. Trump remains very popular in Israel. By contrast, U.S. Vice President J.D. Vance declined to endorse Netanyahu for re-election, just a day after Trump put his “complete and total” support behind the Israeli leader. “The President has the most effective endorsement in American, or international, political history,” said Vance, when asked in an interview if he would also be extending an endorsement of his own. “I endorse American candidates and that’s where I'm focused on, is the midterms, so I'm not getting involved in the Israeli elections.” When pushed for an answer, Vance added: “I'm not anti-endorsing [Netanyahu], I'm not pro-endorsing him.” At stake for Netanyahu could be more than his continued leadership. The premier faces an international arrest warrant for alleged crimes related to the Israeli military campaign in Gaza. And even domestically, critics have demanded an inquiry into Netanyahu’s decision-making around Oct. 7. The attack, which resulted in over 1,200 people in Israel killed and around 250 hostages taken, is considered by many to be indicative of a major security failure. The Gaza health ministry reports that since Oct. 7, 2023, more than 74,000 individuals have been killed. In the absence of independent monitoring on the ground, Gaza’s health ministry is the primary source for casualty data relied upon by humanitarian groups, journalists, and international bodies. Its figures do not differentiate between civilians and combatants and cannot be independently verified by TIME. Another issue driving the election is public fury over ultra-Orthodox communities’ exemption from military conscription for men engaged in religious studies. Despite a shortage of soldiers, less than 10% of eligible ultra-Orthodox men enlist, amid demands from Netanyahu’s allies to exempt them. Eisenkot, Netanyahu’s main rival, has promised to end the de facto exemptions. Netanyahu’s repeated attempts to reform the judiciary also factor into this election, with critics slamming his undermining of democratic institutions and connecting it to his legal interests. Netanyahu is in the middle of a yearslong corruption trial, which he and his allies—including Trump—have claimed is politically motivated. He sought but didn’t receive a presidential pardon from Herzog. About 7.3 million Israeli citizens are eligible to vote on Tuesday. The election fills the Knesset’s 120 seats through proportional representation—which means parties, not personalities, are on these voters’ ballots. After polling stations close at 10 p.m. (4 p.m. EDT), local media are expected to broadcast exit polls. The final vote tally is expected by Oct. 30, with official results published on Nov. 4. But getting a clear picture of the Israeli government is likely to take longer. No single party has ever claimed 61 seats to form a government, meaning Israel has always been governed by coalitions. Such alliances can take weeks or months to form. What those coalitions may look like will depend on the election outcomes for smaller parties, which each need 3.25% of the votes to secure a seat in the Knesset. Once the election winners are determined, President Herzog ⁠will refer to party leaders to choose a preferred Prime Minister and will ask the strongest candidate to form a government within 42 days. If a coalition cannot be formed after multiple attempts, the current Likud-led coalition government will continue to serve as caretaker before another round of elections is held in the coming months.

The Democrats Need a Daddy. I Know Exactly Who It Should Be.
2026-10-27

The Democrats Need a Daddy. I Know Exactly Who It Should Be.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. Finally, a Democrat who actually knows how to have sex. Earlier this month, the New York Post reported that Chris Gallant, the former military pilot and union leader running for Congress in Long Island, New York, was photographed in fetish gear almost 20 years ago. This is the typical kind of sex scandal that would ordinarily trip up a candidate, maybe even for good. But Gallant—who is gay, and already out—hasn’t been significantly impacted by the would-be controversy. The photos were taken consensually, when Gallant was a private citizen, but what’s protecting him more than anything is that he looks good. Ripped and half-hard, there isn’t anything to be embarrassed about here. He’s hot and gay and lightly horny. What exactly is the problem? The dearth of a scandal around Gallant—running as a Democrat in decidedly red Long Island, where he’s still trailing behind his Republican opponent—must be a relief of sorts for the party. Ever since Barack Obama left office, Democrats have been looking for someone with his charm, smarts, and, yes, his good looks. There have been, it seems, a thousand flashes in the pan: Beto O’Rourke’s rolled-up shirtsleeves, Anthony Weiner’s city-kid mouthiness, Graham Platner’s stupid little butch mustache. So far, no one has stuck: Guys like O’Rourke failed to connect with voters in any meaningful way, and others, like Platner, dropped off the face of the earth after being accused of sexual assault. (Don’t get me started on Weiner.) What the Democratic Party—and what Democrats of all ilks—considers attractive these days has changed considerably in the Trump years. There are fewer and fewer intellectual stalwarts like Obama or Pete Buttigieg, and not that many impish party boys/accused harassers like Weiner or Bill Clinton. There’s certainly no one on the national stage who inspires the same kind of insipid loyalty that Donald Trump does. For the last few electoral cycles, it somehow seemed that the Republicans were dominating when it came to hotness, encapsulated through Pete Hegseth struggling with a pair of dumbbells. Republicans owned boobs and lingerie and fucking raw, and while RFK Jr. looks like a raw chicken left in the sun, guys like him have an undeniable confidence that still gets political attention. The Democratic Party was virginized, a group of scolds lecturing everyone about safe sex and the benefits of chaste friendship. The party has long struggled to make sense of what it, and its voters, wants. In the forthcoming midterms, and going into the all-important 2028 election, the Democrats understand that finding a hot candidate is important, namely a straight man who can appeal to both female and male voters through a mix of aesthetics and policy. Take a gander at the TikTok accounts of people like Manny Rutinel and James Talarico, and you’ll find plenty of self-objectifying videos, fan-cams of rolled-up shirtsleeves, compilation clips of dudes being dudes (lifting weights and being mean to Hegseth). But they’re still not nailing it (get it?). It’s like a cursed episode of The Dating Game, with three versions of the Sexy Democrat as contestants. First, a representation of Woke 2 at its worst: Gavin Newsom, governor of California, a presidential prospect for 2028. Lately, he’s been going after the Republicans using millennial and Gen Z internet-speak, but there’s a clear dissonance between his old playbook and new slang. Next: Jon Ossoff, the latest Great White Hope of the Democratic Party. Running for Senate in Georgia, Ossoff is now synonymous with (ugh) clapbacks against Natalie Harp and being a saucy little bitch at confirmation hearings. What Ossoff offers is a pliable blank canvas in the shape of an attractive white man, not vocal enough to be hateable but forceful enough to pin your hopes for the next five generations on this one guy (ask John Kerry how that goes). Finally, one of the newest subjects of political thirst: Abdul El-Sayed, candidate for Michigan Senate, and easily the hottest man the Democrats have offered us in the better part of a decade. He is, objectively, good-looking, and yes, anyone who disagrees is a racist! He’s a doctor, a beefcake, and he’s always walking around in a black V-neck, which is sluttier than anything Shane Gillis complains about on national television. But above all, El-Sayed is a bulldog, happy to bite back in a way we rarely see from any candidate, never mind a Muslim one. As the midterms inch closer—and as we enter another two years of presidential primaries—the Democrats know they need to find a legitimately hot candidate. But it’s not just about finding a good-looking candidate, or a charming candidate, or even a personable candidate. After a decade-plus of living with an abusive father, there’s nothing we need more than a daddy. Gavin Newsom looks like the rat that Lisa Simpson accidentally shears thinking he’s a baby sheep. He is an inherent and visual slimeball: greased-back hair, raspy voice, more teeth than you ever thought someone could have. When his social media accounts started to mock Trump openly and brazenly, it felt like only Newsom—Trump’s spiritual opposite, loose proof of the horseshoe theory—could pull this off. “I, THE PEACETIME GOVERNOR—OUR NATION’S FAVORITE—WILL SAVE AMERICA ONCE AGAIN,” he tweeted in August 2025. “MANY ARE NOW CALLING ME GAVIN CHRISTOPHER ‘COLUMBUS’ NEWSOM (BECAUSE OF THE MAPS!). THANK YOU FOR YOUR ATTENTION TO THIS MATTER. —GCN.” Looking a little like a forgotten McConaughey cousin? Check. Liberal enough to be considered a part of the resistance against the current administration? Sure. Mean to the president? Yeah, that’s the stuff. Newsom never seems more attractive than he does when he’s being a bully. Why else have a 6-foot-whatever nearly 60-year-old guy from the Bay Area in your corner? Newsom has built his iconography around an image of a young man following in his father’s footsteps while also being raised by a long-suffering single mother and overcoming his dyslexia. A more interesting version of Newsom, as both person and candidate, is the guy jeering at the president. What makes Trump attractive to those on the right—at least in spirit, if not his actual looks—is that he’s virtually untouched by consequences. He moves through the world and the meatgrinder of American politics without ever having to account for anything he’s said and done. A man who does whatever the fuck he wants and somehow seems to have fun doing it? Newsom has that down too. Newsom also has an unbearable smarm about him. He has the conventions of attractiveness, from his height to his pearly whites to the almost strategic amount of smile lines he has, but add them up and you somehow don’t leave with much. His political platform remains staunchly centrist, and the image isn’t enough to override the lack of message. You can’t really get someone like Jon Ossoff without Newsom. Less smarmy, less orange, less bitchy, Ossoff recently went viral again for his debate against Senate hopeful Mike Collins after he said Ossoff was a part of the “Epstein class.” In response, Ossoff did what he does, which is look appropriately smug in a tight little suit with a jawline you want to run your tongue along. “Mike, who on your team advised you to raise the Epstein files?” he asked. “You voted to cover up the evidence, Mike.” Somehow, there’s something hot about two casual deployments of “Mike.” Later at a campaign stop, he went after Collins and the Republicans some more. “Mike Collins is a known bigot under federal investigation for the illegal misuse of tax dollars, widely known for his poor character and low integrity, and unfit for any elected office in the United States,” he said. “And now the bigot wants a promotion.” All this while wearing a blue button-up, sleeves rolled up to expose a forearm that you might want to hang from by your teeth, like a dog with a bone. What a slut. What Ossoff and Newsom might not have in effortlessness, they make up for in online clapbacks and debate performances. These are candidates who have decidedly refused Michelle Obama’s earlier edict that we go high when Trump and his ilk go low. Their hotness quotient isn’t just about how they look, but how willing they are to scrap with political candidates who otherwise refuse to play by any rules. If Trump can’t write a tweet like a normal person, then Newsom won’t either. “I don’t think Ossoff is a good-looking man,” said Trump at a rally for Collins last week, suggesting that he probably really, really does. “I actually think Mike Collins is a good-looking man. He’s all man; I’m not sure about Ossoff.” It’s a lot of talk about who is “all man” from a guy whose hands have been melting for the better part of a year. Ossoff and Newsom get under Republicans’ skin in large part because they’re conventionally attractive men who are also adeptly dogging their competition. We find these guys hot because they trigger the same feelings of being attracted to a bully football player, or even the quiet but surprisingly cute nerd who stood up for you in Intro to Calc. It’s hardwired before we’re old enough to vote, a search for a charming protector. Daddies come in many forms, but in this type of politics, a daddy is someone willing to fight for you, even if it’s ugly and even if it’s petty. Where it begins to fall apart is when you start to ask for more than just viral clapbacks. It’s a little like going out with a good-looking guy whose Hinge profile said he loves deep conversation, but he spends the date distracted by his Kalshi bets. Neither Newsom nor Ossoff has much more to offer voters beyond the obvious. Ossoff is happy to fight with Trump’s lackeys, but he’s still a run-of-the mill Democrat. He’s remained somewhat supportive of Israel and is farther to the right than his contemporaries on Medicare for All. Newsom is an obvious antagonist for the Republicans, but it’s hard to forget that he used to roll around on a rug with at least one very famous (and recently disgraced) conservative. These guys are hot insofar as politicians go, but real hotness comes from taking risks. Maybe during these midterms, the Democrats are learning how much reward can come from letting a hot guy run his delicious little mouth. Abdul El-Sayed is kind of an asshole, but what can I say: The ladies love a bad boy!!! Forty-one years old, 5-foot-9 (short-king status; being on the same eye level with someone has a lot of practical uses), and a doctor who benches 315 pounds. He’s been dubbed “Evil Mamdani” while he’s been campaigning for Senate in Michigan. El-Sayed is a hot guy. He’s not just hot for politics, where the bar is so close to hell that even Eliot Spitzer found a hot second wife 12 years his junior to marry. He’s just a hot person, and we should all be so lucky to have him spit in our mouths. But what makes him hotter, undoubtedly, is his huge capacity for being a dick. His own campaign and even the Democratic Party know this and are happy to weaponize it; go browse their respective TikTok accounts and swim in a sea of El-Sayed clapbacks. His third-most-popular TikTok video is from July Fourth, when he danced around in his trademark black V-neck and American-flag cowboy hat, lipsyncing to Taylor Swift. (Also doing a lot of rotations on my personal TikTok is this video of him doing the Zara Larsson dolphin lift—you can see all his tricep definition.) Last week, Vice President J.D. Vance called El-Sayed a “little weasel.” In response, El-Sayed called himself “a fucking wolverine.” El-Sayed has fused two key components of political hotness into one candidate, and it’s something so few political hopefuls do successfully: He’s ready to bite back at any and all Republicans (including those in the crowds of his rallies), and he’s encapsulating hotness through a prism of traditional masculinity. It’s something Graham Platner tried to do while he was running for Senate in Maine: Throughout his campaign, he wore henleys (the straight-male equivalent of a woman wearing lingerie and holding a whip) and T-shirts over his Nazi tattoo, a thick mustache completing a kind of progressive Ron Swanson look. He was the image of the Brawny man right up until sexual assault allegations (not the Nazi stuff????) derailed his campaign. El-Sayed has adopted some of these same visual cues of traditional male behavior. He’s lifting weights in campaign videos, he’s taking his jacket off to show off his biceps, he’s making fun of Vance for having a beard that doesn’t connect. His interpretation of an attractive candidate isn’t just one in tight jeans and T-shirts (which, again, thank you), but one who acts like a rough-and-tumble boy. El-Sayed is always throwing around a football or flexing a muscle or being mean to another man, punching up with a smile on his face. It’s traditional masculinity retrofitted for a progressive audience. Just because his voters want access to healthcare and to get ICE out of the streets doesn’t mean they don’t appreciate seeing him lift something heavy just for the thirst of it. And while Zohran Mamdani is handling idiotic questions about his religion and his wife with grace and a sincere smile, El-Sayed is here to tell you, essentially, to shut the fuck up. He recently went viral for a conversation he had with “Tommy,” an attendee at one of his events who wanted him to publicly denounce the Muslim Brotherhood. (Tommy, it turned out, was a former Josh Hawley aide and the son of a Republican strategist.) Tommy refused to be upfront about why he would assume El-Sayed, of all people, would support them to begin with. “Hi Tommy. How are you? Do you know what the Constitution of the United States is?” he asked. What an icky watch, witnessing Tommy get his ass gently handed to him. But it’s also pretty hot to watch El-Sayed activate his righteous condescending, hands slipping into his jean pockets, rolling his eyes at the absurdity of such a question and refusing to let it turn into a warped John McCain moment. El-Sayed has learned a lot from more centrist candidates in the party. From Newsom, he learned the art of being an antagonist, of how effectively a man can run as Dennis the Menace against the domineering Republican Party. From Ossoff, he learned how to braid in being an intellectual and polished orator, how debate-club tactics can work as long as you eschew the tie and show an inch of clavicle. In deploying a Republican-style aggression toward his political opponents, he’s found a way to merge all of that with a broad chest that I want to rub my face in like I’m just a tiny woman and he’s an amorous bear. The result isn’t just a political leader you want to vote for, but a daddy—someone you can trust to make the bad guys go away, to make the right decisions, and to shove anyone who stands in the way. The condescension is part of it. It’s been a long time since the Democrats had someone who was willing to be as aggressive as Trump, someone willing to use the tools of combative oration to try to win an election. In a few days, we’ll have some kind of answer for whether it was effective. But if you’re worried about the midterms, about whether there is any one Democrat who can swoop in and save all of us, you just need a little self-care. May I suggest this video that includes several close-ups of El-Sayed’s bicep? It says it’s 17 seconds long, but on my computer it seems to last for hours. There, there, kitten. Daddy’s coming. The Far Right Lost a Foreign Election and MAGA Sees Only One Solution
2026-10-26

The Far Right Lost a Foreign Election and MAGA Sees Only One Solution

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-26

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. If you are not a supporter of right-wing authoritarianism, then Brazilian social media was the place to be today. Brazil’s leftist president, Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva, shocked the world with his underdog victory Sunday night, after lagging behind Flávio Bolsonaro in the first round of voting earlier this month. Pollsters and pundits had expected the supporters of the candidates who were eliminated in the first round to shift to Bolsonaro, a Donald Trump ally. Few predicted what actually happened: Lula won by about 4 million votes, securing a fourth term. On social media, the gleeful Portuguese-language memes escaped containment. You needn’t speak the language to enjoy them. There were compilations of hot Brazilians flashing their support for Lula with “L” hand gestures, and A.I.-generated videos of the 80-year-old Lula in stacked heels and glam bodysuits dancing to Lady Gaga. The “Call an Ambulance” meme got a lot of play. And there was a lot for Brazil’s leftists to celebrate: This wasn’t just a blow to the global right-wing movement but to the Bolsonaro family in particular. The younger Bolsonaro ran as a proxy for his father, Jair, who is under house arrest for plotting a Jan. 6–style coup after losing to Lula in 2022. Lula’s win means the elder Bolsonaro will not walk free. It’s a blow to a political dynasty, and a lot of fodder for jokes. If you are a fan of right-wing authoritarianism, however, political social media looked quite different today. On the right, influencers spoke darkly of Lula’s win as a danger to global democracy. This narrative came about thanks to right-wing influencers’ intentional misrepresentation of a last-minute ruling by Brazil’s Supreme Court that extended voting by two hours. Bolsonaro’s supporters have described this ruling as election interference, as Lula’s team had requested the extension, worried that bus shortages in some areas would prevent voters from getting to the polls on time. But Bolsonaro’s team had also made the extension request, concerned about heavy rain in some of the regions where he polled well. Bolsonaro’s boosters seemed to have conveniently forgotten this fact. Bolsonaro himself has already called for the election to be annulled. (This is not likely to succeed.) Conservatives in the U.S. joined in the outcry, complaining about the voting extension and claiming, without evidence, that there had been significant voting fraud. Elon Musk posted about the election several times, implying to his 241 million followers that the election had been rigged and that the “math doesn’t add up.” Kari Lake, who claimed fraud after losing her own 2022 bid to be governor of Arizona, posted that “Brazil elections remind me of Arizona elections.” One political account, in an X post with more than 2 million views, wrote, “insane man… the world just watched an election be stolen in real time.” Even President Trump gave credence to this idea. “It looked like another rigged election, frankly,” he told reporters. And as with the 2020 presidential election here, right-wing pundits moved swiftly from grouching to talk of action. Some spoke of the need for the U.S. government to do something. Steve Bannon suggested on his podcast that the Trump administration needed to “cut off diplomatic relations, and shut down everything with Brazil” because “if we don’t take action, it’s happening here next Tuesday.” (The “it” here being “election stealing.”) Others backed the idea of refusing to recognize the outcome of this election and forcing Brazil to redo it. “This is FAR from over!” wrote one pro-Trump activist who appeared to favor a mass uprising from Bolsonaro supporters. But soon enough, it seemed like one very particular solution rose to the top: sending the U.S. military into Brazil. As the disgraced former New York Rep. George Santos wrote so eloquently: “Fuck Lula it’s time we give him the Maduro treatment!” Santos wasn’t alone in comparing Lula to Nicolás Maduro, the authoritarian Venezuelan president whom the U.S. military captured in a raid in January under charges of drug trafficking. Maduro has been accused of human rights violations and other dictatorial behavior; Lula, while not entirely clean of corruption allegations, has no such history of abuses. (Lula was imprisoned on corruption charges in 2018, but the charges were later annulled.) But in the narrative of the online right, “Lula = Maduro,” as the Utah Sen. Mike Lee wrote. And so influencers are urging Trump to send in a team to kidnap Lula. As the commentator Alex Jones said, “Brazil must be liberated.” Perhaps because the elder Bolsonaro already failed to pull off a successful coup despite being Brazil’s president at the time, these influencers don’t seem confident Flavio can do what they want. Instead, they’re looking to another entry in Trump’s playbook—not for Brazil to copy, but for Trump to play out his moves again. This idea might give Trump supporters hope, but there’s a downside to this argument: It sure seems like a bad sign for the global authoritarian movement if American right-wingers can only trust their own strongman leader to get the dirty work done. OK, let’s shift gears from international relations to something closer to home—like what else you should be reading this evening: Make sense of a MAGA mystery: What on earth is going on with Nicki Minaj? If you’re confused about why the rapper joined Donald Trump at a political rally this weekend (at which the president also talked about her butt …), Luke Winkie can help fill in the details. Back in March, he laid out why Minaj decided to hop on the Trump bandwagon. Ponder the first lady’s foray into diplomacy: Melania Trump is an enigmatic figure in Trumpworld. What is she doing during Trump 2.0? Apparently, she’s been busy brokering a ceasefire between Russian and Ukrainian forces in the occupied city of Oleshky. Heather Schwedel explains what the first lady has been up to. Discover the truth about You Can See Everything: Nathan Fielder’s reality-bending documentary has prompted an explosion of discourse about Elizabeth Holmes’ trustworthiness and whether the convicted fraudster is actually a victim. Slate editor Shannon Palus analyzes the movie’s astonishing magic trick. Indulge in some workplace schadenfreude: Some people despise their jobs, but, for whatever reason, refuse to leave. Alison Green, the advice maven behind Ask a Manager, explores what makes some employees so resistant to quitting a toxic job. That’s it for today. Ian Prasad Philbrick will be back tomorrow. One Justice Made a Startling Admission About the Supreme Court
2026-10-26

One Justice Made a Startling Admission About the Supreme Court

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-26

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. This past week, Justice Elena Kagan gave a speech at Catholic University law school in D.C., warning the American public and her colleagues about the ways that the Supreme Court itself is undermining democratic governance. The speech was noteworthy in its unvarnished language about an American public that has lost confidence in her court, for good reason. On this week’s Amicus bonus episode, Dahlia Lithwick discussed the nature of Kagan’s concerns with Madiba K. Dennie, the deputy editor and senior contributor at the legal commentary outlet, Balls and Strikes, and author of The Originalism Trap. Their conversation has been edited and condensed for clarity. Dahlia Lithwick: Justice Kagan is almost the most careful of all the sitting justices not to say anything polemical, or partisan, or inflammatory. Yes, she can throw down in a dissent, but she’s very careful in her speeches. She doesn’t say things unless she very much intends to say them, and she absolutely went there on this topic of a politicized Supreme Court. It’s worth noting and she did so just a couple of weeks before the midterms. That certainly suggests that she is thinking of this time as a kind of break-the-glass moment for voting rights, for democracy, and maybe more profoundly, for an American public that is losing confidence in the high court. She said the American people “look around this political system and think that all this money chasing all these political candidates is a nightmare … and they’re right.” She’s not just saying the court is putting a thumb on the scale to warp voting rights—that’s the gravamen of the speech—she’s also saying the American people are not fooled that this is a neutral policy. Madiba Dennie: She’s saying the court is making everything worse, and people can see that. People can recognize that it’s distorting democracy in favor of one political party. But beyond that, it’s distorting democracy in favor of big money. Justice Kagan is saying we have this system now that is just flush with cash from who knows where, and it is hard for regular people to find information, to make their voice heard, to have a working relationship with the person who was supposed to be representing them in office because those people are beholden to special interests. And these are all things the court has made possible by getting rid of campaign finance laws, by allowing all sorts of gerrymandering to happen, by making it harder and harder to prove political corruption. So basically, the court is taking a series of actions that make it so democracy can’t work. And she is acknowledging that. We so rarely see people in places like the Supreme Court acknowledge that these are affirmatively bad decisions that are worsening democracy for the overwhelming majority of people. It was a surprising moment to see a sitting Supreme Court justice admit that outright. It’s also a way of saying your vote matters. It’s a way of saying, “I know that you all think the system is cooked, and I see you seeing that the court is cooking it.” And also, get out there and fight for democracy. With the full understanding that the court is constricting the vote, that your vote matters more than ever. Because people have to vote in order to have a Congress that can pursue court reform. The timing isn’t an accident. I want to give you a chance to take a whack at originalism, because I know that she took a whack at originalism, and taking whacks at originalism is your love language. Justice Kagan said, “They were writing the Constitution for the ages. They were writing the Constitution knowing that there would be change in the world. … The whole project of originalism is kind of misconceived, and it’s not a very good match with the Constitution we have.” There are a few things that jump out at me about that statement. One, she is acknowledging that originalism is a project, that it is a thing that the conservative legal movement developed that has goals they want to achieve. Originalism is so often treated as this neutral, objective, totally legitimate theory, so seeing it discussed as a “project” is something that pleases me. Also, the acknowledgment that that project is in conflict with the Constitution, with the way democracy is supposed to work. The Constitution is supposed to be a lasting, living document that continues to serve the people who are alive. In his new book, Justice Samuel Alito describes how he sometimes wonders what the Bill of Rights might have meant to the average patron “at one of the colonial-era taverns”—you don’t even have to leave out the tavern people entirely, but just maybe, you know, think about the rest of us too, perhaps. Secondly, Justice Kagan is acknowledging the disconnect between that project and actually serving people today in a modern multiracial democracy. And she is saying all this straight up, actually plainly saying it out loud. These are things that I feel would have been, if not third rail, at least second rail and off-limits not too long ago. It’s a shift that we now have Kagan saying that there’s a mismatch between originalism and the Constitution, when a few years ago she was saying (somewhat sarcastically) originalism was so prominent that “I guess we’re all originalists now.” It’s a huge shift in the Overton window to move from that, to saying that originalism and the Constitution are inherently in conflict. I think about Hillary Clinton, of all people, saying we need to have 13 justices the other day. These are things that were too spicy for stodgy liberal types just a few years ago, and now we have Hillary Clinton saying we need to have court expansion. It seems like such a shift among the elites finally saying that the system we have now is not sustainable, and that we need to do things like change our method of constitutional interpretation. We need to do things like change how the court is composed and change not only what it is that the court is actually doing, but also how. As you very well know, Dahlia, I have been trying to have this conversation with anyone who would listen for years. But if folks are ready to have it now, I am very happy to have it. And there’s pure Elena Kagan jujitsu going on here. As you say, she takes it head-on. She’s saying that not only is the original intent of the Constitution not originalism, and the original intent of the Framers is not originalism, but it is not even originalist to think of originalism as the only way to proceed. She’s so smart. Big heart eyes for you, EMK. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. If you value our work, please disable your ad blocker. News and Politics - Slate Magazine
Why Republicans Find It So Easy to Defend Child Sex Offenders Like the Man Who Raped Amy Acton
2026-10-26

Why Republicans Find It So Easy to Defend Child Sex Offenders Like the Man Who Raped Amy Acton

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-26

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. When I first met my late husband, I was shocked to learn that he was estranged from his mother. He seemed so family-oriented, and he was warm and receptive to my own mom. If I’m being honest, the estrangement raised a red flag. Was he difficult to get along with? Was he putting on an act with my mother? But months into our relationship, he revealed a dark secret that helped explain things: His stepfather had sexually abused him for years, starting when he was 13. He had been afraid to come forward at the time, but later in life, after years of therapy and his stepfather’s death, he told his mother. She did not believe him, and their relationship never recovered. Child sexual abuse like what my husband and Ohio gubernatorial candidate Amy Acton have endured is not rare. As many as 1 out of 4 girls and 1 out of 6 boys will experience sexual abuse before the age of 18. Three-quarters of child sexual abuse cases are committed by family members. Most perpetrators are men, commonly stepfathers. Child sexual abuse crosses cultural and economic boundaries, affecting millions of Americans. Acton’s story reflects these statistics. Her stepfather was indicted for raping her for years, with the sexual abuse beginning when she was 10 years old. After Acton reported the abuse, her mother didn’t believe her, and they have been estranged since she was 12. Last week, nearly 40 years later, Acton’s mother, Donna Arthur, called her daughter a liar in an ad paid for by Vivek Ramaswamy, her Republican opponent in the Ohio gubernatorial race. The statistics on intrafamilial child sexual abuse are disturbing. Yet we should not be surprised that Ramaswamy’s campaign, and President Donald Trump, thought it was a clever political tactic to exploit Acton’s trauma and invoke the familiar trope that victims are liars. The choice was so stark that even former President Barack Obama weighed in on it while campaigning over the weekend: “There’s a decency that we should expect out of our public officials. And I don’t know what’s happened where the standards of behavior have gotten so low that somebody can run an ad like that.” But it’s quite simple: The ad reflects deeply ingrained societal attitudes toward sexual abuse victims, particularly children. First, and most damning: We don’t believe children. A third of children abused in the home do not receive a supportive response when they disclose the abuse. Female adolescents are the least likely to be believed. Second, we blame children. Victim blaming reflects an often unconscious belief that a survivor’s character is somehow tainted, even when that survivor is a child. This phenomenon stems in part from the just-world cognitive bias, the tendency to believe that the world is fundamentally fair and that good people are rewarded while bad people are punished. Female survivors of sexual assault, for example, frequently report being blamed for what happened, even by prosecutors who are supposed to be seeking justice on their behalf. We are seeing this dynamic in the backlash against Jane Doe at Cornell. Children experience it, too. Nearly 75 percent of children who report sexual abuse receive an overall negative response, including retaliation. Third, we fail to address the physical and psychological harms of child sexual abuse. Child sexual abuse is a public health crisis with both short- and long-term consequences. Even among people who have experienced multiple forms of victimization, child sexual abuse appears to have a unique negative impact on physical and psychosocial functioning. Victims like my husband know that child sexual abuse can cause lifelong trauma, even long after the perpetrator has died. Yet, as a society, we fail to do anything meaningful about it. Systemic failures contribute to this problem, including poor coordination among the institutions responsible for providing medical, psychosocial, and legal assistance to children and families. But fundamentally, we fail children when we don’t believe them in the first place. Research suggests that we have it wrong when it comes to children’s truthfulness. Children rarely lie about sexual abuse. In fact, the opposite is often true: Children may lie to conceal abuse. Those who do report abuse may later retract their disclosures because of pressure from caregivers who refuse to believe them, like my husband’s mother and Acton’s mother. When we dismiss children’s disclosures, we deny them the opportunity to receive protection and support. Some, like my husband, seek therapy as adults. Far too many bury their pain. Acton did not bury her pain, and now Ramaswamy is using it against her. Before the ad featuring her mother, Acton had spoken openly about her trauma. That could not have been easy. I imagine she would rather not have to revisit her past. But just as my husband’s story helped me understand him, Acton’s story helps constituents understand who she is and why she is running for office. Ramaswamy, however, has turned her vulnerability into a campaign tactic. His super PAC is called “Victors, Not Victims,” a framing of strength that has helped attract approximately $60 million in campaign donations. We should want our politicians to demonstrate both strength and vulnerability. We should want them to empathize with marginalized groups, including abuse victims and children. Being sexually abused as a child is not a mark of shame, a character flaw, or a disqualification from leadership. The shame belongs to the abuser, not the child, and certainly not to the adult that child becomes. If the attention surrounding this ad campaign accomplishes anything, it should be to raise awareness of how prevalent child sexual abuse is and how badly we fail children when we discredit them. Let’s not simply chalk this up to nasty politics. Let’s use this moment to confront a culture that disbelieves children, blames victims, and protects those who abuse them—and demand better from our politicians, our institutions, and ourselves. Microsoft releases out-of-band Windows fixes with certificate updates
2026-10-27

Microsoft releases out-of-band Windows fixes with certificate updates

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Did you hear the one about eMudhra and the power of 4? Time to get connected to certificate trust list updates Microsoft has released another out-of-band update for Windows, but this one isn't an emergency fix for the latest breakages. This time, it's all about certificates. The non-security out-of-band release applies to supported OSes ranging from Windows 11 and Windows Server 2025, to Windows 10. It's all about updating the current certificate trust list (CTL) of devices that cannot access Microsoft's certificate-update service, or where automatic CTL updates are disabled. It also contains everything in the October 13 update, including the ongoing VPN issues and the change that could break on-premises Exchange Servers. There might also be oddities with Smart App Control, audio, and Remote Desktop connections for non-admin users, all of which are known issues in the October 13 update. These problems are not resolved in this patch, so be aware of this when installing it. It is also worth remembering that many devices will not need this version since the current certificate list updates usually come down through the update service. However, if that service isn't available or being used, it might be a good idea to get this out-of-band fix (while keeping the known issues in mind), since problems can occur when verifying certificates used by applications or websites. The OOB update came after Microsoft laid out its response to the compromised eMudhra certificate keys. The Indian certification authority's private keys were compromised earlier this month, requiring a massive cleanup effort. According to Microsoft, "they could potentially be used to create certificates or sign content that appeared to come from a trusted CA [certificate authority]." Like many other organizations, Microsoft moved swiftly to block the affected certificate authorities. It did so by updating its Windows Disallowed Certificate Trust List to block the affected certificates. There was also a separate CRLSet release for Edge and WebView2. According to the Windows behemoth, "On October 26, Microsoft released an out-of-band Windows update that removes seven certificates associated with the affected hierarchies: six eMudhra G3 roots and an AAA Certificate Services cross-sign, from the Windows trust list." Microsoft's out-of-band patches are usually released after the company breaks a product and needs to issue an emergency fix. This time, the revision is due to Somebody Else's Problem and a need to get a CTL update out to devices that are disconnected from CTL updates. ® Did you hear the one about eMudhra and the power of 4? Attack on email hosting provider for German far right party AfD exposed 345k accounts
2026-10-27

Attack on email hosting provider for German far right party AfD exposed 345k accounts

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Alternative für Deutschland swats away scandals as reporters swarm leaked email tranche The cyberattack on Alternative for Germany (AfD), the country’s most prominent far-right political party, exposed hundreds of thousands of confidential emails and attachments, as well as more than 345,000 unique email addresses. Have I Been Pwned confirmed the scale of the breach for the first time today, after AfD officially disclosed the September attack on its hosting provider last week. AfD said in its statement that “only a few email accounts were illegally copied,” but admitted a large amount of the data was taken from them, resulting in an extensive data breach. The emails were added to what the party described as “an activist website,” which summarised the email chains into searchable topics and has led to the publication of various revelations, such as a party co-leader’s opinions on colleagues’ “deeply problematic” racial discourse. Per a report from investigative journalist Lilith Wittmann, citing conversations with the cybercriminals responsible for the hack-and-leak job and party sources, the attack was first spotted on September 9 and started with a compromise of a poorly protected WordPress site. The same report claimed the criminals gained persistent access to the servers operated by the hosting provider, identified as AfD-owned Alternita Dienstleistungs GmbH, and exfiltrated more than a terabyte of data. No official sources have verified the hackers’ claims about how the attack unfolded. The Register contacted Alternita Dienstleistungs for its take. AfD said the owners of the affected email accounts have been notified, and that it reported the attack to security authorities. “We are deeply concerned by what has happened,” the party said in an October 20 statement. “Such a digital attack is illegal and a criminal offense. Furthermore, it is an attack on the German party system and thus on democracy. “We are extremely worried that, apparently, the content of the stolen documents was analyzed using a very rudimentary AI. This analysis focused on keywords, not the context. The alleged scandals published so far do not stand up to scrutiny – a fact that, thankfully, is being confirmed by a growing number of commentators on social media.” Among the AI-generated summaries compiled by the “activist site” are chains supposedly expressing views related to Holocaust denial, racist and antisemitic rants, the 9/11 disaster, forged documents, and more. Der Spiegel sifted through the raw files and reported that party leader Alice Weidel seemingly knew about illegal donations related to the 2017 federal elections weeks before she previously said she did. The AfD's position is that the AI summaries of the emails are not to be trusted, but it has not specifically commented on the veracity of the raw email files available for download via torrent. It did not immediately respond to our request for input, but has told reporters previously that it will not comment on whether the emails, or the stories that have been written based on them, are to be trusted. ® Did you hear the one about eMudhra and the power of 4? UK ministers had plenty of time for AI datacenter bosses, but none for environmental groups
2026-10-27

UK ministers had plenty of time for AI datacenter bosses, but none for environmental groups

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Investigation finds businesses dominated govt meetings while communities were left out of decisions about AI Growth Zones The British government has been busy courting AI datacenter companies while apparently finding little time to speak to the people who will have to live next door to their enormous server farms. That’s according to a report published Tuesday by campaign group Spotlight on Corruption, which found that 84 percent of the stakeholders ministers met with to discuss datacenters between July 2024 and June 2026 were businesses or private-sector trade associations. Environmental groups, by contrast, didn't get a single meeting with ministers or senior officials. The findings raise questions about who gets a say in Britain's AI Growth Zones, which the government hopes will attract billions of pounds in investment by making it easier and cheaper to build the infrastructure needed to power its AI ambitions. According to the report, titled Zoned Out, ministers met representatives from 137 different businesses during the two-year period, while permanent secretaries and directors general held no meetings with community groups about datacenters. Local residents didn't fare much better. Ministers met just two community groups, both after decisions had already been made to designate their areas as AI Growth Zones. In one case, the meeting took place on the very day the designation was announced. The government also failed to conduct a national public consultation on its AI Growth Zone strategy, despite the potential consequences for electricity supplies, water consumption, and local communities. Spotlight found no independent consultations with residents in the selected areas, although some developers conducted their own. Even local councils appear to have struggled to get a hearing. Of the ten local authorities and devolved governments affected by the five announced zones, half reported no communication with ministers or senior DSIT officials beyond their applications. Two had exchanged brief emails, while another secured a 15-minute meeting with a junior minister. The government has been more accommodating to the companies building these facilities. AI Growth Zones offer developers faster planning decisions and better access to electricity infrastructure, with the aim of rapidly expanding Britain's computing capacity. As we reported in March, the government has also been consulting on proposals that could allow strategically important datacenters to jump the electricity grid connection queue, potentially ahead of housing developments already waiting for power. So while datacenter developers could soon find themselves jumping the grid connection queue, the people living near these facilities have had little say in the government's plans. Spotlight also takes aim at the government's AI Energy Council, which includes representatives from Microsoft, Google, and Amazon Web Services but no environmental NGOs. Its Sustainability Working Group, established to examine the environmental impact of AI, managed just one meeting between April 2025 and July 2026. Concerns also remain about who is influencing policy from outside government. Spotlight identified 38 appointments of former ministers and senior civil servants by AI companies between July 2024 and September 2026, including nine people who previously worked at DSIT. Spotlight reckons Whitehall's transparency rules could make the picture even murkier. While ministers have to declare their meetings, unpaid advisers don't face the same requirements, even when they're helping shape government AI policy. So there's no way of knowing exactly who has been bending whose ear. The campaign group wants the government to hit pause on new AI Growth Zones and datacenters until it tightens the rules on public consultation and forces developers to be more upfront about how much electricity and water their facilities will guzzle, and the emissions they'll produce. The campaign group argues that Britain is falling behind countries including Germany, Canada, and Ireland, which have conducted national consultations on their datacenter strategies. For a government determined to turn Britain into an AI powerhouse, there's clearly no shortage of companies willing to help build the infrastructure. But finding room at the table for the people expected to live alongside it seems like another matter. ® Did you hear the one about eMudhra and the power of 4? FreeBSD founder ports Omarchy to FreeBSD in ten days, ten releases
2026-10-27

FreeBSD founder ports Omarchy to FreeBSD in ten days, ten releases

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Daemonarchy is unaffiliated with the Linux original, built with AI help, and could complicate FreeBSD's debate over AI contributions Omarchy has already taken up plenty of our column inches, but a new tentacle is reaching into the hitherto untouched FreeBSD. Daemonarchy brings Omarchy's Wayland-based desktop to the BSD world. It's an independent effort by Jordan Hubbard, one of the original founders of the FreeBSD project, and it's unaffiliated with the original Linux-based Omarchy. We looked at Omarchy in August and didn’t like it much. It isn't exactly a Linux distribution. That implies it's a coherent set of packages managed as a whole by uniform tools. Omarchy is a collection of apps and preconfigured settings, packaged up with the help of LLM bots and put up for download. You have to update it using its own special script, rather than the standard tools of the underlying Arch Linux – and in our testing, that update script fell over with error messages when running in a VM. However, there's no such thing as a bad press, and Omarchy is popular and growing. It keeps on attracting sponsorship as well as new users migrating from Windows and macOS, who seem to relish its bizarre, inconsistent, and ad hoc UI as a challenge: trying to close a window in Hyprland, the tiling compositor that forms the core of Omarchy's UI. In Omarchy, windows don't have title bars, let alone anything as old-fashioned as window control buttons you can click. To close a window, you must use a keystroke. In most Linux windowing environments, that's the standard Alt-F4. Not here. Next, we tried Super+W – on a Mac, that's Command+W, which has meant "close the window" since 1984 or so. Still no. We found an answer on Reddit – it's Super + C. Daemonarchy is not directly related. It's a collection of FreeBSD ports of the various tools and components that went into Omarchy, individually adapted to run on FreeBSD, and collected together into an installable ISO image. The current release is version 1.3.1. Remarkably, in approximately 10 days, Daemonarchy has gone through 10 releases. Multiple packages are recent ports by jkh@freebsd.org – the email address of Jordan Hubbard. If the name rings a bell, he was one of the original founders of the FreeBSD project, and has had an interesting career in which he’s worked at various companies. The Register first mentioned him by name 25 years ago, when Wind River bought BSD/OS vendors BSDi. By the following year, Hubbard was at Apple, where he helped freshen up the BSD-based Unix layer underneath the new Mac OS X. In 2015, he helped launch the NextBSD project to modernize FreeBSD with Apple Darwin components, which as we reported in July was recently restarted. The new NextBSD effort is being built with the help of LLM coding assistants. As you might surmise from 18 new FreeBSD ports of Linux tools in a month, as well as from ten releases of the new OS bundle in as many days, Hubbard too is using LLMs to assist in this rapid development process: Daemonarchy’s GitHub repository contains both AGENTS.md and SKILL.md files, which point to Hubbard's own Literate AI project, whose repo describes it as a "A meta-harness for coding agents to follow proper Software Development Lifecycle practices", and says it is "Based on literate programming ideas from Donald Knuth". There’s quite a bit of documentation, including a project specification with a list of goals. Lots of formatted Markdown text is also something of an LLM bot hallmark, though, and it’s not clear to us if this is intended for humans to read, or to guide the bots. Possibly, it's both. It looks like the germ of inspiration was a LinkedIn post by UK IT consultant Sam Sheridan, in which he criticized the difficulty of configuring a new installation of FreeBSD 15.1 with an Nvidia card. He's right: we have written about how much work is involved ourselves. Sheridan's LinkedIn post provoked a comment from Ralph Kepper saying "I'm just gonna say it… omarchy for FreeBSD, its the ugly truth and no one likes it but there it is. omarchy will get us the youngsters and if we do it right we will inspire some great minds along the way". Hubbard had already commented in the thread, and it looks to us like that passing comment has prompted (if you will forgive us the obvious pun) this effort. Daemonarchy is based on FreeBSD CURRENT, which means that it's built on top of what will become FreeBSD 16 – planned for December 2027. The intrepid can get run it now, though. Currently it targets the MacBookPro16,1, meaning the 2019 models of the 16-inch MacBook Pro – one of the final generations of Intel-based MacBooks. A couple of years ago, the Reg FOSS desk attended EuroBSDCon and we met several core project members. Apparently, running the development work-in-progress CURRENT branch is quite normal for hardcore FreeBSD types. If you need to ask how to get it or run it, then this is not the version of FreeBSD for you. We do not personally like Omarchy much. We don't much like the visual design, or the choice of components – and we really didn't get on with the user interface. As mentioned, there are already established keyboard UIs for both macOS and MS Windows, and we’re adept and comfortable with both. As Linux is an OS designed and built on the x86 PC platform, we feel that it should stick to the established Common User Access UI. Its core elements débuted in Windows 1.0, 41 years ago. It's quite a bit older than Linux itself, and led to widespread UI standardization across DOS, OS/2, all subsequent versions of Windows, and more or less all Linux GUIs… at least until the recent rash of Wayland compositors from young hackers who don't know how to drive windows efficiently. We are not saying it can't be improved upon, but re-inventing this particular wheel strikes us as a worked example of XKCD 927. However, Kepper has a point: Omarchy is trendy, and a FreeBSD version – freed of the direct influence of its controversial creator – might win FreeBSD some new fans. Even if not, the Daemonarchy project could have wider impact. For one thing, the effort could help the creation of an easy, graphical FreeBSD installer and a friendly desktop version. It’s also very relevant to the ongoing debate about the use of AI coding assistants in the FreeBSD project. We mentioned this in September 2025 and there’s been no public announcement of a decision yet. In that article, we highlighted Sylve. The project GitHub describes this as a “lightweight GUI for managing Bhyve, Jails, ZFS, networking, and more on FreeBSD”, but the project has even wider ambitions than that. The developers recently shared a video of their new graphical installer for FreeBSD, and we think it looks very good indeed. We are less keen on the other impact that Daemonarchy seems likely to have. With the FreeBSD project's co-founder busily committing code to the project repositories that he is porting and developing with the assistance of Claude, then it seems highly unlikely that the core team will respond by banning AI contributions. ® Our thanks to Register reader Graham Perrin for drawing our attention to Daemonarchy – and the thread that inspired it. Did you hear the one about eMudhra and the power of 4? Year of Linux on the Beach confirmed

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Don't forget to keep code up to date. Even with a cold beverage in hand 2026 might not be the Year of Linux on the Desktop, but it is certainly the Year of Linux on the Beachfront, judging by some digital signage at the Spanish resort of Lloret de Mar. Snapped by Rich Skuse, an aficionado of automotive shonk, this sign skips the town's attractions or useful information entirely. Instead, it gives away its Ubuntu and Firefox roots with an update prompt and a perky "Welcome back," suggesting someone has turned it off and on again. As this signage is likely connected to the Internet, that Ubuntu update is probably a good idea. 26.04.1 is the latest LTS version and contains a raft of tweaks and improvements. Sadly, we expect beachgoers are probably more interested in sun, sand, and sangria than in keeping a Linux installation in tip-top shape. The sign is a recent addition to the Passeig d'Agustí Font. Behind the photographer stand palm trees, with a beach and the azure waters of the Mediterranean Sea beyond. Lloret de Mar underwent several renovations ahead of the 2026 summer season, and we guess the sign was one of them, since it wasn't there in April 2025 (according to a well-known ad slinger). According to Lloret Gaceta, a publication for the region, "The goal is to continue evolving into a more modern, sustainable, accessible, and higher-quality tourist destination, adapted to the new demands of residents and visitors." Readers might be interested to learn that part of this evolution includes a ban on selling alcoholic beverages in containers larger than one liter at beach bars. A few too many adult drinks could explain the configuration that led to this bork, or perhaps an engineer took one look outside, at another balmy day, and decided "that'll do" when it came to configuring the signage. Either way, this is a tourist destination, and the last thing the average administrator wants to be reminded of on their vacation is the need to keep code up to date. As such, we suspect many will simply ignore the distressed digital display and instead seek out somewhere shady to sit, where the seafront can be admired, beverages consumed, and the bork…? Well, it's somebody else's problem. ® Did you hear the one about eMudhra and the power of 4?
