How everyday technology choices drive true workplace sustainability
2026-10-27

How everyday technology choices drive true workplace sustainability

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
SPONSORED FEATURE: Introducing certified, environmentally friendly meeting room solutions, such as Barco ClickShare, in a standardized way across the organization can make the difference between hitting sustainability goals or not For many members of the C-suite these days, sustainability is a top three agenda item alongside technology and AI adoption, according to research from Deloitte. In fact, the report says, technology is considered a vital means of hitting organizational goals here, with the implementation of IT solutions being the most common approach for doing so. The importance of tech's role in this context is reflected in the views of IT decision-makers themselves. A recent survey, conducted by Barco ClickShare, revealed that 96 percent of the 500 US tech leaders questioned consider their sustainability activities to make a significant and positive global contribution to strategy here. A further 98 percent feel their departments should lead the way in such activities. Indeed, more than four out of five felt so strongly about the situation that they would turn down a job offer at a new employer if it did not have a strong sustainability track record, they said. Yannic Laleeuwe is Marketing Director Workplace Collaboration at wireless conferencing and collaboration platform provider, Barco ClickShare. She believes IT functions have this central role to play in tackling sustainability issues because many of their "day-to-day technology choices directly influence an organization's environmental footprint". "Sustainability is about practical decisions: buying reliable technology, extending product life, reducing complexity, and ensuring devices can be managed, repaired and responsibly recycled," Laleeuwe explains. But given that technology-related decisions are rarely made by IT leaders alone, it is clear they cannot tackle sustainability challenges alone either. As Barco ClickShare's report indicates, a mere 6 percent of IT departments are the sole decision-makers on tech investments, which includes meeting room systems. This means that collaboration with other stakeholders is vital. Such stakeholders include not only sustainability and environmental affairs teams. They also consist of procurement, finance, facilities management, operations, and executive leaders. "Working together, it's possible to balance security, usability, cost, lifecycle impact, energy efficiency, repairability and end-of-life recovery before a purchase is even made," Laleeuwe points out. In other words, by collaborating, it becomes much easier to embed sustainability considerations into day-to-day activities. This includes maintaining and upgrading equipment properly to extend its life. It is these everyday IT decisions that can make all the difference in shaping behavior in the digital workplace. One example of this relates to the procurement process itself, which is often complex. But top of the list on any sustainable shopping list should be ensuring that technology in general, but meeting room equipment in particular, is secure, reliable, and easy to integrate with other systems. This is important because if kit is not durable and interoperable, it will come up for replacement sooner rather than later. But such an approach not only generates waste, it also necessitates purchasing new items. These tend to have a high carbon footprint due to the required extraction of raw materials and high energy usage during both manufacturing and transportation processes. As for the key questions prospective buyers should be asking their suppliers upfront here, these include which methodologies their products are based on and what certifications they hold. Valid certifications in this context include TCO Certified's global sustainability for IT products marque and the US government's Energy Star symbol for energy efficiency. In fact, sustainability certifications ranked joint second with cost as the most important factor when purchasing hardware, according to participants in Barco ClickShare's survey. They were topped only by security. However, it is essential to ensure that certifications and carbon-neutral claims offer "transparency, not just reassurance", Laleeuwe warns. As such, "they should show how the product performs, which lifecycle stages are covered, how claims are validated, and what concrete design choices reduce impact," he says. In other words, it is essential to understand whether suppliers have taken environmental factors into consideration across their entire product lifecycle. Such factors include product design, which should ideally be modular so that individual components can be repaired or replaced as required. They also include which materials have been used in the manufacturing process and whether they are recyclable or at least have a low environmental footprint. The same rules apply to packaging. Other essential considerations for IT leaders during the procurement process, meanwhile, include the energy consumption of their chosen conferencing and communication platform during usage. Just as important is whether individual vendors offer take-back programs or other responsible disposal routes for products at end-of-life, such as repair and recycling. The aim here is to ensure that old equipment does not pollute the environment by ending up in landfill. As Laleeuwe points out: "Prioritizing repair and recycling extends product life, avoids premature disposal and turns ESG [environmental, social, and governance] goals into repeatable IT practices." Unfortunately, though, such activities tend to be all too frequently overlooked today. For example, Barco ClickShare's research reveals that only 42 percent of IT leaders currently select equipment that can be repaired by their manufacturer to extend its life. Even fewer (16 percent) rank repairability as a top concern. The figures are even worse in terms of recycling (11 percent) being prioritized. Unsurprisingly then, just over a third of respondents indicated their department does not always go down this route. A key problem here, says Laleeuwe, is that such activities are often simply "less visible at the initial point of purchase", which means they are rarely front of mind. Another common challenge, meanwhile, relates to the ongoing shift towards hybrid and remote working. But according to the Barco ClickShare report, some 95 percent of IT leaders believe such practices have had an impact on their organization's sustainability efforts, ultimately making them more complex. This is at least partially due to meeting room equipment now being spread across office, home, and flexible workspaces. On the downside, this means it has become more difficult to manage and recycle e-waste from remote locations (48 percent). The growing use of personal devices has also made it trickier to ensure the adoption of energy-efficient hardware (46 percent). But on the plus side, there has been an increase in uptake of more energy-efficient cloud services compared to on premise systems (63 percent of respondents). Even here though, things are not always clear cut. As Laleeuwe explains: "IT teams need to look at the full setup: utilization, energy performance, device longevity, security, manageability and end-of-life recovery. The most sustainable choice is the one that delivers the required experience with the least avoidable footprint." One approach that does produce much more reliable results in sustainability terms though is standardizing equipment across the organization, especially in a meeting room system context. In the past, individual business departments all too often purchased such kit without consulting IT, leading to the deployment of fragmented, siloed systems. But standardization has shown it can offer various benefits. On the one hand, Laleeuwe explains: "It limits the number of device types, spare parts, support processes and replacement cycles IT has to manage, while making updates, remote management and lifecycle planning easier." On the other, organizations can benefit from economies of scale - although to what extent depends on how much e-waste or energy usage they can cut based on the size of their IT estate, usage patterns, refresh cycles, and recycling processes. A further benefit of standardization is that it provides organizations with "the control needed to measure, manage and reduce impact consistently", Laleeuwe adds. What this demonstrates is that when ensuring sustainability goals are translated into day-to-day IT decision-making, routine choices matter. In other words, sustainability must become part of procurement requirements, implementation checklists, and lifecycle management as standard. "That means setting shared criteria for certifications, energy efficiency, durability, repairability, recyclability, vendor take-back, and lifecycle reporting," Laleeuwe says. "It also means giving IT, procurement, facilities, finance and sustainability teams joint ownership of the process." One organization that successfully embedded just this kind of approach into its workplace strategy is the Flemish Government. When renovating its Marie-Elisabeth Belpaire headquarters building in Brussels, Belgium, the Agency for Facility Operations took a multi-layered approach to sustainability. For instance, the building itself became the first in Flanders to conform to circular construction practices. This included using 68 percent reclaimed, reused, or recycled materials from the original site in its renovation activities. The building is also close to operating at net zero. This is partly due to its Thermal Energy Storage system, which captures and stores thermal energy for later use. It is also partly because of the introduction of solar panels on its roof and side walls. But another key consideration in ensuring a sustainable headquarters was the widespread adoption of green ICT. This included the introduction of a hyper-efficient Wi-Fi network to help the Government save the equivalent of 550 households-worth of electricity each year. Just as important was the standardization of the Government's meeting room technology using certified sustainable technology. To this end, more than 1,000 ClickShare wireless conferencing and collaboration devices were implemented in more than 1,000 different meeting spaces. The aim of standardizing equipment in this way was both to cut energy usage and the meeting room infrastructure's overall carbon footprint. As Laleeuwe concludes: "The lesson is clear: sustainable meeting rooms work best when technology, workplace design, user experience and lifecycle management are all planned for together and implemented in tandem." Did you hear the one about eMudhra and the power of 4? Fujifilm’s Instax Mini Evo 2 adds a touchscreen and video recording
2026-10-27

Fujifilm’s Instax Mini Evo 2 adds a touchscreen and video recording

The Verge | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
The most capable Instax is now even closer to a traditional digital camera.﻿ The most capable Instax is now even closer to a traditional digital camera.﻿ Fujifilm is trying to find the right balance of fun and photography with its latest hybrid instant camera. Like its predecessor, the Instax Mini Evo 2 feels less like a retro gimmick, and more like a basic digital camera that just happens to have a built-in printer. It also enjoys some key upgrades: a larger 1/2.77-inch sensor compared to the 1/5-inch sensor on the first Mini Evo, a bigger 4.5-inch rear display with touchscreen controls, and basic video recording too — though only the few seconds leading up to a photo. Video is limited in other ways compared to modern standards — the MP4 clips can only be saved to a microSD card (though there is internal storage for photos), no audio is recorded, and quality is limited to 560 × 992 pixels and 24FPS, according to DPReview — but it’s still a feature we wouldn’t expect to see on an instant camera. This is also Fujifilm’s first Instax model to feature an LCD touchscreen, ditching the rear controls in favor of a larger display that takes up the entire back of the camera. Other physical controls have been preserved though, including two dials and a film-inspired print lever. You can use either the dials or touchscreen to access the 10 film and 10 lens effect options that were available on the first Instax Mini Evo, though the selection has changed slightly with this new model. There’s also an Expanded View mode that “gives users a wider field of view than the shooting area.” The camera charges via USB-C, prints 2 x 3-inch Instax Mini format photos, and lets you adjust the exposure, white balance, and flash settings on your snaps. The Instax Mini Evo 2 will be available for $290 and goes on sale at the end of November. Black is the only available color option, but it still retains the classic retro look that’ll probably land well with the youth market. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. Sterling Anderson doesn’t think humanoid robots should be building cars
2026-10-27

Sterling Anderson doesn’t think humanoid robots should be building cars

The Verge | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
General Motors’ product chief breaks down the company’s software evolution, the physics of multisensor vehicle safety, and why personally owned autonomous vehicles beat robotaxis. General Motors’ product chief breaks down the company’s software evolution, the physics of multisensor vehicle safety, and why personally owned autonomous vehicles beat robotaxis. At a splashy event in New York City last week, General Motors unveiled its new tech roadmap centered on AI-driven vehicle development, advanced battery tech, high-powered car computers, and autonomous driving. Some of this was old news: We already knew the automaker was developing hands-free, eyes-off driving automation and had been prototyping new battery chemistries. In a sense, the real purpose of the event was to showcase how all these pieces fit together. With its new “intelligence-defined vehicle” paradigm, GM is banking on artificial intelligence, centralized computing, and virtualized engineering to cut vehicle development timelines in half, aiming to compress a typical four- to five-year process down to just over two. Yet, this high-tech overhaul arrives against a backdrop of steep industry headwinds and razor-thin margins for error. GM’s aggressive pivot comes as the automaker faces a wave of hyperefficient Chinese EV makers that are aggressively expanding globally. At the same time, softening EV demand in the US has forced GM to recalibrate its electrification hopes, balancing multibillion-dollar investments in next-generation battery plants with the ongoing costs of software development. To be sure, for GM to bet its future on unproven software architectures and autonomous technology is a high-stakes gamble: If these complex AI systems falter or if development hits delays, GM risks burning through massive amounts of money while falling further behind agile rivals (like Tesla) that were built as software-first companies from day one. After the event, I sat down with Sterling Anderson, GM’s chief product officer, to talk about the company’s risky new ventures as well as how he plans to steer a century-old Detroit giant through an increasingly fast, software-driven world. This interview has been lightly edited for length and clarity. So this was quite the massive announcement — just lots of disparate pieces. Is there a unifying theme here, would you say? And if so, what do you think it is? That’s it. I think so. Your vehicle is becoming a trusted, intelligent companion, and we’re doing that in a variety of ways. I think I might be one of the only guys who gets as excited as I do about what we’re doing in the development process and making that faster and better. I don’t know how many people are going to come away with that takeaway in quite the spirit that I think it merits, but the level of tests and performance optimality that we can achieve when we can spin design decisions hundreds of times — that previously would have accommodated one — is huge. It’s really huge. I’ve been asked now by two folks I’ve met afterwards, “Hey, what about safety, and does speed come at the cost of safety?” The way we are getting speed is by virtualizing tests in a way that allows us to improve safety. Historically, I would have been able to afford X prototypes that I could send to crash tests, and it took me six months. Now, I kick off thousands of tests in the virtual world that give me feedback much quicker. So I get speed, and I get safety, better coverage, and better understanding. There’s a lot of cake being eaten, too, in this process. “I kick off thousands of tests in the virtual world that give me feedback much quicker.” What kind of AI models are you using? I know you talked about how data is sort of the currency here, so obviously there’s a lot that you’re pulling from your own customers, but there’s a range of products out there. What’s the dividing line between what to look for outside the company versus what to pull from inside? It’s a good question. It depends on what we’re doing with it. In many cases, the transformer-based foundation models enable some of the generative design stuff I was talking about — we have enough data in-house based on decades of design files, analyses, etc., that we can get a pretty well-trained system to express our data well. But we have access to — and anybody has access in many cases to — a lot of data that informs us. The co-training of these models with data that exists beyond GM’s walls tends to make them better. So we do it with all of them. You’ve talked about moving from deterministic code to probabilistic AI that learns from the structure of the world and from the data. How are you specifically embedding these models into the vehicles? How much is run locally versus pulling from the cloud? For any safety-critical functionality, the inference is done onboard because we can’t deal with latency, possible dropouts, or whatever else. Some of these examples you saw from the team earlier today, like the OnStar AI interactions, aren’t safety-critical. You probably noticed, given the latency, that you had some round-tripping happening there because we don’t need to run those all onboard. Now, we’re thinking about distilling them down into onboard models just to deal with the latency, but that’s an on-the-edge user experience element, not a core safety consideration. You talked a little bit during your presentation about how big GM is, which gives you advantages with data, but also a lot of inertia. Back in 2019, GM President Mark Reuss talked about moving toward the “digital brain” of the vehicle, and in 2021 you talked about Ultifi. Have things sped up now? Are you on the glide path at this point? Yeah, and a big enabler of it is that common electrical system, including centralized compute. Historically, I know of very few killers to software speed quite like complexity. The moment a software team has a very large set of deployment targets that don’t share a common baseline is the moment you slow that team down enormously. Rolling this out across our entire portfolio is specifically to enable software speed so we don’t have to continue to support vehicles of various configurations. As that hardware set rolls out, software engineers will suddenly be able to operate like they would if they were in a company with two models in production, which they just haven’t been able to in history. GM is unique in having this huge portfolio of brands with distinct identities. How do you decide what to standardize across every vehicle versus ensuring a Buick remains a Buick and a GMC Sierra remains unique? It’s a combination of things. First, the essence of each brand tends to manifest in aesthetics and interaction models. Aesthetics is something you can effectively skin via design, both in digital interfaces and physical sheet metal. We already do a fairly good job of commonizing the under-the-skin componentry because why wouldn’t we? For a given segment of vehicle, there’s a particular ride quality that tends to be best received by everybody. Our T1-2 platform for our next-generation trucks is common between brands. Where you’ll see us differentiate is in the feature set, the aesthetics, and interaction patterns we effectively skin segment by segment. Sales has dedicated brand channels, but engineering is all one organization. That ensures we don’t ship heterogeneous, suboptimal cars for different brands just because a team that was slightly worse was assigned to one brand or another. I want our engineering team focusing all of their effort on what is the absolute best suspension, powertrain, or drive unit, and then we scale it across the portfolio. Speaking of autonomy, we have autonomous vehicles on the road today — Waymo, Zoox, and Tesla Cybercabs. What are the challenges GM still needs to solve to get to a point where someone can take their eyes off the road comfortably and safely? When you say “eyes off,” you mean autonomous? Autonomously, yes. Hands off, mind off, eyes off. But at the same time, you wouldn’t be recommending people go to sleep in their vehicles yet? We are designing them such that should they fall asleep, it won’t be unsafe, because we don’t have the luxury of ensuring that they’re paying attention. The reason you won’t hear me use terms like “eyes off” is because I hear people use those terms in reference to nonsensical levels of automation. What do you think your mind is doing when your eyes are on your phone? Where’s your mind? Let’s not draw these nonsensical distinctions. If you believe that your driver assistance system is super capable, you are already at risk of complacency — at risk of treating it like it’s an autonomous system. Our philosophy is: If we’re going to design an autonomous system, we’re going to actually design an autonomous system. The path of incrementalism that we’ll take will work for personal ownership, not the one that worked for robotaxis. Instead of regionally specific, speed-limited settings, we’re going to go toward road-type specific settings and expand outward from there. They both converge in the same place — self-driving everywhere at all times — but take different paths to get there. With robotaxis today, we see problems around nuisance situations — like blocking intersections or emergency vehicles. Do you feel personal autonomy, as GM defines it, solves some of that because the driver remains an option to fall back on? The form of the fallback matters. We will not fall back in a safety-critical dynamic situation. It’s just not reasonable to do that. What we can do in a non-safety-critical situation — where the vehicle is stopped and something odd is happening — is hand it back to the driver once we’ve reached a safe place. Having a human in the loop is very useful for that. How do you structure that handoff to avoid driver complacency? We’re not handing back dynamically. This is not an exchange of “you drive, now I’ll drive, oops, I don’t recognize this.” This is not Level 3. In the same way you put a robotaxi in a bounded region, we will put personally owned autonomous vehicles on a particular road. When on that road, we will take the human to their destination and give a graceful countdown — like, “Hey, you’ve got 10 minutes left in this ride.” If they take over preemptively, great. Should they not take over, the system brings the vehicle to a safe state off the off-ramp and says, “I told you 10 minutes ago to take over. We’re stopped, and I’m not going any further.” You have to operate under the assumption that bad actors will try to abuse the system — sitting in the back seat, like we’ve seen with Tesla users. I get far more nervous when misuse happens in the context of a driver-assist system that wasn’t designed to handle every situation. If the vehicle accepts control, our system is designed to deal with more situations that can be thrown at it in that circumstance. So my anxiety level declines dramatically. Features like OnStar AI ask for access to calendars, emails, and personal data. Given past FTC orders regarding GM selling customer data to third-party brokers without permission, how do you ensure customers can trust you with this data? Privacy has to be designed in. With end-to-end encryption, we don’t see your data; you grant permission to an assistant that performs better with context. Data is only available to us at an aggregate, anonymized level. At an individual level, it is encrypted and under your control. When you buy a car, going through dozens of terms and conditions leads to “click, click, click” without reading, which does a disservice to trust. We have dramatically simplified these authorizations down to a couple of clear choices that explain why the car needs data — like reading your calendar for smart routing. You can revoke permission, delete your data, or port it out at any time. You alluded to trust, which is foundational. Decisions made in the past by people who no longer work for this company have an overhang, but that’s not the company we are today. We earn that trust back by designing the architecture so that even if someone made a bad decision internally, the system technically wouldn’t allow them to access raw user data. And the way we do that is by designing it. Look, even if we had someone stupid work for us again and do something like this, they couldn’t. They wouldn’t be able to access it. “Look, even if we had someone stupid work for us again and do something like this, they couldn’t. They wouldn’t be able to access it.” AI is cutting vehicle development times, but we’re also seeing hype around factory automation and humanoid robots from companies like Tesla and Rivian. Is GM heading in that direction? If you want to be perceived as a robotics player these days, it seems you have to have a humanoid robot that does backflips. It’s weird. Advocacy for humanoids often comes from people who historically advocate for first-principles engineering, but humanoids are not born from first principles. They put form over function. The logic assumes the human form factor is optimal for a factory floor, but this form factor evolved to run away from lions on the African savanna! Production environments weren’t built for human forms any more than roads built for horses meant cars should have four legs. The most valuable real estate in a factory is the floor. Having bumbling humanoid robots clamoring in and out of vehicles is not where you start. You start with form factors that look alien — like an octopus hanging from a gantry that reaches into a moving vehicle line. We’re ignoring the “religion” of humanoids and focusing on underlying AI intelligence that can operate various specialized robot form factors for specific tasks. Is GM building these robotics systems itself? We already are. We actively work with heavy machinery suppliers like Fanuc, but we are actively developing many of our own solutions. People are excited about cars getting smarter, but they also want them to be cheaper. How do these announcements lead to more affordable options? LMR (lithium manganese rich) battery chemistry is a huge part of this. It delivers a range close to high-nickel packs at a cost closer to LFP. On a per-range basis, a customer will be able to buy a fourth-gen GM EV for less money than a competitor’s profitable model without relying on subsidies. Additionally, commonizing our electrical architecture reduces wiring length and vehicle mass dramatically, making cars inherently more affordable. Virtualizing development cuts prototype costs, allowing vehicle business cases to clear at lower retail prices. This applies to ICE vehicles and hybrids as well, not just EVs. Will this intelligence lead to higher subscriptions and paywalls? Capability advances inevitably over time without raising financial costs — the device in your lap does far more than computers years ago for less money. Regarding subscriptions: I’m here to build great products. If customers like paying for services like a lease, let them; if not, don’t force them. “The most valuable real estate in a factory is the floor. Having bumbling humanoid robots clamoring in and out of vehicles is not where you start.” [GM spokesperson Chaiti Sen: For immediate features like OnStar AI, it will be included in our basic package. We aren’t going to paywall core baseline experiences or hand customers a bare “tin shell.”] For your sensor suite, you’re using cameras, radar, and lidar. Do you see that remaining in perpetuity, or could you eventually scale back to a vision-only approach like Tesla? If you scale back to vision-only, physics suggests you’ll be in trouble in certain situations. Radar is so cheap that dropping it doesn’t make sense — dropping it is a weird religious stance that ignores physics. Lidar operates on a similar wavelength to visible light and helps co-train camera systems to get better faster. Cameras can’t see through dense fog or measure instantaneous velocity like radar can. Having a multisensor suite provides obvious safety advantages that physics simply demands. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. Data center backlash is uniting America
2026-10-27

Data center backlash is uniting America

The Verge | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Some midterm races come down to ‘people who hate data centers’ versus ‘people who hate them even more.’﻿ Some midterm races come down to ‘people who hate data centers’ versus ‘people who hate them even more.’﻿ If there’s one issue that seems to activate all kinds of voters in 2026, it’s data centers. The rapid buildout of these massive server storage facilities — and the infrastructure construction and expenses that come with them — has sparked local opposition in communities across the country and become a major issue in many congressional and gubernatorial campaigns. At the same time, tech money has been pouring into many of these races. Leading the Future, a super PAC backed by venture capitalist Marc Andreessen and OpenAI President Greg Brockman, has already spent $45 million in a year. And Public First Action, an Anthropic-backed group that says it supports AI safeguards, says it’s raised $80 million. Both have funded Democratic and Republican-aligned groups. Other super PACs see an opportunity to push for stricter AI policies, like the recently created Tomorrow Together, which launched $4 million in ads for battleground House races, with messages about data center development and AI safety. All sorts of candidates appear to be distancing themselves from data centers and taking steps to assuage voters’ concerns about unconstrained buildouts in their backyards. How far they’re willing to go — including by backing an outright moratorium on development — varies. While many Democratic candidates seem likelier to back a pause, that’s not always the case. And though some Republicans have leaned on party messaging about putting development in local control, rather than supporting a ban, others say a pause is exactly what the moment calls for. Deep red Texas has the second-highest number of planned data center projects in the country after Virginia, and across multiple races, data centers have emerged as a key wedge issue. Democratic and Republican candidates are all trying to look tough on data centers but emphasize different messages in their plans. For example, in the Senate race, Democratic candidate and State Rep. James Talarico includes references to environmental impact and sharing corporate profit with the public, while Republican candidate and Texas Attorney General Ken Paxton discusses the need to keep Chinese technology out of US data centers. Both, however, want data centers to bear their own energy infrastructure-related costs and want to do away with certain tax exemptions for them. In July, Talarico unveiled his “Hold Data Centers Accountable Plan,” which would end state sales tax incentives, give communities more of a say in data center development, require a closed-loop water system in new data centers to limit consumption, and create federal standards to protect the energy grid and environment. Talarico has also proposed an “AI Dividend” for Americans that would be funded by a tax on “AI-attributable profits.” For his part, Paxton’s “Texas First Data Center Plan,” unveiled in August, would support Gov. Greg Abbott’s state-level policies, including repealing sales tax exemptions for data centers, requiring them to pay for electricity they use and add to state capacity, and preventing AI data centers from building in rural parts of the state. On the federal level, Paxton says he would co-sponsor an existing bill making it easier for data centers to connect to off-grid power systems, introduce new legislation prohibiting the use of Chinese technology to power US data centers or infrastructure, and hold data center companies criminally liable if the facility “empowers AI chatbots that undermine children’s safety.” Talarico has also proposed an “AI Dividend” for Americans that would be funded by a tax on “AI-attributable profits” In the race for the governor’s office, Democratic challenger and State Rep. Gina Hinojosa has accused Abbott, the Republican incumbent, of “flip-flopping” on data centers. Abbott’s office put out a press release last year celebrating a $40 billion Google investment that would include three new data center campuses, but in August 2026, with campaign season heating up, he announced an audit that could slow new facilities connecting to the state energy grid. Hinojosa, who has recently called for a statewide moratorium on data center development, pounced on the perceived inconsistency. “Greg Abbott has zero credibility here. Nobody believes that the arsonist is about to put out the fire,” she said in a social media video. The outcome of these races remains highly uncertain. Cook Political Report changed its rating for the governor’s race earlier this month from a likely Republican win to one that leans Republican. It says the open field for the Senate race is a toss-up. In Florida, there’s a clash over whether large-scale data centers can be reined in with the right policies or if they should be outright barred in the state. Democratic candidate and former congressman David Jolly has pledged to install a one-year ban on hyperscale data centers on his first day in office, and hasn’t ruled out a permanent extension. In one ad, Jolly puts data centers in the same breath as crime in a list of things he doesn’t want in Florida. His Republican opponent, Congressman Byron Donalds, has previously supported data center development in the state and said Florida is “wide open” to build without destroying the state’s beauty. He’s more recently changed his tune though, and while he still doesn’t want to outright ban data centers, he’s tried to assure voters that he’d support policies protecting them from associated risks like higher energy costs. In July, Donalds introduced the Protecting Ratepayers Act, which would require data center developers to fund the energy and water needed to run their operations. He’s also supported a state law with some similar requirements and environmental guardrails. “In my Florida, if you don’t protect our pocketbooks and our people, you won’t build data centers, period,” he assures voters in one ad. Energy experts have warned, however, that Donalds’ pledge to lean on nuclear and natural gas could actually raise costs for consumers. Despite these efforts, he’s been branded as “Data Center Donalds” by opponents, and polling suggests he may have to fight for conservative voters who also strongly oppose data center development. An October NBC6/Telemundo Station Group poll found that 45 percent of likely voter respondents opposed data centers in Florida, with 38 percent in support and 17 percent undecided. A higher percentage opposed data centers in their own communities, at 59 percent. Still, the poll found Donalds has a lead of 48 percent to Jolly’s 41 percent, with a margin of error of plus or minus 4 percent. Ten percent of respondents were still undecided. In one of the country’s relatively few purple states, both candidates for governor have accused each other of changing their rhetoric on data centers just to get votes. Democratic Gov. Josh Shapiro and Republican challenger and state Treasurer Stacy Garrity each appear to have hardened their stances amid the widespread backlash to data center development, even though they previously touted opportunity in such projects. Shapiro proudly announced a $20 billion investment from Amazon last year that would bring new data centers to the state, and credited Pennsylvania’s new fast track permitting program in helping lure the investment. But this year, Shapiro has tempered his enthusiasm, signing an executive order in August prohibiting nondisclosure agreements for data center developers and taking AI data center proposals off of the fast track. Shapiro has said his views evolved based on community feedback. But Garrity, too, has seemed to turn against data centers as the election has drawn closer. In a radio interview last year, she said rural parts of the state would be “perfect places to put data centers,” and they’d be largely welcomed based on the economic opportunity they’d bring. She also praised the Amazon investment Shapiro secured at the time, though it’s now a target of some of her attack ads. This year, she has said data center developers must engage the community rather than “jam it down their throats,” and she supports a pause on development lasting no more than three years, according to Spotlight PA. An early October Quinnipiac University poll of likely Pennsylvania voters found 64 percent believed AI data centers in the state would do more harm than good, with a plus or minus 3.9 percent margin of error. But a far larger portion of Democratic respondents were wary of them, with 80 percent saying they’d do more harm than good. That suggests it may be even more essential for Shapiro to shore up his position for his base, though the poll had him leading 59 percent to Garrity’s 34 percent. In the battle for Ohio’s Senate seat, data centers are both “the new ingredient (and the new problem),” according to a leaked National Republican Senatorial Committee (NRSC) memo. Republican incumbent Jon Husted and Democratic candidate Sherrod Brown, who’s fighting to win back his former seat, “are in a dead heat,” the NRSC warned in August. And data centers, the group added, “are the anchor hanging around Husted’s neck.” As the NRSC identified, Brown has made his opposition to data centers a central tenet of his campaign, labeling Husted “the face of data centers” in the state. Brown has hammered Husted over his past support for data centers as lieutenant governor, where he helped install tax breaks. Husted has said he believes local communities should be able to decide whether to bring in data centers, and that some may find the revenue useful. “If he loses and data centers get the blame, politicians across the country will take notice” At least as of August, the NRSC still believed voters favored that approach over sweeping bans but acknowledged that current perception is negative. The group warned that losing the Ohio Senate seat could endanger future support for data centers. “If he loses and data centers get the blame, politicians across the country will take notice — and they will not go near the next one,” the memo says. Husted introduced the Ratepayer Protection Act, which would require large load customers like data centers to cover their own energy costs and the infrastructure upgrades needed to accommodate them. The bill passed the House and was seen as a potential life raft for Husted, but narrowly failed to pass the Senate, even with a handful of Democrats voting in favor. The Cook Political Report rates the race as a toss-up, and a RealClear Polling average of recent surveys shows Brown with a slight edge. Data centers are a hot topic across two key races in Michigan with razor-thin margins. The state is handling dozens of new data center proposals, and both candidates for Michigan’s open Senate seat support a moratorium. Republican former Rep. Mike Rogers supports a one-year pause but opposes a federal ban, while Democratic candidate and former public health official Abdul El-Sayed says there should be “no more data centers until we can do it safely and with federal protections.” Rogers’ moratorium support came days after The Lever reported on his investments that reportedly stand to gain from the buildout. El-Sayed put his stake in the ground on data centers relatively early, compared to other Democrats across the country. In February, Politico wrote that “underdogs” like El-Sayed were centering data centers in their primary campaigns, raising what’s now a common topic in campaign ads. El-Sayed is also now locked in what the Cook Political Report rates as a dead heat with Rogers, in a race that could determine the balance of power in Congress. Rogers and El-Sayed are competing for a seat currently held by Democrat Gary Peters, who isn’t seeking another term. In another toss-up race, Democratic organizer William Lawrence is challenging Republican incumbent Tom Barrett for his House seat. Lawrence called for a year-long federal moratorium on new data center construction during his primary campaign, and when he won the nomination, his supporters reportedly chanted, “data centers lost.” In the general election, data centers have continued to be a central theme. Barrett introduced two bills this summer that would strengthen local decision-making on data center development and bar federal lawmakers from signing non-disclosure agreements about data centers. Lawrence’s data center plan includes environmental standards, worker protections, and “zoning buffers” for data center development. Wisconsin is another state where both gubernatorial candidates have backed away from previous support for data centers, but neither has gone as far as to call for a moratorium. Democratic nominee and Milwaukee County Executive David Crowley, for example, set himself apart from his progressive primary opponent Francesca Hong by declining to oppose a development moratorium that she supported. Rep. Tom Tiffany, the Republican nominee, similarly opposes a moratorium in favor of data center guardrails, and voted for a bill allowing faster permitting for data center projects. But Tiffany has still tried to label his opponent as “data center David Crowley,” pointing to past comments he made saying the state shouldn’t “automatically roll back all data center tax incentives.” More recently, Crowley has said he would sign a bill repealing tax subsidies for the facilities. Cook Political Report says the race is a dead heat. Nevada’s Democratic Party created an entire website branding Republican Gov. Joe Lombardo “data center Joe,” highlighting his past supportive comments about data centers and money his campaign took from AI-connected donors. Lombardo, locked in a tight race to keep his seat, has fought the accusations. He introduced an executive order last month that ties tax incentives to developers covering power costs and meeting water use standards, empowering the state to claw back money from those who violate it. But Democratic challenger and attorney general Aaron Ford has said he’d go further, pausing tax breaks entirely for new data centers. At the same time, Ford has been criticized for championing a 2015 bill enshrining data center tax breaks. Ford said his thinking has evolved with new information, and that “the data centers of 11 years ago are not the data centers of 2026.” The issue is particularly salient in a state that’s experiencing significant water issues. The Cook Political Report says the race is a toss-up. Swing state Georgia has been called “one of the nation’s most attractive markets for AI, hyperscale cloud and enterprise data center development,” and its gubernatorial candidates — Democratic nominee and former Atlanta Mayor Keisha Lance Bottoms and Republican nominee and healthcare executive Rick Jackson — have sparred over each other’s data center records. Bottoms supports a pause on development to evaluate environmental and cost effects, while Jackson says it should be up to local communities to decide whether to bring a data center in. At a recent debate in the toss-up race, Bottoms said her opponent “cannot be trusted” on the topic based on his investment in a Texas data center. Jackson shot back that Bottoms approved and gave tax breaks to multiple data centers as mayor, which Bottoms denied, saying she only approved one non-hyperscale data center that wasn’t near a residential community. While Bottoms led the Invest Atlanta board in 2018, The Atlanta Journal-Constitution reported, the group approved up to $440 million in bonds for a mixed-use project at Georgia Tech that included a data center. This was not a “direct financing mechanism” but did help the project get tax abatement, the AJC wrote, and its data center was far smaller than those mainly at issue today. In dry Arizona where concerns about water are a top issue for many voters, the most interesting conflict is within parties, not across them. Democratic Gov. Katie Hobbs and Attorney General Kris Mayes are both locked in close races with their respective Republican challengers, Rep. Andy Biggs and state legislator Warren Petersen. But the two Democrats are split over how to regulate data centers: Mayes supports a statewide pause on development, while Hobbs opposes one. In dry Arizona, the most interesting conflict is within parties, not across them Mayes says the state should “take a beat” to evaluate water and energy needs, a move Petersen has criticized in a rare outright defense of data centers, saying, “If you like your tech and your phone and your computer and the stuff you can use that for, you probably think data centers are important.” Hobbs, meanwhile, says data centers are here to stay, and the state should instead figure out how to best make them work for communities. That puts her significantly closer to her Republican opponent, who similarly opposes a moratorium. Iowa’s open governor’s seat is another close race where data centers are taking center stage. The state economic development authority says Iowa’s wind energy and low construction and business costs have attracted developers there, but their growth has also brought concerns to local communities. Democratic nominee and state auditor Rob Sand has not supported a blanket moratorium but says he would end tax breaks for data centers, make them cover their own costs, and keep NDAs from shielding deals from communities. Republican nominee and businessman Zach Lahn called for a moratorium on new development and eliminating tax incentives for them. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. OpenAI announces three big science discoveries and a new way to work with academics
2026-10-26

OpenAI announces three big science discoveries and a new way to work with academics

The Verge | 2026-10-26

Visit Original Website
This time OpenAI says it’s taking new advice from scientists on how to release the results. This time OpenAI says it’s taking new advice from scientists on how to release the results. Less than a month after its mass release of hundreds of manuscripts that it said contained solutions to long-standing mathematics questions, OpenAI is back with three more “major” discoveries in math, computer science, and physics. What’s different this time is the choice to have external experts that OpenAI describes as “shepherds” guiding the results in computer science and physics, while for the math problem it says that they advised it to release the AI output for researchers to work through their own explanations. Local smoothing for the Euclidean wave equation. The proof uses an auxiliary partial differential equation to establish Sogge’s local smoothing for waves in every spatial dimension at least two. It also yields the Kakeya maximal and dimension conjectures and Stein’s spherical restriction estimates throughout the interior of the conjectured range. We are releasing the model output directly, as we did in our October 6 release. The Exponential Time Hypothesis is False. The paper gives a deterministic algorithm that exactly counts satisfying assignments of fixed-width CNF formulas in subexponential time and polynomial space. The case of 3-SAT refutes the Exponential Time Hypothesis. An independent working group of computer scientists led by Russell Impagliazzo, Ramamohan Paturi, and Ryan Williams will examine and explain the proof. At the group’s request, we are making the materials public immediately so others can examine them as well. This approach could have its own issues, the company admits, and the AGMAI panel of math advisers OpenAI put together just said yesterday that “we recommend that AI companies do not adopt” the practices of designating certain people to receive the output first, citing unequal access, borrowed reputation, and biased selection as a few of the issues. Instead, they recommended that in the future, AI labs release output on scholarly repositories, with Lean-formalized proofs, info on how the problems were chosen, funding for people working on understanding the results, and other information. The physics information has not been released in full, but is a “mathematically rigorous existence proof of certain interacting quantum field theories containing gauge fields and fermions in four dimensions, including Quantum Chromodynamics (QCD) and a unified completion of the Standard Model of particle physics.” According to OpenAI, “David Gross will lead a group including Michael Douglas, Arthur Jaffe, Igor Klebanov, and Nikita Nekrasov that will shepherd this work.” The computer science collaboration is said to prove that the Exponential Time Hypothesis (ETH), which has been widely believed to be true, is actually false. OpenAI says verification of the result will be guided by a group of three experts that includes UC San Diego’s Russell Impagliazzo and Ramamohan Paturi, who were part of the group that originally proposed it in 1999, as well as MIT professor Ryan Williams. The group decided to release the AI output write-up and Lean proof on GitHub today. They also released an open letter to the community asking for input on how to examine the claim, and about setting norms for dealing with AI-generated breakthroughs: We would welcome discussion of three broader questions: What expectations should govern early access to AI-generated results, disclosure of conflicts of interest, and the use of unpublished material for subsequent research? How should credit recognize both new discoveries and the work of checking, fixing, simplifying, explaining, and extending them? How can we support students and researchers as methods and research directions change, while preserving the understanding and judgment needed to question results and develop new ideas? There’s similar documentation for the math breakthrough, where its model “produced a proof of the local smoothing conjecture in every dimension and therefore also resolve the Kakeya conjecture and Stein’s restriction conjecture in all dimensions.” Just a few months ago, NYU professor Hong Wang was awarded a prestigious Fields Medal by the International Mathematical Union for resolving the Kakeya conjecture in three dimensions. Samsung’s Switch 2-ready MicroSD Express card is on sale for less than $50

Visit Original Website
Samsung’s discounted memory cards can store dozens of games, depending on their size. Samsung’s discounted memory cards can store dozens of games, depending on their size. If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement. Running out of space on your Nintendo Switch 2? Thankfully, Samsung’s P9 MicroSD Express card is discounted at multiple retailers, with the 256GB version going for $47.99 (Amazon, B&H Photo) and the 512GB version $99 (Amazon, B&H Photo). The smaller card typically sells for around $80, and the larger option usually sells between $150 and $200, though both are higher than their original launch prices, likely because of the ongoing memory shortage. The P9 is great if you need more storage for your Nintendo Switch 2. The microSD Express card offers sequential read speeds of up to 800MB/s, but only gadgets with a microSD Express card slot can take full advantage of the highest speeds. That includes certain cameras, tablets, and drones with a microSD USH-I slot. First-party Switch 2 games generally run between 10 and 30 gigabytes each — the upcoming The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time remake is 27GB, for example — while third-party games can take up even more space (Cyberpunk 2077 is 60GB). Until recently, the Switch 2 was one of the few devices that could take advantage of the faster MicroSD Express standard, but this year’s Asus ROG Ally X20 also added support for MicroSD Express. While expensive, the latter is an impressive gaming handheld that runs Windows 11, giving it a versatility that’s hard to find elsewhere. Industrial Intelligence: The next growth opportunity for manufacturing
2026-10-27

Industrial Intelligence: The next growth opportunity for manufacturing

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Every manufacturer has its own industrial intelligence. Some is documented, and some resides in the knowledge of experienced workers. Consider an experienced operator preparing to restart a line after a changeover. The machine settings matter, but so does knowing how a particular material behaves, what happened during the previous shift, and when an apparently minor variation deserves a closer look. A procedure can describe the sequence, but successful execution depends on the judgment that comes from experience and the organization’s accumulated know-how. That combination is industrial intelligence: a manufacturer’s accumulated know-how, operating practices, production data, and understanding of its own business. The opportunity now is to make more of that intelligence available wherever decisions are made, then connect it to the physical world of manufacturing. The strategic destination is not more agents, but rather to turn the existing business intelligence into an engine for profitable growth. Expertise has limited reach when it stays with one person, inside one system, or within one plant. Leaders now must determine how to extend that reach without stripping away the context that makes the knowledge useful. For example, an engineer evaluating a process change needs more than a production record. They need to understand the circumstances behind it: the equipment configuration, the quality requirements, and the trade-offs an operator made. Connecting those elements can help people apply experience beyond the place where it originated. When individual knowledge becomes widely accessible, innovation can spread to the maintenance workshop, the planning office, the engineering team, and the production floor. More people can contribute because more people can work with what the organization knows. But access to knowledge is only part of the opportunity. Manufacturing ultimately is physical, and it depends on materials, machines, and products. Physical AI describes systems that can perceive conditions, reason about them, and take action within the physical world. By connecting machines and processes, manufacturers get a clearer view of what is happening on the factory floor. Agents can then reason on top of that information to identify patterns, predict problems, and determine the best course of action. Physical AI brings business intelligence into the physical process, allowing machines and systems to respond to changing conditions. Consider a robotic-handling operation encountering variation in the parts arriving at its station. Vision can help the system assess a part’s position. A model informed by the material and task can help determine an appropriate movement. Connected controls can then execute an adjustment within validated limits. The value is not merely seeing the variation; it is responding appropriately. The same principle applies to quality operations. When Toyota Industries connected previously fragmented paint shop data, engineering and operations teams were given the context needed to understand defect drivers. The company reports that analysis cycles fell from five days to under four hours, while its pilot reduced relevant paint defects by about 25%. The breakthrough was not simply identifying another signal; it was helping people understand why defects were occurring and respond before the outcome was locked in. This is the type of situation where industrial intelligence has become essential. Data may reveal what is happening, but industrial intelligence provides the context for what should happen, which constraints cannot be breached, and when uncertainty requires human judgment. Simulation offers a way to examine proposed actions before applying them to equipment. Manufacturers can explore how a change might affect a process in a digital environment. That does not remove the need for physical validation, but it creates room to learn without treating the production line as the first experiment. Responsible deployment, therefore, begins with operational discipline. Leaders need to establish clear authority or what systems may change, reliable connections to equipment, defined safety boundaries, and a record of decisions and actions. People must be able to recognize uncertainty, intervene, and bring equipment to a safe state. Trust rests on those practical arrangements, not on confidence in a model alone. Manufacturing has long combined human ingenuity with increasingly capable tools. Physical AI extends that relationship. Intelligent equipment can take on suitable repetitive or physically demanding tasks, while agent-assisted operations can help people navigate information-intensive decisions and changing conditions. The aim is not to make machines smarter; it is to see what people can build when knowledge can travel, intelligence can act, and ambition has more room to become reality. Rockwell Automation illustrates the point. At its Singapore plant, technicians use a maintenance copilot that combines manufacturing data, machine manuals, and knowledge captured from experienced workers. This system has reduced the time required for new workers to learn how to troubleshoot hundreds of machines from nine months to three. An engineer could explore more process alternatives before committing to a change. An operator could help improve the system’s response rather than repeatedly managing the same exception. This type of progress requires investment in equipment but also in people: training, time to learn, and playing a meaningful role in shaping how systems work. Machines become more capable so people can become more capable. Industrial intelligence connects what a manufacturer knows: the experience of its people, the signals from its operations, and the lessons accumulated over time. Physical AI gives that intelligence a way to act in the physical world, turning knowledge into decisions and decisions into action. The next challenge for industry is not simply to produce more, but to grow without adding complexity at the same rate. Intelligence can help manufacturers turn experience into scalable capability—improving productivity, accelerating innovation, expanding capacity, and enabling people to solve problems that were previously difficult to tackle. The next frontier of industry is not smarter machines or more automated factories; it is businesses that can continuously learn, adapt, and act. The opportunity is to turn the intelligence embedded in every operation into a compounding advantage for growth. Watch the video series to learn how industrial customers are unlocking intelligence across the value chain, and join Microsoft at Microsoft Ignite to see what’s next for Industrial Intelligence. How solid-state transformers could help power the AI boom
2026-10-27

How solid-state transformers could help power the AI boom

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Transformers are a crucial but nearly invisible part of our grid. The devices help efficiently move power across long distances, but they’re complicated and in short supply. And given that their basic design hasn’t changed much for about a century, they could use an upgrade. As the world races to meet rising electricity demand from EVs, data centers, and more, researchers and companies are looking to modernize transformers. New, solid-state versions could help relieve some of the supply concerns and make sure the grid can handle the growing pressure. Conventional transformers generally rely on huge amounts of specialized steel alloys and miles of copper wire to do their work of transferring electricity between circuits. Solid-state models, in comparison, combine magnetic components with high-power semiconductors, making them more compact and allowing for more control over the flow of electricity. These devices are still in development, and can’t replace all traditional transformers today, especially the largest ones that work at the high voltages necessary for long-distance transmission. But a new generation of transformer technology could be a crucial part of our future grid. Moving electricity through the grid isn’t perfectly efficient: As electricity flows through power lines, some gets lost as heat. How much varies—the higher the voltage, the lower the current, and the less electricity gets lost. So a key function for the grid is stepping voltage up and down. Consider, for example, your local solar plant. For that electricity to efficiently travel to your home hundreds of miles away, it needs to reach a higher voltage than what’s generated by the solar plant. Enter transformers. In its path from the power plant to your outlets, electricity might go through one transformer at the solar farm and another at a substation nearby, stepping up the voltage twice to reach a high enough level to travel on high-voltage transmission lines. Solar panels also produce direct current (DC) electricity, which needs to be converted to alternating current (AC) via an inverter before it flows into a transformer. Closer to its destination, the voltage will be stepped down again, probably at least twice: once at another substation and again at a smaller transformer in your neighborhood. If you’re in the US, electricity from a solar plant might be converted to AC by an inverter at around 480 volts, get stepped up to 115,000 volts for the transmission lines, and finally get stepped back down to 120 volts and 240 volts for your home. Conventional transformers do this work using what essentially amount to loops of wire. The core of a transformer is typically made of a special type of steel called grain-oriented electrical steel. Coils of copper wire sit on either end of that core. The number of loops in each coil determines the voltage on either end of the transformer: If there are more loops on the secondary coil than the primary, the voltage steps up, and if there are fewer, it steps down. When incoming electricity passes through loops on the upstream end of the transformer, that forms a magnetic field in the core. That field induces a voltage in the loops on the other side of the device, which is what allows the energy to continue flowing at a new voltage. This process is called magnetic induction. Transformers form the backbone of electricity transmission and distribution, says Srdjan Lukic, a professor of electrical and computer engineering at North Carolina State University. In addition to doing the job of stepping up and down voltage, transformers also often provide what’s called galvanic isolation, meaning there’s no direct electrical path flowing through them. That can help limit disturbances from moving through the grid, improving safety. The problem is that today’s transformers are big and bulky, and they’re also difficult and expensive to replace, Lukic says. Solid-state transformers essentially add so-called power electronics—semiconductors that convert and control electricity to the typical blueprint of today’s transformers. While a conventional transformer operates at the frequency of the part of the grid it’s connected to, a solid-state transformer actually controls the frequency of the current. Typically, a solid-state transformer first converts incoming AC power from the grid to DC power, and then to a higher-frequency AC power using switches made of a semiconductor like silicon carbide. From there, a magnetic component changes the voltage. Because the advanced semiconductors allow transformers to exert so much control over the frequency and stability of the current, the magnetic core can be much smaller than in a conventional transformer. Some come in at roughly one-tenth the size but have the same capacity, Lukic says. Finally, additional power electronics convert the electricity from high-frequency AC to its required form—generally back to DC power and finally to lower-frequency AC. Today, stepping voltage up and down is many transformers’ only job. But solid-state transformers can do much more. They absorb the jobs of devices like rectifiers and inverters (which convert AC to DC power and vice versa), shrinking the total footprint of the electrical equipment and potentially cutting the amount of power lost in the system. In August, the New York Power Authority and the Electric Power Research Institute (EPRI) tested a one-megawatt solid-state transformer under realistic utility conditions at EPRI’s test site. The device was built in Lukic’s lab, and it was the first such test for a transformer of that capacity, he says. The prototype was roughly 98% efficient—meaning it lost only 2% of the energy fed in. An equivalent conventional transformer and rectifier, by comparison, would be about 96% efficient together, Lukic says. A difference of 2% might seem small, but it is significant, since it means the solid-state transformer could cut power losses from conventional equipment in half, Lukic says. One major goal of the project was to help utilities gain more confidence in these new components, Lukic says. That’s crucial, since these devices could be used on the power grid in the future. In the near term, solid-state transformers could be used within data centers to provide electricity for server racks. The prototype is still a relatively low-voltage system, operating at about 15,000 volts. Industrial facilities like larger data centers often require higher-voltage connections, and transmission lines typically operate at hundreds of thousands of volts. Adapting solid-state transformers to these higher voltages will require further engineering development. But the test was a successful demonstration that solid-state transformers can work under real conditions, Lukic says. Companies developing solid-state transformers are largely focused on data centers right now, Lukic says. There’s an influx of money to support developing the new technology, and the voltage requirements aren’t as high as they will be for larger transformers that will be needed for long-distance transmission. In February, Heron Power raised $140 million to commercialize its solid-state transformer, which the company calls the Heron Link. The technology could be used in data centers, solar power arrays, and grid batteries, says Drew Baglino, the founder and CEO. The company is starting to test its technology at larger scales now, Baglino says. Ten megawatt-hours’ worth of batteries at Heron’s headquarters in Scotts Valley, California, will soon undergo testing with Heron transformers. The company is also planning a pilot with a nearby solar farm, Baglino says. One challenge for solid-state transformers is that their power electronics have more failure modes than conventional transformers. In a traditional device, a failure means that the entire device will typically be taken offline, resulting in power interruptions. But Heron designs with that in mind, Baglino says: The company uses a modular design, with many small devices that work together. If one fails, the rest of the system keeps working until the affected bit can be replaced, which doesn’t require shutting the whole thing down. Heron Power is currently building a $100 million factory in California to make advanced transformers. The facility should begin production in 2027 and reach its full production rate of 40 gigawatts’ worth of transformers per year in 2029. In recent years, companies that make grid equipment have put $1.7 billion into the US supply chain for power transformers, which are used in high-voltage transmission, says Benjamin Boucher, a supply chain analyst at Wood Mackenzie. There’s been an additional $1.3 billion invested for distribution transformers, the smaller devices that help transform power for homes and businesses, he adds. But even this investment isn’t keeping pace with the rapid growth of the grid. Wait times for large conventional transformers—the kind needed to connect new power plants, long-distance transmission, and high-demand sites like data centers—are stretching as long as five years. “Transformers have become one of the biggest bottlenecks for utilities, generation developers, and even data centers,” Boucher says. The big challenge is that it’s difficult to get new transformer manufacturing up and running, because it’s a quite technical process that requires a lot of specialized labor. So even facilities announced years ago are still years from opening, Boucher says. Solid-state transformers could help ease some of that pressure. But high-voltage devices needed for power plant connections and transmission lines are the holy grail for solid-state transformers, Lukic says. Existing solid-state technology can’t be used for those applications yet, so the industry will need to develop new materials and designs to support high voltages and handle electricity safely in a compact device. “We’re facing a big risk related to grid infrastructure,” Boucher says. “Without the transformers, you don’t really have an electrical grid.” Huge grid-scale batteries are thriving, but smaller residential systems have lagged. Startup Switch Bioworks hopes to use microbes to reduce demand for chemical fertilizer. Could new fertilizers help combat high prices? Discover special offers, top stories, upcoming events, and more. Will the real Anthropic please stand up
2026-10-26

Will the real Anthropic please stand up

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-26

Visit Original Website
This story originally appeared in The Algorithm, our weekly newsletter on AI. To get stories like this in your inbox first, sign up here. After weeks of frenzied speculation, Anthropic has filed its IPO prospectus. The 335-page document (100 pages or so are focused on risks) lays out the company’s unique view of the technology it is building and the ways it believes it will change the world. It also warns that there’s a fair chance that its tech could “erode trust, harm society and destabilize the geopolitical order” and, if things don’t go to plan, “pose catastrophic or existential risks to humanity.” “We encourage you, as a prospective owner of our stock, not to think of your purchase in purely transactional terms,” the company writes. “We hope you will see it instead as an opportunity to be part of something greater. The advent of machine intelligence is one of the seminal moments in human history.” This kind of public messaging is a master class in narrative capture. But Anthropic has been honing its storytelling skills for years. Earlier this month, Jack Clark, the company’s cofounder and head of policy, gave a speech at the IMF meeting in Bangkok in which he announced to the assembled economists, bankers, and lawmakers that the singularity is already here. Introduced by the science fiction writer and futurist Vernor Vinge around 40 years ago (and long debated on the nerdier fringes of the internet), the singularity is supposed to be the point at which technology gets so good that it outstrips the abilities of its makers. Humans are left in the dust, the idea goes, no longer able to compete with—or even understand—the fruits of their former creation. And this fictional world that many of us have imagined since we were kids? Apparently we’re already living in it: “I believe that this year we have gained convincing evidence that it has now begun,” Clark told his audience. The company tells a vivid (and in many ways scary) story about a future it portrays as inevitable but one that has not yet happened, often co-opted from well-worn science fiction memes. It presents its technology as mysterious and dangerous, and insists that it is the only company trustworthy enough to tame it. (What investor wouldn’t want in on that?) If you skim through the long sections about risks in the IPO prospectus, it’s clear this is no ordinary offering and no ordinary company. The way Anthropic tells it, business success depends not only on the firm’s ability to translate its technology into profitable products but also to save the world from what it has made. Take another example. Last year, Chris Olah, another Anthropic cofounder, was invited to address the Vatican, where he told Pope Leo XIV and other religious scholars and officials what his company was learning about its technology. “I will be honest: We keep finding things that are mysterious, even unsettling,” he said. “We find evidence of introspection. We find internal states that functionally mirror joy, satisfaction, fear, grief, and unease.” Never mind that many researchers would scoff at such an overt suggestion that Anthropic’s software has humanlike inner experience. This view of its technology makes its way into the prospectus as one more risk to its business. Anthropic states that: “In addition, given our focus on developing frontier AI, and the way we approach model sentience and moral status, we may face heightened scrutiny or pressure in connection with these debates and could elect to adopt policies, safeguards, or limitations regarding model development, deployment, or modification that go beyond existing legal requirements, which could constrain our operations, increase costs, or limit the commercial potential of our models, platform, and applications.” In other words, if Claude wakes up, it could be a problem. I’m not saying they’re necessarily wrong about any of this (although it’s pretty wild to read about machine sentience in an IPO prospectus.) But in a world where nobody knows what’s coming, companies like Anthropic get to fill the vacuum. When so little seems certain, what it says is snatched up and clung to like pieces of a sinking ship. It’s worth taking a moment to ask: What is Anthropic? It might sound like a starter question on a family quiz show, but it’s far harder to answer than you might think. The question has been on my mind since reading an exchange on X last week kicked off by Nathan Calvin, who is chief strategy officer at Encode AI, an organization based in Washington, DC, that advocates for regulation. Calvin was unhappy about the legislation that Anthropic proposed in a draft bill it circulated in secret to US lawmakers (which was then leaked). Anthropic wants to be seen as the good guy, Calvin said. But why should we trust it? “What is Anthropic?” he asked. Here’s one answer: Anthropic is a company whose IPO values it at more than $1.5 trillion, netting its cofounders, including Clark and Olah, vast wealth. But Anthropic has always insisted it is more than that. As Calvin noted: Anthropic might instead be considered an AI safety organization focused on developing powerful technology that is reliable and (almost) risk-free. Or it might be considered a group of people hell-bent on creating a technology with superhuman capabilities before anyone else—maybe because they believe they’re the most responsible people to do so, or maybe because of their egos. In fact, Anthropic is all of these things. As CEO Dario Amodei put it in a reply to Calvin’s post: “Our strategy has always been what I’ve called the ‘race to the top’—by showing how it’s possible to build the technology responsibly, we can raise the bar for the entire industry. The forces that drive the race to the top are hugely more effective if the company practicing them is at the frontier; that’s one lesson I’ve learned repeatedly. And looking at that strategy as a whole, I’m proud of our track record and stand by it.” Amodei makes it sound as if he’s got it all tied up, but playing multiple roles is hard. There are huge tensions between the priorities of an AI safety organization and a profit-driven megacorp, for example. (Amodei admits in his post that he has made mistakes.) Ultimately, the stories Anthropic tells itself should help us understand the stories it tells us. They should help us figure out how much to trust it to self-regulate or whether we should be forcing it to give external safety testers access to its models on our terms rather than theirs. They should help us give it the benefit of the doubt (or not) when what looks like a self-serving piece of legislation comes to light. But I think the stories reveal a multiheaded hydra. Anthropic is many things to many people, and I’m not sure that makes it more trustworthy. In many ways, a good old-fashioned technology company—without the science fiction role-play, without the woo-woo—would be far easier to read. The Download: everyday AI harms and the race to nuclear spaceflight
