The Download: everyday AI harms and the race to nuclear spaceflight

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-26

Visit Original Website
This is today's edition of The Download, our weekday newsletter that provides a daily dose of what's going on in the world of technology. —Sam Hiner, cofounder of the Young People’s Alliance, and Emma Leiken, executive director of the Responsible Technology Youth Power Fund at Hopelab We have historically allowed debates over technology to be defined by the worst thing a product might do, with remarkably little discussion of the insidious damage it does every day. A third of teens now choose to discuss serious matters with AI companions instead of other people. Students are relying on chatbots to write their papers, while social media feeds engineered to provoke envy keep users watching. These products can meet narrow safety requirements yet still erode how we think, learn, and interact with one another. As we enter an era defined by powerful AI products, we should demand that the tech industry do more than address only catastrophic harms. Here’s why AI safety needs to go further. Earlier this year, NASA announced plans to build Space Reactor-1 Freedom, the first-ever nuclear-reactor-powered interplanetary spacecraft, and fly it to Mars by the end of 2028. A successful mission could herald more efficient, potentially faster spaceflight, saving astronauts time and cutting their exposure to harmful cosmic radiation. But nuclear space propulsion has moved slowly. The US has started—and terminated—several programs exploring the technology over the past 75 years. Could the $2 billion bet on Space Reactor-1 Freedom finally pay off?Find out how close NASA is to making nuclear-powered spaceflight a reality. This story is part of “Are we there yet?”  from the new Impossible issue of MIT Technology Review. The series takes stock of six technologies that have long promised to change the world—and scores them on a progress meter. Over the next six days, we’ll explore each one in The Download. (Kyiv Independent)+ Ukraine’s drones are fueling a wild data market. (MIT Technology Review) 2 Anthropic’s Claude accessed Georgia voter data without approvalAnthropic says the AI bypassed a paywall. (MIT Technology Review) 4 OpenAI is now training frontier models without internet accessIt paused training after models breached external systems. MIT Technology Review
The focus on AI safety disasters ignores everyday harms
2026-10-26

The focus on AI safety disasters ignores everyday harms

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-26

Visit Original Website
Consider what passes for success in technology policy. An AI company promises that its chatbot will not encourage suicide. A social media platform makes it more difficult for children to buy fentanyl. These are important protections, and they should have existed all along. But companies can clear these low bars while continuing to make products that leave young people dependent on simulated friendships or trapped in feeds built to keep them scrolling. We have historically allowed the debate over technology to be defined by the worst thing a product might do, with remarkably little discussion of the insidious damage it does every day. As we transition into an era defined by powerful AI products, we should demand that the tech industry do more than merely address catastrophic harms. We should take measures to ensure that its products don’t erode how we think, learn, and interact with one another. A teenager who develops an unhealthy attachment to an AI companion may never appear in a lawsuit or a newspaper, except in the most egregious cases. Neither will the student who begins relying on a chatbot to write every paper, or the young person who measures their life against a social media feed engineered to provoke envy and keep them watching. A product can meet every narrow safety requirement and still leave our entire culture worse off in ways that don’t always make headlines. A narrow definition of success defined around safety is politically convenient for the technology industry, and companies actively promote such definitions. Taking steps to prevent spectacular disasters is good for business: It can mean fewer lawsuits and less pressure for serious regulation. Confronting the everyday consequences of dependence would require something considerably more threatening to their bottom line. It might mean asking companies to add more friction to their products or actively limit engagement or features that lead to negative outcomes, such as addictive attention-hacking algorithms. Technology giants have instead worked to shape legislation around the harms they are already under direct pressure to address. This year, for example, Google backed bills to regulate AI chatbots in several states. The legislation mandated basic protections against sexual content and promotion of self-harm that tech companies should have established regardless, but with broad carve-outs that could exempt some of the most popular services. Passing these bills allowed legislators to claim a safety win while efforts to promote stronger legislation—laws designed to prevent children from becoming emotionally dependent on AI—failed. This playbook worked in states like Nebraska, producing legislation heavily influenced by industry at the expense of bills that would have done more to hold companies accountable. We have been through a version of this with social media. Globally, people who use the internet now spend an average of more than two hours a day on social media platforms—time that has been quietly taken from ordinary life and handed over to companies whose profits depend on keeping us there. No one, outside perhaps a few tech CEOs, wanted these algorithms to occupy such a large portion of our waking hours, and their cultural engineering can actually contribute to violence on a devastating scale. Meta’s platforms have been cited for contributing to the Rohingya genocide in Myanmar, anti-Muslim riots in Sri Lanka, and alleged calls for violence during Ethiopia’s Tigray war. The same incentives operate in the feeds that shape young Americans’ understanding of politics, their peers, and the wider world. AI is extending this logic into parts of life that social media could influence only from a distance. It offers to write our thoughts for us and stand in for the people we might otherwise turn to. A third of teens now choose to discuss serious matters with AI companions instead of other people. MIT researchers found that young people using ChatGPT to write essays showed weaker brain connectivity during the task, and their evidence indicated that “cognitive debt” persisted even after the task was complete. There is plenty we still need to learn about the long-term effects. We already have reason to ask whether technology marketed as making us more capable is eroding the habits and relationships that help us develop capabilities in the first place. Follow this way of thinking to its logical endpoint and you get a world of perfectly sanitized entertainment, with chatbots ready to spare us the difficulty of thinking or of dealing with another person’s feelings (or even our own). But anyone who has struggled to write something worth reading, or repair a damaged friendship, knows how much gets lost in that bargain. We build judgment through effort and friction, and we learn to live with other people by confronting the fact that they have needs and opinions of their own. We should demand safety as a minimum, yes—and then demand considerably more. Technology companies and school districts should have to show beyond a reasonable doubt that AI improves learning and critical thinking before making it a fixture in classrooms. Companies should be prohibited from designing companions that pretend to be conscious or profess love to users, and they should face independent scrutiny of how their systems affect relationships and people’s ability to think for themselves. The companies that have inserted their products into nearly every part of modern life have earned obligations far greater than avoiding the next terrible headline. Sam Hiner is the cofounder and executive director of the Young People’s Alliance, where he coordinated a coalition of advocates to pass laws regulating AI companions in eight states in 2026—laws such as California’s SB1119. Sam graduated from the University of North Carolina atChapel Hill in 2025 and was named to the 2026 Forbes 30 Under 30 Social Impact list. Emma Leiken is executive director of the Responsible Technology Youth Power Fund at Hopelab, which has raised more than $9 million for youth-led tech accountability work, and teaches technology policy at the Columbia School of International and Public Affairs. She was previously TikTok’s global head of youth safety and well-being policy and worked on responsible technology at Omidyar Network. Ten years after AlphaGo’s match against Go champion Lee Sedol, today’s AI still isn’t tapping into the machinery that made that win possible. An exclusive interview with Nick Cammarata, the researcher whose 26,000-word viral blog post has AI safety folks celebrating. Breathless claims about AGI and new capabilities fall apart pretty quickly under scrutiny. Surveillance towers, increasingly powered by AI, promised faster responses to crossings. Our investigation of deaths near the towers found they are failing to deliver. Discover special offers, top stories, upcoming events, and more. We’re having trouble saving your preferences. BNY says it now tracks its AI token spending to avoid overpaying
2026-10-27

BNY says it now tracks its AI token spending to avoid overpaying

The Next Web | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
BNY has built tools to track how it spends AI tokens and to route tasks to the most suitable models, CFO Dermot McDonogh told Bloomberg Television. The bank runs about 400 AI agents and spends $4B a year on technology. Banks are under pressure as AI bills rise. BNY has built tools to track how much it spends on AI and to send each task to the most suitable model, chief financial officer Dermot McDonogh told Bloomberg Television. The aim is to avoid overspending as AI costs rise. “We’ve become quite disciplined and developed some sophisticated telemetry so we can track how we’re spending and how we’re using our tokens,” McDonogh said. “We don’t use expensive models for cheap queries,” he added. He said sending tasks to the right model will matter more “over time as costs come into play.” BNY uses models from the big cloud providers and is not tied to any one of them, McDonogh said. That holds even though BNY chief executive Robin Vince joined OpenAI’s board in July. The bank runs about 400 AI agents, and AI is only part of its $4B annual technology budget. Companies are paying more for AI even as the price per token falls, because AI agents burn through far more tokens. In June, Commonwealth Bank of Australia’s chief executive warned of rising bills, and in July Atlassian put its engineers on monthly AI budgets. The comments mark a change in tone for BNY. In July, McDonogh told Fortune the bank did not track token use and focused on results instead, saying, “It’s not something we spend any time talking about.” He still does not want cost control to hold back AI use. “We don’t talk about it in relation to efficiency or job cuts,” he said. “We talk about it in relation to upskilling and trying to do better things.” Other banks have been more open about cuts. Citi chief executive Jane Fraser said in July that AI job losses have started at the bank. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Thanks! Israeli ad tech firm heavily targeted Starlink users, sources say
2026-10-27

Israeli ad tech firm heavily targeted Starlink users, sources say

The Next Web | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
An Israeli ad company showed huge numbers of ads to people using Starlink, SpaceX’s satellite internet service, probably to track them. That is what researchers believe, Reuters reported on Tuesday, citing six people who know about the work. The researchers work at TrendAI, part of Japanese security firm Trend Micro. The sources said they found that the firm, Discover-Tech, was the top bidder for ads aimed at Starlink users in more than 100 countries. These included several where Starlink is banned or restricted. Advertisers bid in split-second auctions to show ads in apps and on web pages. These auctions can leak details about the person who will see the ad, such as where they are and what device they use. Over time, that data can be used to work out who people are and follow them online. TrendAI published a report on 15 October, but called the firm only “Shadow-Earth-79”. The sources told Reuters that it was TrendAI’s codename for Discover-Tech, and that executives dropped plans to name it over legal concerns. The report said the firm was the top ad buyer in Syria, Libya, Yemen and South Sudan, even though there is almost no demand there for legitimate Western ads. It said, with moderate-to-high confidence, that this looked like “targeting and tracking infrastructure rather than a commercial advertising operation”. Discover-Tech was already under scrutiny. On the same day, Canadian research group Citizen Lab said the firm had used targeted ads to help deliver spyware to devices at Al Jazeera, among others. Ad security firm Confiant said its ads had appeared inside government and media networks. Google and others have since cut the company off from their ad platforms. Discover-Tech did not respond to Reuters for over two weeks. SpaceX also did not reply. Tracking Starlink users has become a business of its own. The dishes connect through SpaceX’s satellites, not local phone companies, so they can get around a country’s internet controls. Ukraine’s military, for example, relies on the service at the front line. In May, Israeli newspaper Haaretz reported that Israeli-owned businesses were offering clients ways to identify and track Starlink users, with ad data playing a key role. Starlink’s reach keeps growing, as SpaceX now seeks 80,000 cell sites in the US for its mobile plans. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Thanks! Remote’s Job van der Voort says AI companies now pay PhDs to label data
2026-10-27

Remote’s Job van der Voort says AI companies now pay PhDs to label data

The Next Web | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
TNW’s Cristian Dina (left) and Remote CEO Job van der Voort at HumanX in Amsterdam, 23 September 2026 Remote chief executive Job van der Voort wants his staff to be 10 times more effective a year from now, because their AI tools will be 10 times more effective too. The company runs payroll and hires staff in other countries for its customers. Rather than replace people with AI, he wants to train the ones it has, he told TNW. Van der Voort, who is Dutch, studied neuroscience and left his PhD to start a company with a friend. It ran out of money fast, he said. He then joined GitLab, where staff worked remotely all over the world, and hiring and paying them across countries proved a constant struggle. He left GitLab in January 2019 and started Remote a week later. He runs it with co-founder Marcelo Lebre. He spoke to Cristian Dina at HumanX in Amsterdam on 24 September. It is the latest episode of the TNW Podcast, after Boomi’s Steve Lucas, Daytona’s Ivan Burazin and Glean’s Arvind Jain. The full conversation is in the video below. Remote has about 2,000 employees and keeps growing. Van der Voort does not plan to grow the workforce much, or to shrink it, he said. When a team gets busier, the first thing people ask for is another hire. They make good arguments, which is what makes it tricky. Remote told staff to try solving the problem with AI first. “It works as a good forcing function to say, well, you cannot hire more people, right? Because then people have to look for solutions.” Job van der Voort, Remote chief executive Some people at Remote took to AI quickly. A large group did not, he said. So Remote built an AI course and made it compulsory. It has since made the course free for anyone as AI for Actual Work. Some payroll teams thought tools like these could not work for them, because they had done the job the same way for 20 years. Remote still fights that, he said. His answer is not to fire them and hire people who already use AI. “The people that I hire, I want to take care of them and I want them to get better.” Job van der Voort Most of Remote’s customers are startups that employ knowledge workers. Van der Voort said Remote has not seen them hire faster or slower. AI companies are the exception: they are growing “like crazy,” he said. The last big shift was the wave of layoffs in 2022, when venture funding started to fall. Since then the market has been stable. No customer has told Remote it is replacing an employee with an AI agent, he said, though Remote does not always know why someone leaves. Companies are trying to make their people more effective, and most are “not on the AI bandwagon yet,” he said. The change he does see is between employees and contractors. Demand for independent contractors has gone up “massively,” he said, because AI companies need people to label data and give feedback to their models. It began with simple labelling. Now many are PhDs who do the work alongside their research. He expects that market to keep growing for the next few years. “What’s going to happen beyond that I don’t know. I don’t think anybody knows.” Job van der Voort Remote is profitable and cash flow positive, he said. Its last funding round was a Series C in 2022, and it has raised no private capital since, because it has no need to. It has no plans to go public or to sell. Its customers run from Burger King to Anthropic, he said, and number in the tens of thousands. Its own staff are in 90 countries, with the largest group, about 20%, in the US. Remote can be accessed through an MCP server, so authorised agents like Claude or ChatGPT can work with it within the user’s permissions. Remote is building agents that automate payroll tasks across countries, such as chasing staff to submit expenses on time. Remote does come up against Deel, Oyster and Rippling in some deals, he said. But its main rivals are local payroll firms that have done the same work for two decades, and sometimes an office of accountants. Labour law differs in every European country, and the challenge grows as Remote gets bigger, he said. He is not optimistic. “I think it’s bad for Europe that we have so much complicated rules around hiring and employing people and setting up businesses.” Job van der Voort His next company could be in robotics, because he thinks software is “sort of getting solved,” he said. He thinks about it every day. For now, he plans to keep growing Remote. His advice to graduates is to join a startup, fail a lot and keep going, because “the way to win is basically to just stick around.” Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Thanks! Alibaba wins injunction against Pentagon rule
2026-10-27

Alibaba wins injunction against Pentagon rule

The Next Web | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
A US federal judge has temporarily blocked a Pentagon rule that could cost companies their defence contracts if they share lobbyists with Alibaba, Reuters reported. The California court granted Alibaba’s request for an injunction on Monday while its legal challenge continues. The dispute began in June, when the Pentagon added Alibaba to a list of companies it says support China’s military. The US has been tightening restrictions on Chinese technology more broadly. The Treasury Department has also introduced limits on US investment in Chinese AI and biotechnology. Inclusion on the Pentagon list does not trigger formal sanctions, but it restricts the department’s ability to do business with the companies named. From 2027, the restrictions are also due to cover purchases of their products through third-party suppliers. A separate US law bars Pentagon contractors from employing lobbyists who represent companies on the list. That meant firms sharing lobbyists with Alibaba risked losing defence work. After Alibaba was added in June, the company dismissed dozens of its Washington lobbyists, Reuters reported, citing a source. Monday’s ruling temporarily prevents the government from enforcing the lobbying restriction against Alibaba’s lobbyists. It does not remove Alibaba from the Pentagon’s list, a separate part of the company’s legal challenge. Alibaba sued the US government in June, arguing that its inclusion on the list had “no basis in fact or law”. The company says it serves consumers, delivery businesses and corporate clients, and denies having ties to China’s military. Neither Alibaba nor the Defense Department commented on the ruling, according to Reuters and Dow Jones. The case is part of a wider legal challenge to the Pentagon’s list. Chinese drugmaker WuXi AppTec won a court order in August that kept it off the list. In September, two subsidiaries of carmaker BYD sued over the lobbying restriction, arguing that it violates their free speech rights. Drone maker DJI is also challenging its designation after an appeals court ordered the government to review the decision again. The immediate financial impact on Alibaba is likely to be limited. The company has little business in the US, according to Morningstar, and remains focused primarily on its home market. Last month, it unveiled its own AI chip for use in its data centres. “We view the preliminary injunction as a symbolic and procedural win for Alibaba rather than a fundamental game-changer,” Morningstar analyst Chelsey Tam told Dow Jones. Mike Howard, Whitespace’s new chief product officer Whitespace, a UK company that builds AI for defence and government, has hired former Meta product manager Mike Howard as its chief product officer. He will set the direction for its products and build the team behind them as the company prepares for its next funding round. Mike Howard has worked in technology and data for more than 20 years. At Meta, he helped grow an engineering team in London to about 100 people, first working on the technology itself and later leading on product. Earlier in his career, he spent time at Deloitte, analysing data and building financial models. He joins as Whitespace tries to grow its main product, an AI platform called Collective. The software learns how an organisation works and uses that knowledge to help its staff make decisions more quickly. So far, much of that work has been in defence. Whitespace says it has contracts with the UK Ministry of Defence and NATO. Last year, it also agreed to build AI software for the military with Defence Holdings, a listed company. It now wants to sell Collective to government departments, banks and healthcare providers as well. “If an organisation can make better, faster and smarter decisions than its competitors, or enemies, it wins,” said chief executive and co-founder Paul Jenkinson. Jenkinson said the company had spent a decade building technology for some of the most demanding settings and now wants to take that experience to customers around the world. Howard, he added, will help it grow both its products and the team that builds them. The company, which was founded in Belfast in 2015, raised a £10m ($13m) Series A in November 2025 from Beach Equity and White Cloud, UKTN reported at the time. It is now preparing to raise a Series B. Whitespace calls itself a sovereign AI company, meaning its technology is built and controlled in the UK. Apple’s iOS 27.2 gives Siri AI a memory and five more languages
2026-10-27

Apple’s iOS 27.2 gives Siri AI a memory and five more languages

The Next Web | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Apple’s Siri AI shown on a MacBook, iPhone, iPad, Apple Watch, and Apple Vision Pro Apple released iOS 27.2 on Monday, the first big update to iOS 27, alongside new versions of iPadOS, macOS, watchOS, visionOS and tvOS. Most of the new features are for Siri AI, the rebuilt assistant Apple released in beta in September. Siri AI now works in French, Japanese, Korean, Portuguese and Spanish as well as English, Apple said in a Newsroom post. Chinese (traditional), Norwegian, Turkish and Vietnamese follow by the end of the year. The assistant is still labelled a beta, and the device must be set to a supported language. Siri AI can now recall past conversations, so users can ask about something it said before, such as the restaurants it suggested last week. That comes from Apple’s release notes, which MacRumors published in full. Siri AI can also draw on more of a user’s data. That now includes photos sent in Messages, the people invited to Calendar events, voicemails, and Safari history and Reading List. A new setting, Excluded Apps, lets users choose, in one place, which apps Siri, Search and suggestions can access. Users can also select and copy just part of a Siri reply. The Siri AI features need an iPhone 15 Pro, iPhone 15 Pro Max or any iPhone 16 model or later. Siri AI remains unavailable in the EU on iOS, iPadOS and watchOS. Apple first delayed it in Europe in June, blaming the Digital Markets Act. In Monday’s post it said: “Apple is working constructively with the EU in an attempt to find a path forward.” Apple, in its Newsroom post The release notes also say Siri AI is not available in mainland China, where Apple’s first AI features are due in December. EU users do get one change of their own: iOS 27.2 adds an alternative, expanded prompt for App Tracking Transparency there, MacRumors reported. In Messages, iOS 27.2 suggests actions based on the conversation, such as adding a dinner invitation to Calendar. Photos and Image Playground now support SynthID, the watermark Google uses to mark AI images, to help show whether an image was made or changed with generative tools. Google opened its SynthID Detector to everyone earlier this month. The Apple TV app gets a profile for each person in a household. FaceTime can add a third-party sign language interpreter or live captioner to a call. Shared Albums in Photos can warn people before an album expires. Apple Music gets its new icon and branding. The iPhone Duo has its own release notes. They add Edge Light, which uses the screen to light the user’s face on video calls, alongside the Apple Pencil support reported on Monday. On the Mac, macOS Golden Gate 27.2 brings the same Siri memory and Messages suggestions. Apple also released iOS 26.7.2 with security fixes for older iPhones. All the updates install from Settings, then General, then Software Update. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Thanks! Check your inbox to confirm your subscription.
